This approach aims to maintain therapeutic levels of ranibizumab, leading to better visual outcomes, increased treatment adherence, and reduced burden on patients, caregivers, and the healthcare system. The primary mode of action of the PDS is the pharmacologic activity of the ranibizumab drug product.

The pharmacology of ranibizumab alone was characterized in nonclinical studies performed during the original development and marketing authorization of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection. These legacy studies are included in Module 4 of this application for completeness. No new pharmacology studies have been performed for the Port Delivery System with ranibizumab (PDS).

For the original development of ranibizumab, the pharmacokinetics and toxicokinetics of ranibizumab were investigated in vivo using New Zealand White rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys. To support the development of the PDS for continuous delivery of ranibizumab, PK and TK studies were performed in Yucatan minipigs, as the eyes of the original toxicology species (rabbit and cynomolgus monkey) are too small to accommodate the size of the PDS implant.

To support the original development of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection, the safety of ranibizumab was characterized in nonclinical studies including single-dose toxicity studies in New Zealand White rabbits, repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys, and an embryo-foetal development study. For the development of the PDS, additional studies have been performed. Toxicology studies with the PDS include a 6-month chronic toxicity study in minipigs and two tolerability studies in rabbits using surrogate PDS implants.

The PDS toxicology studies (with the exception of pilot rabbit Study 13-1963 and investigative surgical Study 16-0261), as well as pivotal legacy toxicology studies ranibizumab administered via IVT injection, were conducted in accordance with Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) Principles of GLP [C(97)186/Final], and were conducted in a country that is a member of the OECD Mutual Acceptance of Data program.

## 4.2. Analytical methods

Throughout the development of ranibizumab for IVT injection a range of bioanalytical methods was developed and validated.

For the legacy studies, a sandwich ELISA with a colorimetric detection system was developed and validated to quantitate ranibizumab in rabbit serum, rabbit aqueous fluid, rabbit vitreous fluid, rabbit retinal tissues homogenate, Cynomolgus monkey serum and Cynomolgus monkey vitreous fluid. No maximum dilution was established for any matrix; therefore, the assay had an indefinite upper limit of quantitation. A non-validated ELISA method was used to measure levels of ranibizumab in the following fluids and tissues for Studies 00 141 1757 and 00 142 1757: Cynomolgus monkey aqueous fluid, Cynomolgus monkey retinal tissues For the studies with ranibizumab PDS in minipigs, ELISA methods were developed to detect ranibizumab in minipig serum and ADAs. The ELISA method for the detection of ranibizumab in minipig serum was qualified/validated under different projects with assay quantification ranges of 0.0075 to 0.3 ng/mL and 0.030 to 1.2 ng/mL, respectively. Because the assay was more sensitive than needed, it was re-validated with an assay quantitative range of 0.350 to 28.0 ng/mL. The sensitivity of the ADA assay during qualification was determined to be 93.0 ng/mL, and in validation was determined to be 59.0 ng/mL. The assay was able to detect 2500 ng/mL of this surrogate antibody in the presence of 100 µg/mL of ranibizumab.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3. Pharmacology

## 4.3.1. Pharmacodynamics

## 4.3.1.1. Primary pharmacodynamics

The pharmacology of ranibizumab alone was characterized in nonclinical studies performed during the original development and marketing authorization of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection. These legacy studies are included in Module 4 of this application for completeness. No new pharmacology studies have been performed for the Port Delivery System with ranibizumab (PDS). Results from the legacy studies can be summarized as follows:

- As evaluated by surface plasmon resonance, it could be shown that ranibizumab binds with high affinity to the VEGF-receptor binding site on the active isoforms of VEGF-A (rhVEGF110, rhVEGF121, rhVEGF165).
- In an in vitro proliferation assay ranibizumab inhibited the proliferation of human umbilical vein endothelial cells (HUVECs) induced by rhVEGF165, rhVEGF121, and rhVEGF110 in a concentrationdependent manner.
- Ranibizumab was also found to block VEGF-induced tissue factor upregulation in HUVECs. Tissue factor regulates angiogenesis (new blood vessel formation).
- Ranibizumab exerted a concentration-dependent inhibition of VEGF-induced vascular permeability in a guinea pig skin model, as measured by a modified Miles Assay.
- Further, in a cynomolgus monkey study (Krzystolik et al. 2002), ranibizumab was administered by IVT injection either before laser-induced retinal injury or after the formation of mature CNV lesions. Findings from this study indicated that IVT injection administration of ranibizumab prevented formation of clinically significant CNV lesions and decreased leakage of already formed CNV lesions.

The aforementioned in vitro and in vivo proof of concept studies were already assessed during the initial MAA procedure for ranibizumab administered via IVT injection (Lucentis). The studies support the use of ranibizumab in the proposed indication wet AMD. No re-assessment is deemed necessary for the PDS.

## 4.3.1.2. Secondary pharmacodynamics

In line with the requirements of ICH S6 and ICH S7a guidelines, no dedicated studies to investigate secondary pharmacodynamic effects with ranibizumab or ranibizumab PDS have been performed. During the initial MAA for ranibizumab administered via IVT injection, studies were submitted which confirm that no complement-dependent cytotoxicity or antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity is expected, which is consistent with the lack of the Fc region in ranibizumab.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.1.3. Safety pharmacology

No dedicated safety pharmacology studies have been performed. However, in line with the requirements of ICH S6 guideline, several safety pharmacology endpoints were assessed as part of three repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys. No treatment-related effects on respiration rate, heart rate, and body temperature were observed in cynomolgus monkeys given up to 2.0 mg/eye of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection as often as every 2 weeks for up to 26 weeks. Because the ocular and systemic ranibizumab concentrations following treatment with PDS 100 mg/mL are within the range experienced with IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg and lower than those achieved in studies in monkeys with IVT ranibizumab injections every other week, no additional safety pharmacology studies have been conducted for the PDS.

## 4.3.1.4. Pharmacodynamic drug interactions

No dedicated in vitro nonclinical studies on pharmacodynamic drug interactions have been conducted with ranibizumab or ranibizumab PDS. The approach is justified with the local (ocular) administration and expected metabolism by degradation to small peptides and individual amino acids.

## 4.3.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 4.3.2.1. Absorption

No dedicated absorption studies have been performed. Ranibizumab absorption was characterized in single-dose PK studies in rabbits (98-223-1757, 99-101-1757) and cynomolgus monkeys (00-1411757) after IVT injection. Overall, a dose-dependent pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab was observed after IVT injection. Systemic levels are dependent on the relatively slow release of ranibizumab from the vitreous. In rabbits and monkeys, serum levels were &gt;1000-fold lower than vitreous concentrations. The levels in aqueous humour and retina were approx. 2-3-fold lower than the vitreous levels. Drug clearance from the vitreous is slow and a T1/2 of approx. 3 days was observed.

The aforementioned studies are legacy studies which were performed during the authorization of IVT ranibizumab (Lucentis). For dedicated studies with the PDS implant: see other PK studies in section 3.7 of the assessment report.

## 4.3.2.2. Distribution

Dedicated non-clinical distribution studies were not performed with ranibizumab PDS. Legacy studies with IVT ranibizumab in rabbits (00-141-1757) and cynomolgus monkeys (98-223-1757) confirmed that ranibizumab penetrated into the retina, to the site of choroidal neovascularization. In these studies, iodine-125 labelled ranibizumab penetrated to the retinal pigment epithelium and was found in or on the optic nerve and optic nerve head, all retinal layers, ciliary body epithelium and stroma, anterior and posterior lens capsule, zonula fibres, iris epithelium, stroma, and corneal endothelium.

## 4.3.2.3. Metabolism

No metabolism studies were conducted.

## 4.3.2.4. Excretion

No excretion studies were conducted.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.3.2.5. Pharmacokinetic drug interactions

Non-clinical PK drug interaction studies were not performed with the PDS given the local (ocular) administration of ranibizumab. Due to the low systemic levels of ranibizumab, systemic pharmacokinetic interactions are not expected.

In a legacy study (00-580-1757), the potential for intraocular drug interaction was addressed. A combination of ranibizumab IVT injections with intravenous verteporfin photodynamic therapy following laser-induced choroidal neovascularization (CNV) was evaluated in cynomolgus monkeys. Ranibizumab disposition was not affected by the combination therapy.

## 4.3.2.6. Other pharmacokinetic studies

In addition to the legacy studies with IVT ranibizumab, two nonclinical studies evaluating the pharmacokinetics and toxicokinetics of ranibizumab PDS have been performed.

A study (13-2666) in female mini-pigs was conducted to compare the pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab following a single IVT injection, intravenous (IV) administration, or repeat ocular administration via the PDS implant. Following IV administration, ranibizumab is eliminated quickly, with a half-life of approximately 0.2 days (5 hours). Following IVT administration, ranibizumab enters the systemic circulation with an ocular half-life of approximately 6.8 days. Following RPDS implant there is a steady increase in initial- prior to Day 12. After Day 12, the serum concentration data were confounded by the presence of serum anti-ranibizumab antibodies but the continuing increase in serum concentrations over 40 days confirms the long-term sustained drug release ability of the PDS implant.

A GLP 6-month toxicity study (14-2350) in Yucatan minipigs was conducted to evaluate the toxicokinetics of ranibizumab 2.3 mg/eye delivered via the implant, which was either filled once prior to implant insertion (Group 2) or refilled monthly for seven total doses (Group 3). The exposure to ranibizumab PDS was similar in animals from Groups 2 and 3 after the first dose. After the initial administration of ranibizumab PDS, ranibizumab mean Tmax values were 27.0 days for Group 2 and approx. 21 days for Group 3. After the final administration of ranibizumab PDS on TK Study Day 168, ranibizumab mean Tmax value was approx. 168 days for animals in Group 3. Following monthly dosing, ranibizumab PDS had an accumulation ratio over the first dose of 15.3 and 20.5 for Cmax and AUC, respectively based on data from one animal. The levels of circulating drug levels are influenced by the presence of ADAs.

## 4.4. Toxicology

## 4.4.1. Single-dose toxicity

Single IVT injection administration of ranibizumab up to 2.5 mg/eye in GLP local tolerance rabbit studies was well tolerated (Studies 98-359-1757, 00-205-1757, and 02-406-1757). No other singledose toxicity studies were conducted.

The omission of single-dose toxicity studies is acceptable in accordance with ICH S6(R1) guideline.

## 4.4.2. Repeat-dose toxicity

4-, 13- and 26-weeks pivotal GLP studies were conducted in cynomolgus monkey by IVT route. To maximize the dose and the systemic exposure, ranibizumab IVT injections were given bi-weekly in each eye. In addition, a 16-weeks (9-weeks treatment, 7-weeks recovery) non-pivotal GLP study was also conducted in cynomolgus monkey by IVT route investigating different dosing regimens and the effects of oral and topical corticosteroid treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Dose-dependent, reversible ocular inflammation was observed following repeated IVT injection administration of ranibizumab at doses up to 2.0 mg/eye (a 2-fold margin to a human dose based on differences in vitreous volume) given every 2-4 weeks for up to 6 months (Studies 98-358-1757, 98361-1757, 99-539-1757, and 01-463-1757). Administration of oral and topical corticosteroids did not mitigate the inflammatory response. The inflammation was considered consistent with a non-specific immunogenic response to a humanized protein in monkeys, and no assurance to the clinical situation can be given in this respect.

To support the development and authorization of the ranibizumab PDS, a 6-month repeat-dose toxicity study (Study 14-2350; GLP) in Yucatan minipigs was conducted. The minipig was selected as the appropriate species to assess PDS chronic toxicity and toxicokinetic because the eye of minipigs is large enough for the PDS. Porcine VEGF is more than 90% homologous with human VEGF by DNA sequence and is predicted to differ from human VEGF by five amino acids. The contact residues for ranibizumab binding to VEGF are conserved between human, macaques, and pigs. Ranibizumab also binds pig VEGF (average in vitro KD values of 128.33 and 75.91 pM to recombinant pig and human VEGF, respectively, in Study 20 1473). The study evaluated three groups with PDS implant insertion in the right eye: a group with a single dose administered via the PDS, a group with PDS implant insertion followed by monthly refills, and a control group with PDS implant insertion with implant filled with vehicle.

Sham surgery procedure alone was associated with some ophthalmic abnormalities, while insertion of the implant was associated with more significant findings. Implant insertion without ranibizumab was associated with conjunctival hyperaemia, vitreous haemorrhage, movement of the implant leading to traumatic cataracts or RPE mottling. Administration of single or multiple doses of ranibizumab via the PDS implant was associated with even increased severity of ocular inflammation. Overall, an additional risk for ocular events associated with surgery, implant insertion, and the re-fill procedure could be identified. Nevertheless, from a clinical perspective, it was concluded that the decrease in treatment frequency and visits with PDS may increase persistence/adherence to the treatment and reduce the burden to patients. Therefore, the benefit of less visits outweighs the additional risk associated with surgery, implant insertion, and the re-fill procedure.

## 4.4.3. Genotoxicity

No genotoxicity studies have been conducted.

## 4.4.4. Carcinogenicity

Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted.

## 4.4.5. Developmental and reproductive toxicity

## Fertility

The effect of ranibizumab on fertility has not been investigated extensively. Although no effects on reproductive tissues were noted in repeat-dose IVT injection monkey studies, inhibition of VEGF in human female reproductive tissues could potentially impair fertility.

No reproductive and developmental toxicity studies with the implant have been conducted (in accordance with ISO 10993-1) but the 6-month necropsy findings from the PDS toxicity study in minipigs (Study 14-2350) did not identify any macroscopic or microscopic abnormalities in reproductive organs in sexually mature animals.

Taking also into account the median age (above 70 years) of the targeted AMD population, the absence of specific fertility studies with ranibizumab can be considered as justifiable and the risk for human fertility can be assessed based on the weight of evidence.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Embryo-fœtal development

With regard to reproductive and developmental toxicity the applicant refers to the legacy toxicity Study 08-0590, which was performed with ranibizumab administered via IVT injection in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys. Bilateral IVT injection of ranibizumab to pregnant female cynomolgus monkeys at dose levels of 0.125, and 1 mg/eye on day 20, 34, 48, and 62 of gestation did not elicit maternal toxicity, developmental toxicity or teratogenicity. No effect on placental weights or structure was detected. Skeletal abnormalities, including incomplete ossification of the petrosal bone of the skull and shortened supernumerary vertebra, were seen at a low incidence in foetuses from dams given ranibizumab 1.0 mg/eye; no skeletal abnormalities were seen at the lower dose of 0.125 mg/eye.

Following repeat IVT injection of ranibizumab 0.125 or 1.0 mg/eye in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys, serum ranibizumab concentrations were approximately dose proportional and were consistent with concentrations predicted from previous PK and TK data in non-pregnant monkeys. In some of the animals that were positive for serum antibody to ranibizumab, serum ranibizumab concentrations were higher than expected/predicted after the third and fourth dose. The cause of this effect has not been elucidated experimentally, but a plausible explanation for it is that the anti-therapeutic antibody acts as a carrier protein for ranibizumab, thereby decreasing its clearance in antibody positive animals.

At the higher dose of 1.0 mg/eye, mean trough serum ranibizumab levels ranged from 3.8 to 19.8 ng/mL and were up to 17-fold higher than the observed serum maximum ranibizumab concentration of 1.1 ng/mL in patients treated with PDS 100 mg/mL in Phase II Study GX28228.

## 4.4.6. Toxicokinetics and exposure margins

Safety margins are based on serum data from the repeat-dose (every 2 weeks [Q2W]) chronic IVT toxicology study in cynomolgus monkeys (Study 01-463-1757) and the clinical study of the PDS refilled Q24W from the Phase III Study GR40548, Report 1100486. The calculated safety factor for systemic exposure following PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W ranged from 1500 to 2100 based on Cmax and AUCτ,SS, respectively, suggesting a lower clinical systemic exposure of ranibizumab via the PDS in humans than exposures in cynomolgus monkey via IVT injection. The estimated ocular safety factors are 32-fold based on Cmax, and 10-fold based on AUCτ,SS.

## 4.4.7. Local tolerance

In legacy studies with ranibizumab for IVT injection, local tolerance was evaluated in rabbits:

- Pilot study 98 223A 1757 (non-GLP): In a series of pilot studies, following IVT administration in rabbits, 625 μg/eye of rhuFab V1 and rhuFab V2 was determined to be the highest well-tolerated dose and was selected for additional PK studies. Additionally, an E. coli protein ECP content of ≤ 0.5 µg/eye in the dose solution appeared to be well tolerated. Ocular inflammation was observed with DNase, but not with rhuMAb E25.
- Studies 98-359-1757, 00-205-1757, and 02-406-1757 (GLP): Single IVT injection of ranibizumab up to 2.5 mg/eye was well tolerated. On ophthalmic examination (OE), low incidence of vitreous floaters and transient mild iris inflammation were observed. These were correlated with microscopic observations of minimal neutrophil and mononuclear cell infiltrates in the vitreous, ciliary body, choroid, iris, sclera, and the filtration angle and/or optic disc. OE-observed mild inflammation and microscopically observed minimal cell infiltrates were considered not adverse.

For ranibizumab PDS, local tolerance was evaluated within the pivotal 6-months repeat-dose toxicity study in Yucatan minipigs (Study 14-2350). In this study, ranibizumab (2.3 mg/eye) was delivered via the implant filled either once with a single dose or refilled monthly for seven total doses. Ranibizumab delivery by the implant was associated with ocular inflammation, which increased in severity in eyes administered multiple doses of ranibizumab. Ocular inflammation was consistent with a non-specific immune-mediated response to ranibizumab (humanized protein), which was supported by presence of anti-therapeutic antibodies in all animals administered ranibizumab. In addition, the surgery for implant insertion itself was associated with transient mild conjunctival hyperaemia localized over the implant, vitreous haemorrhage, and movement of the implant (which was not optimized for a porcine eye) leading to traumatic cataracts or retinal pigment epithelium mottling.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 4.4.8. Other toxicity studies

In legacy studies with ranibizumab for IVT injection, the following aspects were addressed:

- Tissue cross-reactivity (Study 98 279 1754): no target antigen-specific or cross-reactive binding of rhuMAb VEGF (full length antibody counterpart of ranibizumab) to normal human tissues was observed.
- Haemolytic potential (Study 98 360 1757): the haemolytic potential of ranibizumab in cynomolgus monkey and human whole blood, the compatibility of ranibizumab with cynomolgus monkey and human serum and plasma, and with fresh human vitreous fluid was evaluated. Ranibizumab did not cause haemolysis of human erythrocytes and was compatible with cynomolgus monkey and human serum and plasma and with human vitreous fluid.
- PDT Therapy following Laser-Induced CNV (Study 00 580 1757): the safety and pharmacological activity of IVT injections of ranibizumab in combination with intravenous verteporfin-photodynamic therapy (PDT), following laser-induced choroidal neovascularization (CNV) in cynomolgus monkeys and the safety of combination treatment in normal animals with no laser-induced CNV lesions was evaluated. Data from this study indicate that IVT injection of ranibizumab in combination with PDT is safe and causes a greater reduction in angiographic leakage than PDT alone in experimental CNV. Ocular inflammation induced by ranibizumab was observed. Treatment with PDT did not alter the inflammatory response.

To support the authorization of ranibizumab PDS, ocular implantation studies with surrogate PDS implant (without ranibizumab) were performed in rabbits. The surrogate PDS implants were made from the same materials (i.e., PSU, titanium, silicone, and adhesive) used in the manufacture of the implant, but they were non-functional, did not contain ranibizumab, and were scaled one-third size to match the smaller size of the rabbit eye.

In a 2-month pilot ocular toxicology study in female NZW rabbits (Study 13-1963; Non-GLP), implantation of a surrogate implant into the vitreous was tolerated. Findings were limited to those related to the surgical implantation procedure and included mild to moderate conjunctival hyperemia and chemosis that were comparable between the non-functional implant and sham control eyes. In two of five animals the surrogate implant had been extruded into the subconjunctival space. The extrusion/migration of the implant was considered to be due to anatomical differences in scleral thickness and shape of the globe at the implantation site.

In a 6-month ocular study with non-functional polysulfone injection-molded PDS in male and female rabbits (Study 14-0137; GLP), the surrogate implant remained in place in 23/24 eyes. The placement of the implant was tolerated, but with procedure-related findings observed. No implant related effects were observed on mortality, body weight, clinical observations, ophthalmic examinations, intraocular pressure, ERG, clinical pathology, or microscopic ocular and systemic analysis. Overall, it can be concluded that the implant itself is tolerated. Nevertheless, there are risks for infections, ocular irritation etc. related to the surgery process.

## 4.4.9. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

The active substance is a natural substance, the use of which will not alter the concentration or distribution of the substance in the environment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Furthermore, ranibizumab is already used in existing marketed products and no significant increase in environmental exposure is anticipated.

## 4.5. Overall discussion and conclusions on non-clinical aspects

## 4.5.1. Discussion

## Pharmacology

Ranibizumab a recombinant humanized IgG1 monoclonal antibody Fab targeted against VEGF is considered a known active substance. Ranibizumab administered via IVT injection was first approved for use in nAMD under the tradename Lucentis in January 2007. The intended purpose of the present MAA is the administration of ranibizumab via a new route of administration, an implantable port delivery system.

The pharmacology of ranibizumab has been thoroughly investigated in the scope of the MAA of ranibizumab intended for IVT injection and these in vitro and in vivo studies are considered valid also for the present MAA. During the approval process of Lucentis, the non-clinical studies were thoroughly evaluated with regard to PD, PK, safety and efficacy. It is acknowledged that relevant studies were included in the current submission for completeness, but the assessment is focused on PK (due to the PDS) and the nonclinical studies conducted specifically with the PDS. The applicant uses a customized formulation of ranibizumab, which differs from the formulation used for Lucentis. No novel excipients are used in this customized formulation and no significant impact on PD, PK is expected by the new formulation. It is therefore considered acceptable to refer to the legacy studies evaluating ranibizumab nonclinical pharmacology and ranibizumab administration via IVT injection in rabbits and cynomolgus monkeys. Considering the known pharmacological activity of ranibizumab the approach is fully supported.

## Pharmacokinetics

The main pharmacokinetic (PK) findings are derived from animal studies from the original development of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection. Two studies are conducted in minipigs following administration via the PDS. The method for the quantification of ranibizumab in minipig serum is an ELISA. The minimum quantifiable concentration of ranibizumab in vitreous humour was determined to be 1.56 ng/mL in minipigs. For the detection of antibodies to ranibizumab in Minipig Serum two conjugated reagents to capture antibodies directed against ranibizumab are used: biotin-conjugated ranibizumab and digoxigenin (DIG)-conjugated ranibizumab. The relative assay sensitivity was determined to be 59.0 ng/mL. The assay was able to detect 2500 ng/mL of the surrogate antibody in the presence of 100 µg/mL of ranibizumab.

A study in female mini-pigs was conducted to compare the pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab following a single IVT injection, intravenous (IV) administration, or repeat ocular administration via the PDS implant. Following IV administration, ranibizumab is eliminated quickly, with a half-life of approximately 0.2 days (5 hours). Following IVT administration, ranibizumab enters the systemic circulation with an ocular half-life of approximately 6.8 days. Following release from the PDS implant, there is a steady increase in ranibizumab serum concentration- prior to Day 12. After Day 12, the serum concentration data were confounded by the presence of serum anti-ranibizumab antibodies but the continuing increase in serum concentrations over 40 days confirms the long-term sustained drug release ability of the implant.

A GLP 6-month toxicity study in Yucatan minipigs was conducted to evaluate the toxicokinetic of ranibizumab 2.3 mg/eye delivered via the implant, which was either filled once prior to implant insertion (Group 2) or refilled monthly for seven total doses (Group 3). The exposure to ranibizumab via PDS implant was similar in animals from Groups 2 and 3 after the first dose. After the initial administration of ranibizumab via the implant, ranibizumab mean Tmax values were 27.0 days for Group 2 and approx. 21 days for Group 3. After the final administration of ranibizumab via the implant on TK Study Day 168, ranibizumab mean Tmax value was approx. 168 days for animals in Group 3. Following monthly dosing, ranibizumab via the PDS implant had an accumulation ratio over the first dose of 15.3 and 20.5 for Cmax and AUC, respectively based on data from one animal. The levels of circulating drug levels are influenced by the presence of ADAs.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Safety margins are based on serum data from the repeat-dose (every 2 weeks [Q2W]) chronic IVT toxicology study in cynomolgus monkeys (Study 01-463-1757) and the clinical study of the PDS refilled Q24W from the Phase III Study GR40548, Report 1100486. The calculated safety factor for systemic exposure following PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W ranged from 1500 to 2100 based on Cmax and AUCτ,SS, respectively, suggesting a lower clinical systemic exposure of ranibizumab via the PDS in humans than exposures in cynomolgus monkey via IVT injection. The estimated ocular safety factors are 32-fold based on Cmax, and 10-fold based on AUCτ,SS.

Overall, the pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab via the PDS are well-characterized in nonclinical models. The data supports the continuous delivery capability of the device and demonstrates acceptable safety margins. The provided data is comprehensive, methodologically sound, and aligns with EMA and ICH guidelines. Despite some data gaps, the information is scientifically robust and supports the clinical development of the PDS.

## Toxicology

To support the original development of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection, the safety of ranibizumab was characterized in legacy nonclinical studies including single-dose toxicity studies in New Zealand White rabbits, repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys (representative studies include Studies 99-101-1757, 00-141-1757, and 01-463-1757), and an embryo-foetal development study (Study 08-0590).

Toxicology studies with the PDS include a 6-month chronic toxicity study in minipigs and two tolerability studies in rabbits using surrogate PDS implants (Studies 13-1963 and 14-0137) An investigative surgery study was performed to qualitatively evaluate various surgical techniques for decreasing the post-operative vitreous haemorrhage rate observed in Yucatan minipigs (Study 160261).

Sham surgery procedure alone was associated with some ophthalmic abnormalities, while insertion of the implant was associated with more significant findings. Implant insertion without ranibizumab was associated with conjunctival hyperaemia, vitreous haemorrhage that sometimes prohibited fundus visualization, movement of the implant leading to traumatic cataracts or RPE mottling. Administration of single or multiple doses of ranibizumab via the implant was associated with even increased severity of ocular inflammation. The applicant argues that the inflammatory cellular infiltrates were consistent with a non-specific immune-mediated response to ranibizumab. This is not completely agreed with. The aim of the PDS is to continuously deliver ranibizumab to the eye and maintain the vitreous concentration of ranibizumab at therapeutic levels. The PDS implant can be refilled and the contents of the implant are exchanged with fresh ranibizumab after extended intervals, such as every 24 weeks (Q24W), that is, approximately twice a year. The concept is that the decrease in treatment frequency and visits with PDS may increase persistence/adherence to the treatment and reduce the burden to patients. Considering the findings in the 6-Month ocular toxicity study with the PDS in female Yucatan minipigs, it needs to be carefully evaluated whether the benefit of less visits outweighs the additional risk associated with surgery, implant insertion, and the re-fill procedure. Furthermore, following monthly dosing, ranibizumab via the implant had an accumulation ratio over the first dose of 15.3 and 20.5 for Cmax and AUC, respectively. Administration of multiple doses of ranibizumab via the implant was associated with an increased severity of fibrosis and inflammatory cells presence compared to administration of a single dose.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Per current ICH S6(R1) Guidance on the Preclinical Safety Evaluation of Biotechnology-Derived Pharmaceuticals (ICH 2011), no genotoxicity studies with ranibizumab were conducted. No carcinogenicity studies have been conducted with ranibizumab, consistent with ICH Guideline S6(R1) (ICH 2011).

With regard to reproductive and developmental toxicity the applicant refers to the toxicity study (Study 08-0590), which was performed with ranibizumab administered via IVT injection in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys and to the trough serum ranibizumab concentrations following ranibizumab administration via IVT injection at 1.0 mg/eye which was shown to be to be up to 17-fold higher than the observed serum maximum ranibizumab concentration in patients treated with PDS 100 mg/mL. Following repeat IVT injection of ranibizumab 0.125 or 1.0 mg/eye in pregnant cynomolgus monkeys (Study 08-0590), serum ranibizumab concentrations were approximately dose proportional and were consistent with concentrations predicted from previous PK and TK data in non-pregnant monkeys. At the higher dose of 1.0 mg/eye, mean trough serum ranibizumab levels ranged from 3.8 to 19.8 ng/mL and were up to 17-fold higher than the observed serum maximum ranibizumab concentration of 1.1 ng/mL in patients treated with PDS 100 mg/mL in Phase II Study GX28228. No new safety concerns are expected when ranibizumab is delivered by the PDS. VEGF is an angiogenic factor involved in the formation of new blood vessels during embryonic and foetal development and placentation, and ranibizumab can reach the systemic circulation and inhibit VEGF systemically. No reproductive and developmental toxicity studies with the implant have been conducted (in accordance with ISO 109931) but the 6-month necropsy findings from the PDS toxicity study in minipigs (Study 14-2350) did not identify any macroscopic or microscopic abnormalities in reproductive organs in sexually mature animals. The effect of ranibizumab on fertility has not been investigated extensively. Although no effects on reproductive tissues were noted in repeat-dose IVT injection monkey studies, inhibition of VEGF in human female reproductive tissues could potentially impair fertility.

Studies were conducted with surrogate non-functional PDS implants designed for use in rabbits. The surgical implantation itself was tolerated (although only three of five implants remained), abnormal clinical observations of ocular irritation was seen and considered related to the surgery process. In a 6Month Ocular Study with non-functional polysulfone injection-molded PDS in male and female rabbits the surrogate implant remained in place in 23/24 eyes. The placement of the implant was tolerated, but with procedure-related findings observed. No implant related effects were observed on mortality, body weight, clinical observations, ophthalmic examinations, intraocular pressure, ERG, clinical pathology, or microscopic ocular and systemic analysis. Overall, it can be concluded that the implant itself is tolerated by a there are risks for infections, ocular irritation etc. related to the surgery process.

An ocular surgery study with PDS with ranibizumab in male Yucatan Minipigs revealed that post-scleral dissection choroidal laser cautery is the most appropriate and effective way to eliminate or reduce/reset intra-operative bleeding.

The toxicology studies were well-designed and appropriately executed, with clear alignment to EMA and ICH guidelines. The results are scientifically sound and support the safety of the PDS with ranibizumab for clinical use. While some limitations exist, such as the lack of genotoxicity and carcinogenicity data, these are justified by regulatory standards. The presence of ADA in animal models is acknowledged but not considered predictive of human immunogenicity. Overall, the toxicology profile of ranibizumab via the PDS is favourable, and the data are sufficient to support its continued clinical development.

## 4.5.2. Conclusions

The non-clinical data provided for ranibizumab administered via the PDS are comprehensive and scientifically robust. The pharmacological and pharmacokinetic profiles are well-characterized and support the continuous delivery mechanism of the device. Toxicological evaluations align with regulatory standards and confirm an acceptable safety margin. Despite minor data gaps, such as the absence of genotoxicity studies, the justification is sound.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, the evidence supports the continued clinical development of the PDS with ranibizumab and no concerns are raised on the provided nonclinical developmental data.

## Pharmacology

The pharmacology of ranibizumab alone was characterized in nonclinical studies performed during the original development and marketing authorization of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection (Lucentis). No new pharmacology studies were performed with the PDS. Because the ocular and systemic ranibizumab concentrations following treatment with PDS 100 mg/mL are within the range experienced with IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg and lower than those achieved during non-clinical in vivo studies, the legacy studies are considered relevant.

In vitro and in vivo primary PD studies showed that ranibizumab binds to the receptor-binding site of active forms of VEGF-A. The binding of ranibizumab to VEGF-A prevents the interaction of VEGF-A with its receptors (VEGFR1 and VEGFR2) on the surface of endothelial cells, reducing endothelial cell proliferation, vascular leakage, and new blood vessel formation.

No dedicated secondary PD studies were performed. However, it was confirmed that no complementdependent cytotoxicity or antibody-dependent cellular cytotoxicity is expected, which is consistent with the lack of the Fc region in ranibizumab.

Also, safety pharmacology studies do not raise concerns. There were no ranibizumab-related effects on cardiovascular and respiratory system in cynomolgus monkeys.

## Pharmacokinetics

No dedicated nonclinical absorption, distribution, metabolism, or excretion studies were conducted for ranibizumab (administered via IVT injection or the PDS). The absorption from the eye into systemic circulation has been characterized in the PK or TK studies. The main PK findings are derived from animal studies from the original development of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection. Two studies are conducted in minipigs with the PDS. Following insertion of the PDS implant pre-filled with ranibizumab 100 mg/mL, the observed serum PK profile was consistent with continuous delivery of ranibizumab from the implant.

In line with ICH S6(R1), omission of metabolism, excretion and PK drug interaction studies is acceptable for this product class.

## Toxicology

To support the initial development of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection, the safety of ranibizumab was characterized in nonclinical studies including single-dose toxicity studies in New Zealand White rabbits, and repeat-dose toxicity studies in cynomolgus monkeys. Toxicology studies with the PDS include a 6-month chronic toxicity study in minipigs and two tolerability studies in rabbits using surrogate PDS implants.

Overall, it can be concluded that the implant itself is tolerated. However, there are increased risks for infections, ocular irritation etc. related to the surgery process. These aspects are reflected in the clinical assessment. Considering the findings in the 6-Month Ocular Toxicity Study with the PDS in female Yucatan minipigs it needs to be carefully evaluated whether the benefit of less visits outweighs the additional risk associated with surgery, implant insertion, and the re-fill procedure.

In general, the programme is in line with regulatory guidance, (ICH S6(R1), ICH S5(R3) and ICH M3(R2)). The data is adequate for the assessment of the non-clinical safety and the toxicology studies may be considered adequate to support the use of ranibizumab PDS in patients with wet AMD.

## 5.1.1. GCP aspects

The clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the applicant.

The applicant has provided a statement to the effect that clinical trials conducted outside the community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

Based on the review of clinical data, CHMP did not identify the need for a GCP inspection of the clinical trials included in this dossier (see section 2.4.3.).

## 5.2. Clinical pharmacology

The key PK, PK-PD, and immunogenicity analyses that support PDS for the treatment of nAMD include:

- Characterization of serum ranibizumab PK data from Studies GX28228 and GR40548 by noncompartmental analysis (NCA)
- Summary of serum and aqueous humor ranibizumab concentrations over time to evaluate the PDS release and ranibizumab PK behaviour, including long-term follow-up
- Population PK (popPK) analyses (based on both serum and aqueous humor PK concentrations) to characterize ranibizumab release into the vitreous via the PDS implant
- A PK-CST model to explore the relationship between vitreous concentrations and clinical response in terms of CST
- Integrated summary of immunogenicity for ranibizumab administered via the PDS, including overall risk assessment and evaluation of anti-drug antibodies (ADAs), neutralizing antibodies (NAbs), and the impact of ADAs/NAbs on pharmacokinetics, efficacy and safety.

## 5.2.1. Methods

## Assays used for quantification of ranibizumab concentrations

Free (non-VEGF-bound) ranibizumab concentrations in human serum were quantified by ELISA. Assay quantification range for AMD was 15.0 pg/mL to 600 pg/mL. Both precision and accuracy of the QC samples tested were within ±20%. Selectivity of the assay was found to be acceptable in matrices of different disease states (age-related macular degeneration (AMD), retinal vein occlusion (RVO), and diabetic macular oedema (DME)). No interference with haemolytic or lipemic samples was observed at tested concentrations. However, interference was observed with bevacizumab and rhVEGF at concentrations of 100 pg/mL or greater and 100 ng/mL or greater, respectively. For this reason, patients with bevacizumab pretreatment were excluded from analyses in the PK evaluable population. Relevant interference with rhVEGF is rather unlikely given that serum VEGF concentrations have been reported to be below 1 ng/mL (Gu et al. 2014; Kimura et al. 2007; Rogers et al. 2018). In ICD 406 Version 2.00 Validation Report Addendum 4, interference with IL-6 and faricimab was analysed. No interference or cross-reactivity from concomitant therapeutic IL-6 were observed. Dilutional linearity was demonstrated for dilutions up to 1:1500. Long-term stability at -80°C was demonstrated for 1882 days. All serum study samples were analysed within the demonstrated long-term storage stability period.

Ranibizumab concentrations in human aqueous and vitreous humor were also measured by an ELISA method. The quantification range for this assay was 20,000 - 800,000 pg/mL and the assay performed with adequate precision and accuracy. No relevant matrix effect and no interference with VEGFR1, VEGFR2 and rhVEGF and aflibercept was observed. However, interference with bevacizumab was evident at LQC (30,000 pg/mL) at bevacizumab concentrations of 20 ng/mL and 200 ng/mL, at HQC (400,000 pg/mL) interference of bevacizumab concentrations at 200 ng/mL was evident. As mentioned above, due to this finding patients pretreated with bevacizumab were excluded from PK analyses. In ICD 597 Version 1.01 Validation Report Addendum 2, interference with IL-6 was analysed. No interference and no-cross reactivity were observed in the quantification of ranibizumab for IL-6. Long-term stability at -80°C was demonstrated for 1105 days. All aqueous humor study samples were analysed within the demonstrated long-term storage stability period.

## Assays used for determination of anti-ranibizumab antibodies

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A 3-tiered approach (screening, confirmation and characterisation) was applied for the qualitative determination of anti-ranibizumab antibodies in human serum.

For the determination of ADA, a bridging antibody ELISA was developed and validated with regard to the following parameters: cutpoint, immunodepletion, relative sensitivity, drug tolerance, recovery, selectivity, precision, and stability of assay reagents and samples. Sensitivity of the assay, relative to the positive control used, was 4.27 ng/mL. Precision of the assay was adequate. The assay tolerated up to 100 ng/mL ranibizumab for adequate detection of 30 ng/mL or 500 ng/mL ADA. No interference was observed with haemolysed or lipemic samples.

For the detection of neutralizing antibodies to ranibizumab, an ELISA-based assay was developed with a preceding step in which all VEGF present in the sample is removed by magnetic bead extraction. This step was necessary to avoid interference of VEGF with the detection of NAb. Validation experiments revealed that the assay performed with adequate precision. No interference was observed with haemolysed or lipemic samples, rhVEGF, aflibercept and soluble VEGFR2. Interference was observed at low (150 ng/mL) and high (800 ng/mL) level of NAb with 1000 ng/mL VEGFR1 and with 100 ng/mL bevacizumab. Similar to the ADA assay, ranibizumab tolerance was determined to 100 ng/mL in presence of 800 ng/mL NAb positive control and 12.5 ng/mL in presence of 250 ng/mL NAb positive control. Referring to ranibizumab concentrations in the clinical samples, ranibizumab serum concentrations did not exceed 12.5 ng/mL at all timepoints evaluated for ADA and NAb in either Study GX28228 or Study GR40548.

## 5.2.2. Pharmacokinetics

## 5.2.2.1. Introduction

The PK characteristics of ranibizumab when administered via IVT injection have been previously characterized extensively in clinical trials in neovascular age-related macular degeneration (nAMD) and other indications (Xu et al. 2013, Zhang et al. 2014). Since the PDS is designed to provide continuous delivery of ranibizumab, the PK profile of ranibizumab administered via the PDS differs from that of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection.

## 5.2.2.2. Evaluation and qualification of models

## 5.2.2.2.1. Population pharmacokinetics

A population pharmacokinetics (PopPK) model has been developed for the ranibizumab IVT and PDS based on data from the LADDER (Phase 2), PORTAL (Extension Study) and ARCHWAY (Phase 3) studies, including both serum and aqueous ranibizumab pharmacokinetics (PK) data (data cutoff date August 2021).

The popPK model developed for ranibizumab via PDS in patients with nAMD by Kågedal et al. 2023 used for external validation was built on a slightly reduced data set: The dataset for this population PK analysis included serum samples from 164 patients in LADDER, 276 patients in ARCHWAY, and 110 patients (54 enrolled from ladder and 56 enrolled from Archway) in PORTAL. From these patients, 4069, 2016, and 488 serum concentration observations were obtained. Ranibizumab levels in serum and aqueous humor samples were measured using a validated, previously described, enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) with an LLOQ of 15 pg/mL for serum and 20,000 pg/mL for aqueous humor concentrations. Aqueous humor samples for PK analysis were obtained from 28, 149, and 118 patients in LADDER, ARCHWAY, and PORTAL, respectively, who had not received bevacizumab prior to enrolling in Ladder or Archway.

*Patients in the Portal trial were also included in the Ladder or Archway trials.

The PK of ranibizumab in serum displays flip-flop kinetics. Ranibizumab PK was modelled as a first order device release rate (kR) from the device into vitreous followed by a first order release (vitreous elimination rate (kE)) into serum from vitreous. Results from the analyses indicated that the pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab via PDS was best described by the following: a PDS implant compartment, two vitreous compartments, one for the study eye and one for the fellow eye. The vitreous compartments were linked to a single serum compartment, and the study eye vitreous compartment was linked to an aqueous humor compartment. The PDS implant compartment releases drug into the study eye vitreous compartment with a constant release rate. Aqueous humor concentrations were estimated to be a ratio of the vitreous compartment. Of note, the model structure and data used in the framework of this MAA differ from the previous submission, where the aqueous study eye compartment was missing, including the Frac\\_aq parameter.

Figure 2: Population PK model structure (Kågedal et al. 2023)

<!-- image -->

CL = clearance; ITV = intravitreal injection; Kε = vitreous distribution rate constant; kr = device release rate; Fracag = aqueous to vitreous concentration ratio; PDS = Port Delivery System; V。 = volume of distribution for the central compartment.

The serum PK profile of ranibizumab was described by a one-compartment disposition model. The model included two vitreous compartments linked to the serum, one for study eye and one for fellow eye. A PDS compartment and an aqueous humor compartment were linked to the vitreous compartment of the study eye, respectively.

Source: Kågedal et al. 2023.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The baseline CrCL was found to be the only significant covariate on ranibizumab clearance from serum, consistent with earlier findings with IVT ranibizumab in patients with nAMD (Xu et al. 2013, Zhang et al. 2014). However, CrCL has no clinical relevance as there is no impact on vitreous concentrations, and systemic ranibizumab levels remain low relative to levels required to inhibit VEGF systemically. No clinically significant covariates have been identified for ranibizumab exposure for patients with nAMD.

The popPK model developed for ranibizumab via PDS in patients with nAMD by Kågedal et al. 2023 was used to assess the additional PK data available from the long-term extension Study GR40549 using the external validation approach using a later data cutoff date from PORTAL (May 31, 2024).

- Additional PK data from Study GR40549: Serum and aqueous humor ranibizumab concentrations collected in Study GR40549 from the previous data cut (27 August 2021) to the updated data cut (31 May 2024), which were not included in the model development were included in the external validation analysis.
- Patients previously treated with bevacizumab: Patients who received prior IVT bevacizumab treatment who had PK observations after 36 weeks from the last dose of bevacizumab were also included. PK observations that were sampled less than 36 weeks after the last bevacizumab dose were excluded since the analytical assay may interfere with bevacizumab.

Data from patients with fellow eye or supplemental treatment were included in the popPK analysis. Patients with septum dislodgement as well as patients with bilateral PDS were excluded. In total, 278 patients were included in the population PK analysis population (180 patients with prior bevacizumab treatment and 98 patients with additional samples in Study GR40549 since 27 August 2021), resulting in a total of 2082 serum observations and 937 aqueous observations.

## Population PK model: Evaluation and qualification

For the published popPK model used for the further external validation step, goodness-of-fit plots, including conditional weighted residuals versus time after implantation, as well as observed data versus individual or population predictions, VPCs, and ETA distributions have been provided (Kågedal et al. 2023). ETA\\_shrinkage was 8, 17, 67, 0 and 49% for ke (elimination rate constant for flow from vitreous to serum), CL, Vc, EPS (interindividual variability serum residual error) and kr (implant release rate) respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 3: Conditional weighted residuals versus time after implantation

## External validation step

The lower limit of quantification (LLOQ) in serum was 15 pg/mL and 20,000 pg/mL in aqueous humor.

PK samples with a concentration below the lower limit of quantification (BLQ) concentration were flagged in the dataset. In total, 9.6% of the serum and 8.4% of the aqueous humor PK observations were reported as BLQ and were excluded from the analysis without further assessment of their impact on parameter estimates. A total of 937 aqueous humor and 2082 serum PK observations from 278 patients were included in the external validation after data exclusions.

Goodness-of-fit plots as well as simulation-based diagnostics (i.e., visual predictive checks [VPC]) were conducted to assess the predictive performance of the previously developed popPK model in describing the additional data.

Serum observations for the external validation are shown in Figure 6 and aqueous humor observations are shown in Figure 7. Serum observations show an apparent decrease in elimination rate as time after last dose increase. Aqueous humor observations do not show the same tendency over time. There is little aqueous humor data available in the PDS 10 and 40 mg/mL dose groups.

The performance of the model was deemed adequate for the external validation data. No major trends were present in CWRES for both serum and aqueous humor data seen in Figure 8 and Figure 9. Both population and individual predictions describe the data adequately for both serum Figure 10 and aqueous humor Figure 11.

Simulation-based diagnostic plots (VPCs) stratified by nominal dose show that the median as well as the variability is adequately described for serum (Figure 12) as well as aqueous humor (Figure 13). The prediction correction component of the VPC is used here to normalize the observations and predictions to assess model fitness when multiple types of doses are administered (PDS in study eye, IVT in study eye, IVT in fellow eye). Prediction-corrected VPCs stratified by nominal dose and study (all data, Figure 14 and Figure 15) indicate an overall acceptably well description of data (serum and aqueous humor).

## 5.2.2.3. Absorption

## In vitro drug release characterization

The ranibizumab release from the PDS implant has been characterized in vitro. The active release rate curve from the Phase III design verification study for the initial filled implants is shown in Figure 16, and for the refilled implants in Figure 17.

The measured active release rate data for ranibizumab (100 mg/mL) fill concentration was 3.55 µg/day at 28 week total release (27 week midpoint) after both initial fill and refill, meeting the 2.3 µg/day at 26.3 weeks requirement. At 38-week total release duration (37-week midpoint) following the refill, the measured active release rate was 2.06 µg/day, meeting the 1.5 µg/day at 36 weeks requirement.

The commercial ranibizumab PDS formulation is identical to the Phase III formulation; therefore, no change in the drug release is expected.

## Results of relevant PK parameters

For the 100 mg/mL PDS in study GX28228, ranibizumab Cmax (geometric mean) in serum in the PK population with exclusions was 1067.43 pg/mL after implantation and 1125.99 pg/mL after refill (total). Median Tmax was 27.44 days after implantation and 6.91 days after refill. In the PK population with exclusions, Cmax in serum was 1080.7 pg/mL after implantation and 1131 pg/mL after refill. Median Tmax in this population was 29 days after implantation and 6.97 days after refill.

In study GR40548, the observed geometric mean Cmax and the median Tmax in serum in the PKEvaluable Population for the patients from selected study sites with additional PK sampling in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm was 450 pg/mL and 26.1 days, respectively.

As per population PK analysis, mean Cmax in serum was predicted to be 463.6 pg/mL and mean Cmax in vitreous humour was predicted to be 27.3 µg/mL for the PDS at 100 mg/ml administered Q24W.

## 5.2.2.4. Distribution

Volume of distribution was not determined by noncompartmental analyses. In the popPK model, volume of the central compartment (Vc) was fixed to 3270 mL, which is the value estimated for Vc in the former model (M. Kagedal and T. Lu. Population pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab in age-related macular degeneration: an update, 2020).

## 5.2.2.5. Metabolism

Like other monoclonal antibodies (mAbs), ranibizumab is metabolized into smaller peptides and amino acids primarily through intracellular degradation within the reticuloendothelial system. Renal excretion does typically not occur due to the large molecular size of mAbs.

## 5.2.2.6. Elimination

Clearance was not determined by NCA. The apparent terminal half-life (geometric mean) of ranibizumab in serum, when administered by PDS at 100 mg/mL, was determined to be 119.07 days after implantation and 143.87 days after refills in study GX28228 and 482.22 days in study GR40548.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The popPK model was used to simulate serum concentrations over time for a population. The initial systemic ranibizumab clearance (CL0) was estimated as 30,010 mL/day and the clearance at infinite time (CL0*FracCL) was estimated as 10,314 mL/day. The CL is reduced by around 11% to 26,800 mL/day at week 4 and by 43% to 17,200 mL/day at week 24 after dose.

## 5.2.2.8. Pharmacokinetics in the target population

## STUDY GX28228

PK sampling was performed throughout the study in the PDS arms, with serum samples collected at randomization, Day 1 (≥ 60 minutes after implant insertion), Day 7, Day 14, at each monthly study visit throughout the study and at - 2 and 7 days after refill-exchange procedure. Serum PK samples were collected in the IVT arm only at Ctrough (predose before select monthly IVT injections). An optional aqueous humor sample (collected prior to or immediately following implant refill-exchange procedure), and a vitreous or aqueous humor sample (collected at the start of explantation for patients undergoing an explantation procedure), was collected to further characterize implant kinetics and relate serum to ocular ranibizumab concentrations. Given the limited number of aqueous or vitreous humor samples in this study and the variable time of collection due to differing time of refill-exchange, no summary of observed aqueous or vitreous humor data was provided. Aqueous humor concentrations were however included in the population PK analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Two populations were considered in the PK analysis:

- A Full PK population which consisted of all patients in the efficacy population who had at least one serum PK sample. The population had 58, 62, 63, and 41 patients in the PDS 10 mg/mL, PDS 40 mg/mL, PDS 100 mg/mL, and IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg monthly arms, respectively.
- A PK population with exclusions defined for the PDS arms, which consisted of 16, 25, and 27 patients in the PDS 10 mg/mL, PDS 40 mg/mL, and PDS 100 mg/mL, respectively, which excluded patients who had prior IVT bevacizumab, received fellow eye ranibizumab treatment, and/or received supplemental IVT ranibizumab.

## Serum PK Results

The median serum concentration-time profiles based on time in study (i.e., independent of whether or when a refill-exchange procedure was performed) are shown in Figure 19. This figure illustrates the overall serum ranibizumab exposure across PDS arms and the minimum concentration for the IVT ranibizumab injections arms; however, given the PRN dosing regimen in this study, this concentrationtime profile does not illustrate the release of ranibizumab from the PDS implant. Figure 20 shows the median serum concentration-time profile including data up to first refill-exchange procedure in the PK population with exclusions. A summary of PK parameter estimates for the PDS arms based on NCA is shown in Table 14.

## STUDY GR40548

Serum PK samples were collected from all patients at all sites at the randomization visit and at Weeks 4, 24, 36, and 96. At selected sites, additional PK samples were collected from patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm on Days 2 and 7 and Weeks 12, 48, and 72.

Limited PK samples were taken from patients in the IVT arm because the serum pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab following IVT administration had been well characterized. Serum PK samples were collected at pre-dose before IVT injection from all patients in the IVT arm at all sites at the randomization visit and at Weeks 4, 24, 36, and 96. In addition, at selected sites, a serum PK sample was collected in patients in the IVT arm 1 to 5 days after an IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection in order to collect a sample near Cmax.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Aqueous humor PK samples were collected along with serum PK samples from patients who consented to this optional sampling in both treatment arms on Weeks 24, 28, 48, 52, 72, 76 and 96. In addition, mandatory aqueous humor samples were collected at the time of meeting supplemental treatment criteria and the subsequent study visit or at an early study termination visit or explant visit.

The PK analysis populations included:

- PK Population (n = 247 in PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 165 in IVT arm) was based on the subset of patients in the Safety Population with at least one post study treatment PK sample available.
- PK-Evaluable Population (n = 83 in PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 76 in IVT arm) consisted patients in the PK population excluding patients receiving IVT injections of ranibizumab in the study eye post PDS implant (as supplemental treatment in the study eye), patients with fellow eye ranibizumab or bevacizumab treatment, or prior bevacizumab treatment in either eye.
- Aqueous Humor PK-Evaluable Population (n = 39 in PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 45 in IVT arm) was the subset of patients in the PK-Evaluable Population with at least one aqueous humor sample.

## STUDY GR40549

Optional aqueous humor samples were collected at enrolment (Cohorts 3 and 4 only) and at each refillexchange visit and 8 weeks post-refill-exchange visit. In addition, mandatory aqueous humor samples were collected at the time of meeting supplemental treatment criteria and the subsequent study visit. Serum samples were also collected at the same time points as aqueous humor samples, if the aqueous humor samples were collected.

The PK analysis populations include serum and aqueous humor PK data with the PK clinical cutoff date (CCOD) of 31 January 2025.

The PK analysis populations included:

- PK Population (n = 225 patients total; 75, 88, 13, and 49 patients in cohorts 1-4, respectively) consists of the subset of patients in the All PDS Population with at least one measurable post-study treatment PK sample available.
- PK-Evaluable Population (n = 67 patients total; 24, 21, 2, and 20 patients in cohort 1-4, respectively) consists of patients in the PK population, excluding patients receiving IVT ranibizumab injections in the study eye post PDS implant (as supplemental treatment), patients with fellow eye ranibizumab treatment, explantation, bilateral PDS implantation, or septum dislodgement. Serum PK observations from patients who received prior IVT bevacizumab treatment collected within 36 weeks from the last dose of bevacizumab were also excluded.
- Aqueous Humor PK-Evaluable Population (n = 123 patients total; 47, 39, 6, and 31 patients in cohort 1-4, respectively) consists of patients with at least one aqueous humor sample, excluding patients with supplemental treatment, explantation, or septum dislodgement. Aqueous humor PK observations from patients who received prior IVT bevacizumab treatment collected within 36 weeks from the last dose of bevacizumab were also excluded.

## 5.2.2.10. Pharmacokinetic interaction studies

N.A.

## 5.2.3. Pharmacodynamics

No specific pharmacodynamic studies were performed in humans.

For the assessment of PD parameters such as central subfield thickness (CST), centre point thickness (CPT) investigated in studies GX28228, GR40548 and GR40549, please refer to section 5.2.3. of this report.

Further PD investigations included the analysis of immunogenicity and a population PK/PD analysis of CST.

## 5.2.3.1. Mechanism of action

Ranibizumab binds to the receptor-binding domain of biologically active forms of VEGF-A. VEGF-A has been shown to cause neovascularization and leakage in models of ocular angiogenesis and vascular occlusion and its overexpression has been demonstrated to play a key role in vascular retinopathies. The binding of ranibizumab to VEGF-A prevents the interaction of VEGF-A with its receptors (VEGFR-1 and VEGFR-2) on the surface of endothelial cells, reducing endothelial cell proliferation, vascular leakage, and new blood vessel formation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.3.2. Primary and secondary pharmacology

For the assessment of PD parameters such as central subfield thickness (CST), centre point thickness (CPT) investigated in studies GX28228, GR40548 and GR40549, please refer to section 5.2.3. of this report.

## 5.2.3.3. Pharmacodynamic interactions with other medicinal products or substances

## Not applicable

## 5.2.3.4. Genetic differences in PD response

Not applicable

## 5.2.3.5. Immunological events

The potential for the PDS to induce an immunogenic response to ranibizumab was assessed in Studies GX28228 and GR40548.

It is important to note that all patients included in the PDS Phase II Study GX28228 and in the Phase III Study GR40548 had been treated with at least 1 prior IVT ranibizumab injection into their study eye and had no restrictions on prior ranibizumab treatment to the fellow eye. Thus, some patients could potentially have developed ADAs to ranibizumab in response to prior IVT anti-VEGF injections, resulting in a positive test at study baseline.

## Potential Impact of ADA on Safety

Summaries of intraocular inflammation by ADA and NAb status were prepared (see Table 21).

Most of the intraocular inflammation events occurred within the first 7 days following the PDS implantation surgery, with few events occurring after Day 37 and very few events occurring after refillexchanges, indicating that the intraocular inflammation seen with PDS was mostly related to the surgical procedure (Study GR40548 Final CSR Report No. 1110574, Section 6.3.5.3). There were no cases of posterior uveitis, including retinitis or retinal vasculitis.

## 5.2.4. Pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD)

A Population PK-CST model was developed where the concentration-time-course profile in vitreous was related to the efficacy endpoint CST. The model was used to simulate how CST changes over time with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W dosing regimen compared to IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W.

The previously developed PK-CST indirect response model with a linear drug effect on kout was used to describe the observed CST data, including the variable initial response based on dose-level from studies GX28228 and the long-term CST data from studies GR40548 and GR40549. The model was revised to include the PK parameters from the popPK developed by Kågedal et al. 2023 to derive the typical vitreous ranibizumab concentration to drive the effect on CST.

Data: In total, the dataset for developing the model included 588 patients with 25,813 CST observations. CST dynamics were observed to be similar in the extension study GR40549, therefore, additional CST data is unlikely to affect the parameter estimates.

Model development: The previously developed PK-CST model (Report No. 1104047) was evaluated for its ability to describe the new long-term CST data. It is an indirect response model with a linear drug effect on the elimination rate (kout), where all parameters were fixed to the values previously reported and ran using MAXEVAL = 0 procedure in NONMEM (PK-CST Report No. 1132088). Ranibizumab effect was modelled using the popPK parameters (Kågedal et al. 2023) derived previously and used for external validation of PK data. Actual dosing history was used to obtain longitudinal ranibizumab concentrations in the vitreous to drive the effect. The best model that described the observed long-term CST data was established by fitting the previously reported PK-CST model to the current dataset, using post-hoc Bayesian estimation (MAXEVAL set to 0 in the $ESTIMATION command). The PKPD analysis was carried out using NONMEM (Version 7.5.1, ICON Development Solutions, Elicott City, MD, USA) on workstations with Intel® Xeon꜠ processors, Ubuntu 20.04 and the GNU gfortran compiler (Version 9.4.0,

ftp://ftp.globomaxnm.com/Public/nonmem7/compilers). The NONMEM installation was validated according to qPharmetra SOP. Exploratory data analysis and post-processing of NONMEM results was carried out in R version 4.3.3. Most of the model development was carried out using first order conditional estimation with η-ϵ interaction (FOCE-I). Other methods such as importance sampling estimation (IMP) and stochastic approximation of the EM algorithm (SAEM) were also explored.

PK-CST model: Evaluation and qualification

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The following model structure of the PK-CST model was selected. The model for anatomical changes in terms of CST measured by OCT has been developed based on LADDER and PORTAL data and validated with Phase 3 data (ARCHWAY).

Figure 35: Schematic of the CST Model

<!-- image -->

Source: Report No 1132088

CST changes after administration of ranibizumab with PDS and monthly IVT injections was described by an indirect response model with a linear drug effect on the indirect response model elimination rate (kout). Baseline CST was separated into a disease baseline that could be affected by ranibizumab and a minimum baseline that was unaffected by ranibizumab, effectively acting as a maximum drug effect. Typical vitreous ranibizumab concentrations to drive the effect were derived from the popPK model. The resulting parameter estimates are provided in the Table below. A total of 25813 CST observations from 588 patients were included in the analysis. Structure and model estimates are equal to those

submitted previously.
