## 5.2.5. Dose selection and therapeutic window

The recommended dosing regimen is a PDS initial implant fill with ranibizumab 100 mg/mL, followed by subsequent refill-exchanges with 100 mg/mL Q24W. Referring to the applicant, this choice is supported by the following:

- The observed clinical safety and efficacy data from the dose-ranging Study GX28228, where ranibizumab concentration of 100 mg/mL in the PDS with PRN refill-exchanges was identified as the most effective among the studied PDS doses, with similar outcomes to monthly IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg IVT injections.
- The observed clinical safety and efficacy data in Study GR40548 Archway, which demonstrated that the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W regimen is non-inferior and equivalent to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W regimen and is generally tolerated.
- Observed aqueous humor and serum PK data and popPK analysis demonstrating that ranibizumab ocular and systemic concentrations following treatment with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W are within the range (Cmax - Cmin) of concentrations experienced with monthly IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injections.
- PK-CST analyses assessing the relationships between PK and CST suggest that the PDS 100 mg/mL provides similar reduction of CST for up to 24 weeks as IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W, while the lower PDS doses (10 mg/mL and 40 mg/mL) showed less pharmacological activity.

## 5.2.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical pharmacology

## 5.2.6.1. Discussion

## Pharmacokinetics

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The pharmacokinetics (PK) of ranibizumab with monthly IVT (IVT treatment) has generally been well described in AMD and DME patients. The PDS, in contrast to IVT monthly or pro re nata, is designed to provide continuous delivery of ranibizumab in the vitreous, therefore, the PK profile of ranibizumab administered via the PDS differs from that of ranibizumab administered via IVT injection.

Three studies provided data for the description of PK of ranibizumab delivered via the final PDS in nAMD patients; the pivotal Phase III Study GR40548 (Archway) and two supportive studies; Phase II Study GX28228 (Ladder) and open-label long-term extension Phase III Study GR40549 (Portal, this study is still ongoing and no CSR is available). All studies contributed with longitudinal serum samples and aqueous humor samples. Samples for immunogenicity assessment were collected only in studies GX28228 and GR40548, but not in GR40549.

PK was characterized by noncompartmental analysis in studies GX28228 and GR40548. In addition, PopPK analyses were conducted to describe ranibizumab PK in serum and predict ranibizumab concentrations in the vitreous and to explore covariate effects, as well as ranibizumab drug disposition following treatment with PDS.

The recommended dose of Susvimo is 2 mg (0.02 mL of 100 mg/mL solution) continuously delivered via the implant with refills administered every 24 weeks (approximately 6 months).

## Analytical methods

ELISA-based methods were used for the quantification of ranibizumab in human serum and aqueous or vitreous humor. Both methods were adequately validated and investigated performance parameters met the acceptance criteria of the EMA guidance (EMEA/CHMP/EWP/192217/2009 Rev. 1 Corr. 2**).

For the qualitative determination of anti-ranibizumab antibodies in human serum, a bridging antibody ELISA was developed and validated. The assay performed with adequate precision and sufficient drug tolerance. For the detection of neutralizing antibodies to ranibizumab, an ELISA-based assay was developed with a preceding step in which all VEGF present in the sample is removed by magnetic bead extraction. Validation was overall acceptable.

## PopPK model

The popPK model used for external validation has not been provided within a dedicated modelling and simulation report but could be found in a publication (Kågedal et al. 2023) and supplemental material. The model was built based on log-transformed serum and aqueous humor data from the Ladder, Archway, and Portal trials. An updated base model (from Xu at al. 2013) used as a starting point, however this (updated) base model could not be assessed from the publication. This hinders the detailed assessment of the Kågedal popPK model (model building, covariate step, …), being the underlying model for the external validation exercises conducted. Several assumptions including Vc are based on such previous analyses. However, since the data set was detailed not to be informative for the central volume of distribution in serum, fixing to 3270 mL could be agreed, since the values is in the expected range for the central distribution compartment.

From the publication it is also informed that based on PDS in vitro characterization (unpublished), it was estimated that 2.5% of the dose is pushed directly into the vitreous at refill exchange. To account for this bolus, the assumption was made that an additional dose to the vitreous (2.5% of the administered dose) was added in the analysis dataset. Whether or not (additional) observed data are justifying this assumption is unclear.

For the published popPK model used for the further external validation step, model diagnostics provided (Kågedal et al. 2023) suggest an overall acceptable description of PK data. A slight underprediction of serum PK data more than 6 months after refill for PDS 100 mg/mL (Ladder) could be detected. In addition, also just after PDS dose 100 mg/ml in the aqueous humor, the serum concentrations are slightly under predicted and the percentile range is not very well matched but shifted. ETA\\_shrinkage reached very pronounced levels up to 67% (VC) and Kr (47%). Data and model estimates (e.g. several error terms) indicate high degree of variability in the data.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

External validation step: No major trends were present in CWRES for both serum and aqueous humor data and both population and individual predictions. Thus, the performance of the model was deemed adequate for the external validation data for the purpose to describe the PK data. In this line, prediction-corrected VPCs stratified by nominal dose and study (all data) indicate an overall acceptably well description of data (serum and aqueous humor). Some deviations could be detected in the p5 and p95 percentiles.

## ADME

Drug release rate from PDS was characterized in vitro and revealed that the PDS implant continuously releases ranibizumab into the vitreous. The measured active release rate for ranibizumab (100 mg/mL) fill concentration was 3.55 µg/day at week 28 after both initial fill and refill and 2.06 µg/day at week 38 following the refill. According to the applicant, these values met both the 26.3-week and 36-week requirement for active release rate of 2.3 µg/day and 1.5 µg/day, respectively, as described in the specification. The active ranibizumab release rate specifications were derived from simulations using a PK/PD model built on data from study GX28228, which confirmed that an active release rate of 2.3 µg of ranibizumab per day at 26.3 weeks and 1.5 µg of ranibizumab per day at 36 weeks is required to achieve efficacious vitreous concentrations.

Higher release was seen during the first weeks after implantation (~ 10 - 17 µg/day) and release rate continuously decreased. Diffusion of ranibizumab was dependent on the formulation composition, i.e. drug concentration, pH and ionic strength.

As other PK parameters, Cmax and Tmax describing ranibizumab absorption or appearance in serum are dependent on release from PDS/vitreous compartment. Based on the rather infrequent collection of PK samples, Cmax and Tmax might not have been adequately captured, as already discussed in the preceding EMA scientific advice (EMEA/H/SA/4242/2/2019/III). According to PK data from study GX28228 in both the full PK population and PK population with exclusions, peak serum ranibizumab concentrations are detected approx. 1 month post implantation. After refill however, median Tmax was about 7 days. In response to the D120 LoQ during the initial MAA, the applicant elaborated that it is expected that the time of maximum serum concentrations after a refill-exchange procedure would be earlier than after implantation due to the refill bolus, which is a small dose (~2.5%) of ranibizumab that is released through the release control element into the vitreous at the time of a refill-exchange procedure. Due to the relatively flat concentration-time profile, small differences in the profile can lead to large differences in observed Tmax. In addition, the serum PK sample collection schedules in study GX28228 were different after implantation and after refill-exchange procedures, which may have contributed to variations in Tmax. The uncertainty regarding Tmax values is acknowledged. Ultimately, Tmax was not considered a key PK parameter for PDS due to the relatively flat concentration-time profile, and since no accumulation was observed with multiple dosing (i.e., at steady state), as expected based on the dosing interval and half-life.

Cmax in study GR40548 as well as the estimate for Cmax derived by population PK analysis was found to be approx. 50% lower than Cmax described in study GX28228, while Tmax was comparable and in both studies. The large %CV of Cmax in study GX28228 (256.6 - 272.5%) likely contributed to the higher mean or geometric mean Cmax as compared to study GR40548. The lower variability seen in study GR40548 (%CV=34.2) was explained to be a result of fewer observed high initial spikes after implant insertion. It was suggested that the high initial spikes observed in study GX28228 are caused by the transfer of a small amount of drug directly to serum, potentially due to transient direct access to blood circulation through the needle wound. Notably, the observed high initial spike concentrations in study GX28228 were not found to be related to changes to the implant insertion surgical procedure (IFU Version 10 and subsequent IFUs) or to any differences in the implant itself. The insertion tool used to perform the initial implant fill and implant insertion however changed between Studies GX28228 and GR40548 and, according to the applicant, it cannot be ruled out that the observed differences in the occurrence of high initial serum concentration spikes could be related to the change in insertion tool device. Ultimately, no definite root cause for the observed differences in high initial serum concentration spikes between Studies GX28228 and GR40548 was identified. However, the high initial spike concentrations observed in Study GX28228 were transient, did not result in continued high systemic concentrations and did obviously not correlate with the occurrence of vitreous haemorrhages. Vd and CL were not determined by noncompartmental analyses. In the popPK model, volume of the central compartment (Vc) was fixed to 3270 mL, which is the value estimated for Vc in the former model (M. Kagedal and T. Lu. Population pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab in age-related macular degeneration: an update, 2020). The initial systemic ranibizumab clearance (CL0) was estimated as 30,010 mL/day and the clearance at infinite time (CL0*FracCL) was estimated as 10,314 mL/day. The half-life of ranibizumab PDS 100 mg/mL is indicated as apparent half-life (ranging between 106 - 482 days, depending on study and/or analysis), since ranibizumab displays flip-flop kinetics after vitreal administration and t1/2 is highly dependent on the release of ranibizumab from the PDS and subsequent release from vitreous to serum. Parameters such as t1/2 could not be reliably determined by non-compartmental analysis in the individual studies due to e.g. sparse sampling, dependency on follow-up time until refill-exchange, and influence of time-varying systemic clearance. PopPK modelling was used, which was able to handle sparse sampling and to capture time-varying systemic clearance. Based on the popPK analysis, t1/2 in aqueous humour was 106 days and t1/2 in serum was 203 days, as described in the SmPC. Dose-proportionality Referring to the dose-exposure data from study GX28228 and the population PK analysis, PK of ranibizumab in serum and vitreous after administration by PDS appeared approximately doseproportional. Further dose-proportionality analyses conducted for the PK parameters AUCinf and Cmax revealed a linear relationship across all PDS dose levels evaluated (10-100 mg/mL). Intra- and inter-individual variability High variability in ranibizumab concentrations was observed early after dose (implantation/refill): some administrations showed large concentration spikes after dose while others did not. This is also resembled in a large %CV of Cmax in study GX28228 (256.6 - 272.5%). Variability described as %CV on AUC per dosing interval was 37 - 71% in studies GX28228 and GR40548. Similarly, in the popPK model (Kågedal et al. 2023), the error model consists of a proportional part in serum (0.303 (CI 95%: 0.281 - 0.328)) and in aqueous humor (0.768 (CI 95%: 0.671-0.846)) and one part with a high initial value in serum, 1.076 (CI 95%: 0.901-1.259), that declines with a rate constant 0.253 d -1 (CI 95%: 0.181 - 0.343). The between patient variability in vitreous concentrations is primarily a result of the estimated variability in PDS release rate which is informed by the observed rate of decline in serum and regarded high for Vc. Inter-individual variability in implant release rate was estimated to be rather small at Omega 0.0112 (10.6 %CV). IIV in Clearance (initial clearance at time of PDS dosing) was estimated to be at Omega 0.0450 (21.5 %CV). IIV in the elimination rate constant for flow from vitreous to serum was estimated to Omega 0.0886 (30.4 %CV), IIV in the serum residual error was calculated to Omega 0.3801 (68 %CV) and IIV in Vc was calculated to Omega 1.68 (209 %CV). In total, overall variability and assumed errors appear high, in particular for Vc. Vc was fixed to 3270 mL from previous analyses, a value that is also characterized by high shrinkage (67%). Of note, most recent data have not been used to estimate these values, being derived from data collected by data cut-off 2021.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## PK in the target population

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In study GR40548, the geometric mean serum ranibizumab concentration in the PK-evaluable population was 333 pg/mL at Week 24 (24 weeks after implant insertion) and 254 pg/mL and 242 pg/mL at Weeks 48 and 72 (24 weeks after 1st and 2nd refill-exchange, respectively). The geometric mean of serum Cmax was 450 pg/mL for the 100 mg/mL PDS, while serum Cmin was determined to be 300 pg/mL. In contrast, the geometric mean serum Cmax, determined in serum on Day 2 (24 hours post dose) was 1840 pg/mL, and serum Cmin was 28.8 - 58.9 pg/mL throughout the treatment period up to Week 72 for IVT ranibizumab injections. Thus, it is suggested that ranibizumab serum concentrations achieved with the PDS are contained within the concentration range achieved with monthly IVT ranibizumab injections at the 0.5 mg dose.

The change in serum concentrations over the 24-week refill-exchange interval was relatively small. In addition, Ctrough levels after 24 weeks with the 100 mg/mL PDS were largely above the Ctrough reached with monthly IVT injections of 0.5 mg ranibizumab. In this regard, less frequent dosing, as for example conducted in study GX28228, could have been considered as well. Still, the proposed dosing regimen does currently not give rise to safety concerns based on ranibizumab exposure, as no accumulation is observed and serum concentrations achieved with the PDS are contained within the concentration range achieved with monthly IVT ranibizumab injections at the 0.5 mg dose. The ongoing Velodrome study (WR42221) investigates PK, efficacy and safety of the PDS delivering ranibizumab 100 mg/mL Q36W, compared to the Q24W regimen in patients with nAMD, so a better assessment on the optimal refill-exchange interval might be possible in the future.

Serum AUC(0-168days) for the PDS in the PK-evaluable population was 56.27 ng*day/mL. PK analysis in aqueous humor confirmed that ranibizumab did not accumulate in the aqueous humor when administered with refill-exchange every 24 weeks.

Based on trough concentrations determined at Week 24, 48 and 72 in aqueous humor, ranibizumab concentrations reached with the 100 mg/mL PDS (865 - 1340 ng/mL) were approx. 3-fold higher than concentrations reached with IVT injections of 0.5 mg/mL (250 - 413 ng/mL). Peak concentrations of ranibizumab in aqueous humor after IVT injection have not been measured in this study. However, referring to literature, ranibizumab Cmax after IVT injection of 0.5 mg ranibizumab was 56,100 ng/mL (peak on Day 1 post injection). Thus, ranibizumab concentrations reached in aqueous humor with the 100 mg/mL PDS are also within the concentration range of IVT ranibizumab.

A decrease in both serum and aqueous humor concentrations was observed over time for the PDS 100 mg/mL arm, specifically if geometric mean concentrations, as presented in Table 16 of the D80 clinical AR, are referred to. This observation could be explained by some patients exhibiting extremely low concentrations at later timepoints. However, the overall changes were small and mean ranibizumab concentrations were still within the therapeutic range (within Cmin-Cmax observed with monthly IVT 0.5 mg injections). Of note, decreasing ranibizumab concentrations over time were not that obvious in the long-term extension Study GR40549, see below.

Study GR40549 is an ongoing open-label extension study enrolling patients from parent studies GX28228 or GR40548. PK results are overall consistent with previous findings in the parent studies. No clear decrease of ranibizumab concentration over time could be observed in this long-term extension study, but the study is not completed yet. The applicant committed to provide the full PK data set from study GR40549 once the study has been completed and the CSR is finalized (expected Q4 2030).

PK parameters derived from popPK analysis were overall comparable to the values described in the NCA analysis (study GX28228 and study GR40548). With the PDS (100 mg/mL Q24W), mean serum AUC24W was predicted to be 64,440 pg/mL*day, mean serum Cmax was predicted to be 463.6 pg/mL, and mean serum Cmin was predicted to be 287.4 pg/mL. With IVT administration of 0.5 mg Q4W, mean serum AUC24W was predicted to be 105,900 pg/mL*day, mean serum Cmax was predicted to be 1,912 pg/mL, and mean serum Cmin was predicted to be 97.87 pg/mL.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Aqueous exposure of ranibizumab with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W was predicted as follows: AUC24W was 341,600 ng/mL*day, Cmax was 2,981 ng/mL, and Cmin was 1,144 ng/mL. With IVT administration of 0.5 mg Q4W, mean aqueous AUC24W was predicted to be 720,900 ng/mL*day, mean aqueous Cmax was 14,330 ng/mL and mean aqueous Cmin was 712.9 ng/mL.

Vitreous exposure of ranibizumab with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W was predicted as follows: AUC24W was 3,132,000 ng/mL*day, Cmax was 27,330 ng/mL, and Cmin was 10,490 ng/mL. With IVT administration of 0.5 mg Q4W, mean vitreous AUC24W was predicted to be 6,609,000 ng/mL*day, mean vitreous Cmax was 131,400 ng/mL and mean vitreous Cmin was 6,536 ng/mL.

Conclusively, the popPK analysis further supports that, with the PDS at 100 mg/mL Q24W, ranibizumab concentrations in serum, aqueous humor and vitreous humor are maintained within the range (Cmax-Cmin) of monthly IVT 0.5 mg injections. Cmax was shown to be lower and Cmin was shown to be higher with the PDS as compared to IVT.

Ranibizumab concentrations are predicted to be &gt; 50,000-fold lower in serum as compared to vitreous. Overall, no relevant safety concerns are expected from above-described exposures achieved with the PDS as new sustained release formulation.

The popPK model was further used to estimate ranibizumab exposure in case of bilateral PDS implantation. Also here, it is indicated that bilateral PDS yields ranibizumab serum exposures within the Cmin-Cmax range of PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W (eye 1) and IVT 0.5 mg Q4W (eye 2).

## PK in special populations

In the popPK model, CrCL was found to be a significant covariate influencing systemic ranibizumab exposure, while no influence on vitreous exposure was detected. Higher CrCL led to an increase in ranibizumab CL. However, this effect was not deemed clinically relevant, considering that there is no impact on vitreous concentrations, and systemic ranibizumab levels generally remain low relative to levels required to inhibit VEGF systemically. Consequently, no impact on efficacy is expected and no dose adjustments are deemed necessary in case of renal impairment.

Given the mechanism of drug release for PDS implant (driven by ranibizumab diffusion), an impact of patient factors on the release rate was considered unlikely. The assessment of covariates on the PDS implant release rate was therefore limited to covariates of clinical interest, which are age and sex. Both were found to have no significant influence on PDS release rate.

## Pharmacodynamics

The mechanism of action of Susvimo is the same as for intravitreally injected ranibizumab. Ranibizumab is a humanised recombinant mAb fragment targeting the human vascular endothelial growth factor A (VEGF-A). By binding to VEGF-A isoforms it inhibits binding of VEGF-A to its receptors.

No specific pharmacodynamic studies were performed in humans. PD parameters investigated in studies GX28228, GR40548 and GR40549 (CST, CPT) are assessed in section 3 of this assessment report.

## Immunogenicity

In study GX28228, the treatment-emergent ADA incidence among patients treated with PDS 100 mg/mL PRN was 15.3%, while the incidence of treatment-emergent ADA after intravitreal injection of 0.5 mg ranibizumab was 14.6%. Treatment-emergent NAb incidence was 3.39% and 0% for the PDS and for the IVT 0.5 mg regimen, respectively.

In study GR40548, the treatment-emergent ADA incidence among patients treated with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W was 13.4%, while the incidence of treatment-emergent ADA after IVT injection of 0.5 mg ranibizumab was 9.1%. Treatment-emergent NAb incidence was 6.1% and 2.4% for the PDS and for the IVT 0.5 mg regimen, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As mentioned by the applicant, all ranibizumab ADA samples that were collected and analysed had corresponding ranibizumab concentrations that were unlikely to interfere in the ADA assay.

Overall, the incidence of ADA and NAb to ranibizumab was low. In study GR40548, the incidence of ADA and NAb was slightly higher in patients with PDS as compared to IVT administration. However, differences are not considered clinically meaningful. A similar ADA incidence range for the IVT regimen as previously observed (2.0-9.4%) in the IVT ranibizumab clinical studies was observed in study GR40548.

No significant (and consistent) impact of ADA positivity on ranibizumab exposure at Week 96, efficacy (as evaluated by change from baseline in BCVA at Week 96 and over time) or safety (intraocular inflammation) was observed. Noteworthy, it is agreed with the applicant that due to the low incidence of ADAs observed to date, no definite conclusions may be drawn from the data.

In general, considering the local administration with the PDS and the overall low ADA incidence rates, it is questionable in how far anti-ranibizumab ADA detected in serum could meaningfully impact PK or efficacy at the site of action. The data provided are acknowledged and no further concerns are raised.

## PK/PD analysis

Population PK/PD analysis was conducted to simulate the relationship between the different ranibizumab dosing regimens (PDS at the 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL and IVT regimen at 0.5 mg Q4W) and the efficacy endpoint central subfield thickness (CST).

Ranibizumab effect was modelled using the popPK parameters (Kågedal et al. 2023) derived previously and used for external validation of PK data. The use of the updated popPK model is supported and the use of pop PK-CST modelling to support the dosing regimen is acknowledged. The impact is regarded low in light of the available clinical Phase 3 data.

Model structure and parameter estimates are the same as submitted previously. Several evaluations and explorations for model selection have been conducted but seem to have issues in fitting long and short-term data and trends in doses. Thus, the previous PK-CST model deemed to be the best describing the new CST data, without any parameter estimation (MAXEVAL=0 in the $ESTIMATION command). This however indicates that the model is not very robust.

Diagnostic plots indicate some light trends were present for the CWRES over time from first dose post randomization or over PRED. Individual predictions do not fall on the identity line, however, the population prediction does not describe the data well, which is line with what has been assessed previously. As discussed by the applicant, the population prediction from an indirect response model is dependent on the pre-treatment baseline value. CST at baseline is considered to be highly variable (large IIV estimates) and thus, the entire curve is misrepresented if the pre-treatment baseline is poorly described for a subject. As a result, a fixed CSTmin was included in the model, presumably to create a functional model which allows predictions at all. However, this is not considered to truly reflect the in vivo situation. In summary, the informative value of the described PopPK/PD model should be considered with caution.

VPC plots indicate in general, that the medians are acceptably well predicted. A slight underprediction of the long-term decline of CST of the PDS 100 mg/mL dose group in the PORTAL study could be detected. However, the model has some issues in describing the border percentiles. This regards almost every study and dose.

Given that the model is deemed to be of low impact only, and the median values are acceptably well described, the simulations based on the model can be considered plausible to be considered supportive information only with regard to the rationale for the PDS dosing regimen of 100 mg/mL Q24W in nAMD patients.

Simulations illustrated that a PDS dose of 100 mg/mL refilled Q24W resulted in similar reduction of CST as Q4W IVT injections of 0.5 mg, while PDS 10 mg/mL gives an increase in CST after the two initial IVT injections. Additionally, the fraction of subjects that increase more than 50 µm in CST since time of implantation was analysed. Simulations revealed that approx. 17% of the subjects receiving the lowest PDS dose of 10 mg/mL increased more than 50 µm, while only 1% of the subjects in the highest PDS dose of 100 mg/mL increased more than 50 µm, most likely to occur in the few weeks before refill. These results overall support the choice of the 100 mg/mL Q24W PDS regimen.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.2.6.2. Conclusions

Overall, pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab delivered intravitreally by PDS has been adequately characterized. Since the PDS is a sustained release formulation, investigated PK parameters are highly dependent on the release from the PDS (and the vitreous compartment). PK results therefore naturally differ from PK observed with monthly IVT injections.

The recommended dosing regimen is supported by PK data. It was shown that ranibizumab serum, aqueous and vitreous concentrations achieved with the PDS at 100 mg/mL Q24W are maintained within the range (Cmax-Cmin) of monthly IVT 0.5 mg injections. As a result of the sustained release from PDS, Cmax was determined to be lower and Cmin was determined to be higher with the PDS as compared to IVT.

Overall, the incidence of ADA and NAb to ranibizumab administered by PDS was low and did not meaningfully differ from IVT administration. Currently, there is no indication of a relevant impact of the presence of ADA or NAb on PK, efficacy or safety. Due to the limited data available, no valid conclusions may however be drawn.

PK-PD simulations using a PK-CST model illustrated that a PDS dose of 100 mg/mL refilled Q24W resulted in similar reduction of CST as Q4W IVT injections of 0.5 mg. Simulations supported the 100 mg/mL Q24W dosing regimen as proposed for the PDS in the present application.

## 5.3. Clinical efficacy

Clinical evidence supporting the marketing authorisation application is primarily based on the pivotal Phase III clinical study (GR40548/ Archway) investigating the efficacy, safety and pharmacokinetics of the PDS in patients with wet AMD. In addition, results from the dose-finding Phase II study (GX28228/ Ladder, study complete) and the long-term extension study (GR40549/ Portal, study ongoing) are also provided to further substantiate the efficacy, safety and the B/R profile of the PDS.

## 5.3.1. Dose response study

Study GX28228 (Ladder) is a Phase II, multicentre, dose ranging, randomized, active treatmentcontrolled study. This study evaluated the efficacy, safety, and pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab delivered through the PDS using three different ranibizumab formulation arms (10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL), refilled on a PRN (pro-re nata, as needed) regimen, compared with the control arm (0.5 mg monthly IVT injections of 10 mg/mL ranibizumab formulation) in patients with nAMD. The study also evaluated the safety of the PDS.

Approximately 220 patients at up to 60 sites in the United States were planned to be randomized in a 3:3:3:2 ratio to PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL arms and ranibizumab IVT arm, respectively, within an approximate 24-month period of time.

The study has been completed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Study design

Figure 46: Study Schema (Figure 1 from final CSR)

<!-- image -->

M9 =Month 9; PRN =as needed (as per the refill criteria); PDS=Port Delivery System with ranibizumab

- a See Section 3.6.3 for details of pre-screeningrun-in treatment with intravitreal ranibizumab
- b See Section 3.6.1 for details of refill criteria

Patients had the implant (prefilled with approximately 20 µL of either the 10 mg/mL [approximately 0.2 mg dose], 40 mg/mL [approximately 0.8 mg dose], or 100 mg/mL formulation [approximately 2mg dose] of ranibizumab) surgically inserted in the study eye at the Day 1 visit following their randomization visit.

Starting at the Month 1 visit, patients were evaluated monthly for the need for implant refill with the 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, or 100 mg/mL formulations of ranibizumab according to their randomization as per protocol-specified refill criteria (see below). If the criteria were not met, no implant refill was given. At each refill, a volume of approximately 100 µL of ranibizumab was injected in situ into the implant through the septum to exchange the remaining contents of the implant with newly introduced ranibizumab. The volume of newly introduced ranibizumab remaining in the implant after the refill-exchange procedure was approximately 20 µL. After the initial fill of the implant with ranibizumab, patients were evaluated for their implant refill according to protocol-specified refill criteria (see below) at each of their scheduled visits until the study treatment completion.

A protocol amendment (Version 5) allowed enrolment and implant insertion surgeries to be paused to enable real-time review of post-implant insertion safety data by the Internal Monitoring Committee (IMC). The high rate of vitreous haemorrhage observed prompted a modification to the Instructions for Use (IFU). On implementation of this pause, dosing continued as per the protocol for patients who had already received study drug (e.g., patients who have performed Day 1 visit), while patients who had not yet received study drug stayed in Pre-Screening, Run-In, Screening, or Randomization period (as applicable) and received monthly open-label IVT ranibizumab treatment until enrolment and implant insertion surgeries were restarted. Patients who had been randomized but not yet received the study drug repeated the Randomization assessments after enrolment and implant insertion surgeries recommenced, and before receiving the study drug according to optimized IFU.

After Version 7 of the protocol was implemented, if a study patient met criteria for lack of clinical efficacy, he or she received a supplemental injection (referred to as rescue injections in the protocol) with open-label ranibizumab (0.5-mg IVT injection of 10 mg/mL formulation) followed by a refill with the 100 mg/mL formulation one month later. After that, he or she received the 100 mg/mL formulation for all future refills, when refill criteria were met.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Refill Criteria

Starting at the Month 1 visit, all randomized patients were assessed monthly for refill.

At 1 month after initial fill, patients randomized to the PDS arms had their implant refilled only if any of the following criteria were met:

- Decrease of ≥ 10 letters in BCVA at the current visit compared with the baseline BCVA, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Increase in CFT of ≥ 100 µm at the current visit compared with the baseline CFT, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Presence of new macular haemorrhage, due to nAMD disease activity

For subsequent assessments, patients randomized to the PDS arms had their implant refilled only if any of the following criteria were met:

- Increase in CFT of ≥ 75 μm on SD-OCT at the current visit compared with the average CFT over the last 2 available measurements, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Increase in CFT of ≥ 100 µm from the lowest CFT measurement on study, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Decrease of ≥ 5 letters in BCVA at the current visit compared with the average BCVA over the last 2 available measurements, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Decrease of ≥ 10 letters from best recorded BCVA on study, due to nAMD disease activity

OR

- Presence of new macular haemorrhage, due to nAMD disease activity

CFT measurements used to determine need for refill were assessed by the investigator. From 14 January 2017, CFT measurements were assessed by the investigator and then confirmed by a central reading centre.

Patients assigned to the PDS arms were scheduled for a safety evaluation visit 7 (± 2) days after each refill.

Management of Patients Who Met Lack of Clinical Efficacy Criteria

Criteria for meeting lack clinical efficacy are shown in the Table below (as per the protocol version at the time of the final analysis, these criteria were amended in Version 6 of the Protocol).

Patients who met criteria for lack of clinical efficacy received supplemental treatment with IVT injection of open-label ranibizumab. One month after meeting criteria for lack of clinical efficacy and receiving a supplemental IVT ranibizumab injection, the patient received a mandatory refill with the 100 mg/mL ranibizumab formulation. At the next monthly visit and until the end of the study, the patient received a refill with the 100 mg/mL ranibizumab formulation if the patient met the protocol-defined refill criteria.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Table 22: Lack of Clinical Efficacy Criteria (Table 6 from final CSR)

| Event                     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|---------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Lack of clinical efficacy | Loss in BCVA of ≥ 15 letters from the best recorded BCVA on study following 2 consecutive ranibizumab implant refills (as per protocol- specified refill criteria) occurring 1 month apart due to nAMD disease activity and not attributable to a change in the ocular media (i.e., cornea, lens, aqueous or vitreous humor, epiretinal membrane development, etc.) unless there was at least a five-letter increase in BCVA, in which case a refill occurred. and/or Increase in CFT ≥ 150 μm from the lowest CFT measurement on study following 2 consecutive ranibizumab implant refills (as per protocol-specified refill criteria) occurring 1 month apart due to nAMD disease activity and not attributable to a change in the ocular media (i.e., cornea, lens, aqueous or vitreous humor, epiretinal membrane development, etc.) unless there was a decrease in CFT ≥ 75 microns from the last refill, in which case a refill occurred. |

The study was to continue until the Sponsor decided, based on the primary analysis results, to either terminate the study and discontinue study treatment or to offer patients entry into the PDS Extension study (GR40549 [Portal]). Study participation for patients in the implant arms (excluding screening period) was expected to last approximately 13-38 months dependent on the date of their randomization to the study.

## Study population

Patients with subfoveal neovascularization secondary to AMD diagnosed within 9 months and treated with and responsive to IVT anti-VEGF agents were enrolled in the study.

The key inclusion criteria were as follows:

- Age ≥ 50 years
- Newly diagnosed with nAMD within 9 months prior to screening visit
- Patient must have received at least 2 prior anti-VEGF injections (including ranibizumab, aflibercept or bevacizumab). However, the most recent anti-VEGF injection must have been ranibizumab and must have occurred at least 7 days prior to the screening visit.
- Demonstrated response to prior IVT anti-VEGF treatment, as evidenced by the following:

Decrease in central foveal thickness (CFT) of &gt;50 µm since commencing IVT anti-VEGF treatment OR

Stable or improved BCVA since commencing IVT anti-VEGF treatment

- BCVA using ETDRS charts of 20/20 - 20/200 Snellen equivalent
- All macular CNV lesions were permitted

The key exclusion criteria were as follows:

- Subfoveal fibrosis, subfoveal atrophy, or subretinal hemorrhage (greater than 2.54 mm 2 involving the centre of the fovea) in the study eye

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Active infectious conjunctivitis, keratitis, scleritis, or endophthalmitis in either eye
- Use of anticoagulants, antiplatelets (other than aspirin), or medications known to exert similar effects at the time of study entry for a pre-existing condition

The study population enrolled in the Phase II study GX28228 was overall comparable to the population enrolled in the Phase III study with regard to ocular eligibility criteria. In addition, patients must have demonstrated response to any anti-VEGF IVT therapy within 9 months prior to screening.

However, in study GX28228 a minimum of 2 prior anti-VEGF injections (including ranibizumab, aflibercept or bevacizumab) was required for inclusion in the study, in contrast to the Phase III study, where at least 3 prior injections and one additional ranibizumab IVT injection at screening were required in order to increase the likelihood for patients to reach a vision plateau prior to randomization. Thus, patients in the Phase II study had most likely not yet reached their vision plateau.

The primary efficacy objective of Study GX28228 was to evaluate the relative efficacy of the PDS based on time from implant insertion until a patient first requires the refill-exchange according to protocol-defined refill criteria.

To demonstrate the consistency of the results between study GX28228 and the pivotal Phase III study, the applicant has presented additional supportive analyses of BCVA of study GX28228 performed using similar statistical methods as in study GR40548.

## Disposition of subjects

Between 28 September 2015 and 21 August 2018, 244 patients were enrolled at up to 60 sites in the US. Of those 244 patients, 12 patients were enrolled in the non-randomized OAT sub-study - see Figure below. Thus, overall, 232 patients were enrolled in the main study. Seven of these were randomized at a non-compliant site and were thus excluded from further analysis. Finally, 225 patients were randomized in a 3:3:3:2 ratio to PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL arms and to the ranibizumab IVT arm, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Figure 47: Patient Disposition (Figure 2 from final CSR)

<!-- image -->

ªOral antithrombotic substudy results are presented separately in Section 9

bSee Section 3.9.7.5 and Section 4.3

For the OAT (oral anti-thrombotic) sub-study, patients were enrolled to a single-arm cohort after the completion of the main study enrolment. Hence, patient characteristics in the OAT sub-study are not comparable with PDS 100 mg/mL pro re nata (PRN) patients in the main study GX28228. The followup time was also shorter for these patients (range: 8 to 14 months), compared to the patients in the main study GX28228 (mean of 22 months).

The randomized population comprised 225 patients. Five of them, who were randomized into the PDS arms, decided not to undergo implantation because of the unexpectedly high incidence of vitreous haemorrhage. Apart from these 5 patients, all others were treated with study treatment as randomized, leading to 220 patients in the Efficacy Population (see below).

This high rate of vitreous haemorrhage after implant insertion at the beginning of the study resulted in a revision and optimisation of the surgical procedures. Consequently, the rate of vitreous haemorrhage decreased from 50.0% (11/22 patients) prior to optimisation to 3.8% (6/157) afterwards in the 10 months after implant insertion and to 5.1% (8/157) over the entire study duration. It was stressed by the applicant that none of the vitreous haemorrhage events was related to the refill-exchange procedure.

It was stated that, during the study, in total 20 patients withdrew from treatment with PDS (10 patients [17.2%] in the PDS 10 mg/mL arm, 7 patients [11.3%] in the PDS 40 mg/mL arm and 3 patients [5.1%] in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm), with the main reasons in the PDS arms being lack of efficacy (8 patients in total) and AEs (6 patients in total). All withdrawals in the PDS arms occurred after Month 10. Nine of the 10 patients in the PDS 10 mg/mL arm, 4 of the 7 patients in the PDS 40 mg/mL arm and 2 of the 3 patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm had their implants removed prior to their last visit.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In the IVT arm, 5 patients (12.2%) withdrew from treatment; the main reason for discontinuation was withdrawal of consent by subject (3 patients [7.3%]).

The primary analysis was performed after the last patient's Month 9 visit occurred based on the 28 June 2018 snapshot data. The final analysis was performed after the database lock occurred on 2 May 2019, based on the Study GX28228 Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP) Version 3.

The Efficacy Population was defined as all patients who were randomly assigned to study treatment and received at least one study treatment. Patient data were summarized by treatment arm and analysed according to the treatment actually received and not according to the treatment to which they were randomized, in the event of a discrepancy. Patients who received an implant were considered PDS patients.

## Demographic characteristics

Overall, in the Efficacy Population, the mean (SD) age of the patients was 73.8 (8.4) years; 141 (61.4%) were female; and the majority patients were White (215 patients [97.7%]). Baseline demographics were generally balanced across treatment groups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Baseline ocular characteristics/ Prior and concomitant treatments

Baseline ocular characteristics were generally balanced across treatment groups. Overall, 145 patients (65.9%) had 20/40 or better vision at baseline (after receiving initial anti-VEGF IVT treatment to check for treatment response).

The mean number of anti-VEGF injections prior to randomization in the study eye was 2.7-3.1 in the PDS arms and 2.9 in the IVT arm, and all patients had received at least 1 prior dose of IVT ranibizumab in the study eye as per protocol. Forty-four patients (20.0%) received IVT bevacizumab as part of the pre-screening anti-VEGF treatment with no marked imbalances.

Prior surgical procedures in the study eye were reported in 69 patients overall (31.4%) and were generally balanced across treatment arms. The most common surgical procedure in the study eye before screening was cataract surgery.

In the first 10 months, concurrent ocular procedures were performed in 28 patients in the PDS arms (15.6%) and in no patients in the IVT arm. The most frequently reported concurrent ocular procedure was vitrectomy for vitreous haemorrhage or retinal detachment (13 patients [5.9%] in the PDS arms).

## Previous and concurrent diseases

The most frequently reported diseases in the medical history were systemic hypertension (147 [66.8%]) and diabetes (40 [18.2%]). During the study, eye disorders were observed more frequently in the PDS arms, including cataracts (reported in 77.6%-86.4% of patients in the PDS arms compared with 70.7% of patients in the IVT arm) and vitreous detachment (reported in 46.8%-56.9% in the PDS arms compared with 39.0% in the IVT arm).

## Efficacy results

The main objective of the dose-finding study GX28228 was to identify the most appropriate dose and regimen for the PDS for the subsequent pivotal Phase III study.

## Primary endpoint

The primary endpoint, TTFR (time from implant insertion until a patient first requires the implant refill according to protocol-defined refill criteria), was analysed both at the primary analysis, which was conducted after the last patient's Month 9 visit occurred, and at the final analysis. Both analysis results were similar. The final analysis results are described below.

For patients who did not meet the protocol-defined refill criteria on or before the analysis cut-off date, the time of refill was censored. The censoring date was defined as the date of a patient's last visit on or before the cut-off date, or the date when the patient discontinued from the study, whichever occurred first. Observed data were used for these analyses.

Time to first refill was also censored for the following patients:

- At the time of an IVT anti-VEGF injection in the study eye if administered before the first refill
- At the time the refill criteria could not be assessed, which was defined as at least 2 refill variables (BCVA, CFT, or new macular haemorrhage) unable to be evaluated for any reason, or were affected by a clinical reason different from nAMD activity, before the first refill

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## - At the time of explant

To support the dose level selection among the implant groups for future trials, the difference in time to first required refill between each of the following pairs of PDS arms were assessed using a log-rank test stratified by baseline BCVA score (≤ 65 lett ers vs. ≥ 66 letters) and number of prior anti-VEGF IVT injections (≤ 3 vs. ≥ 4), at a one-sided significance level of 15%:

- o PDS 100 mg/mL versus PDS 10 mg/mL
- o PDS 100 mg/mL versus PDS 40 mg/mL
- o PDS 40 mg/mL versus PDS 10 mg/mL

The median TTFR was 8.7, 13.0, and 15.8 months for the PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL and 100 mg/mL arms, respectively (final analysis).

A dose response was observed: PDS 40 mg/mL and 100 mg/mL arms tended to have a longer time to first required refill, compared with PDS 10 mg/mL arm; the differences were statistically significant at two-sided 0.3 level (i.e. 1-sided 0.15 level) with HR=0.56 (70% CI: 0.43, 0.72) and log-rank p-value = 0.0166 between PDS 100 mg/mL and 10 mg/mL arms and HR=0.67 (70% CI: 0.52, 0.85) and log-rank p=0.0875 between PDS 40 mg/mL and 10 mg/mL arms (final analysis). TTFR in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm tended to be longer than that in the PDS 40 mg/mL arm (HR=0.89 with 70% CI: 0.69, 1.15 at the final analysis), but the difference was not statistically significant.

| BCVA and new macular hemorrhageª               | 0                     | 0                     | 0                      |
| CFT and new macular hemorrhageª                | 1 (1.3%)              | 4 (7.5%)              | 0                      |

## Secondary endpoints

Visual acuity

The mean BCVA changes from baseline at Month 9 in the PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL and 100 mg/mL

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

groups, based on all observed data, were -3.1, 0.2, and 4.8 letters, respectively, which support a dose response. The change in BCVA in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm was comparable to that in the IVT arm, with mean difference of 1.6 letters (95% CI: ±1.8, 4.9). The results were similar for analyses with censoring for IVT anti-VEGF injection in the study eye, use of selected prohibited medication, or receiving an explant.

With regard to the change in BCVA from baseline over time, there was a decrease in BCVA observed in the PDS arms immediately after implant insertion, as expected from any vitreo-retinal surgery. By Months 2-3, BCVA in the PDS arms, on average, recovered to baseline values. Change in BCVA generally remained stable afterwards in the PDS 10 mg/mL and 40 mg/mL arms. The change in BCVA in the 100 mg/mL arm remained stable and was generally comparable to the IVT arm after Month 4.

The change from baseline in BCVA averaged over Month 1 through Month 10 supported the main analysis findings of a dose response in PDS arm and similar change in BCVA outcome between PDS 100 mg/mL and the IVT arms after recovery from the surgery.

The proportions of patients losing &lt;15 letters, &lt;10 letters, &lt;5 letters, and &lt;0 letters and gaining ≥15 letters over through Month 10 were also supportive of the main analysis findings of a dose response in PDS arms and similar change in BCVA outcome between PDS 100 mg/mL and the IVT arms after recovery from the surgery.

Additional supportive analyses were performed for BCVA, following those BCVA analyses performed in the pivotal Phase III study GR40548, using the MMRM method as well as the trimmed mean approach:

With regard to the change from baseline in BCVA score averaged over Month 9 and Month 10 analysed with MMRM, the findings were similar to the main analysis results described above, with the change in BCVA in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm being comparable to the IVT arm, with adjusted mean difference of 1.8 (95% CI: -1.7, 5.2). The findings from the MMRM analyses with BCVA with data censored after supplemental IVT anti-VEGF injection in the study eye, select prohibited therapy, or explant were also similar to the main analysis.

The trimmed means approach was used to evaluate the difference in change in BCVA averaged over Months 9 and 10 between PDS 100 mg/mL and the IVT arm (ranibizumab 0.5 mg). 10% of patients who were considered to have the worst outcomes were trimmed from analysis, including those patients in the PDS arm who received a supplemental IVT anti-VEGF injection in the study eye, received any prohibited therapy (other than oral corticosteroids more than 10 mg/day or any fellow eye treatment), or underwent explantation; or if patients in any treatment arm discontinued study treatment due to lack of clinical efficacy or adverse event. 6.8% of patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm met the 'must be trimmed' criteria. The trimmed mean changes from baseline averaged over Month 9 and Month 10 were +6.0 and -+5.4 letters for the PDS 100 mg/mL and the IVT arms, respectively, with the adjusted difference in mean changes (95.03% CI) of 0.6 letters (-2.2, 3.4).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant concluded that, in this supportive trimmed mean analysis, the changes in BCVA were comparable between the PDS 100 mg/mL and the IVT arm and consistent with the other BCVA analyses performed.

## Anatomical outcomes

The mean change from baseline in CFT, excluding both retinal pigment epithelium (RPE) and pigment epithelial detachment (PED) height (i.e., measured between the internal limiting membrane and the inner third of the RPE), through 24 months showed little change in the PDS 40 mg/mL, PDS 100 mg/mL and IVT arms, and increased modestly in the PDS 10 mg/mL arm (see below).

Figure 50: Plot of Mean Change from Baseline in CFT (without RPE and PED Height) over Time during the Entire Study - Observed Data without Censoring - Efficacy Population (Figure 6 from final CSR)

Mean Change from Baseline in Central Foveal Thickness (without RPE+PED Height) over Time during Entire Study (Observed Data without Censoring), EfficacyPopulation Protocol: GX28228

<!-- image -->

Based on all observed data, the mean change from baseline in CFT without RPE and PED height in the PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, 100 mg/mL, and IVT arms, were 28.0, 2.9, -3.4, and -6.9 microns respectively, indicating very little difference between the PDS 40 mg/mL, 100 mg/mL, and IVT arms.

There was a bigger difference in mean change from baseline in CFT if the measurement included RPE and PED height (i.e., measured between the internal limiting membrane and the Bruch's membrane) between the PDS groups and the IVT group; specifically, the mean change from baseline in CFT at Month 9 were 21.9, 23.6, 7.5 and -29.6 microns in the PDS 10 mg/mL, PDS 40 mg/mL, PDS 100 mg/mL and IVT arms, respectively.

## Implant functionality

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Among the 15 explanted implants (mostly due to lack of clinical efficacy, see CSP Section 5.4.4), 12 were analysed for functionality by in-vitro release testing. No evidence of implant clogging was observed in any of the examined implants.

For primary and secondary endpoints, a dose response was observed across the PDS treatment arms. In addition, clinical benefit in terms of visual acuity (BCVA) and anatomical changes observed for patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm was comparable to monthly ranibizumab injections. Therefore, the 100 mg/mL concentration was chosen for the PDS arm in the pivotal Phase III study. Choosing this dose is overall considered adequate, based on the available data from study GX28228.

However, the rationale for choosing the Q24W refill regimen for further clinical development was questioned during the evaluation by the CHMP, based on the fact that in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm, more than 68% of patients proceeded 9 months and more than 59% went even 12 months not meeting the refill criteria. Furthermore, the median time to first implant refill was 15.8 months in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm. For those patients, a Q24W refill interval might bear the risk of overdosing. Within this context, a longer time to refill might have been adequate for further clinical development, also against the background of less frequent burdening refill-exchange procedures.

The applicant argues that vision deterioration with pro-re-nata (PRN) treatment was seen in Ladder Study PDS 100 mg/mL PRN arm after patients rolled over into Study GR40549 and that the initial BCVA gains were lost at Month 21 post-implantation when the mean BCVA went back to preimplantation levels, concluding that this loss in BCVA was most likely due to the long-term effect of the initial PRN regimen used in Ladder Study, where a clinically meaningful nAMD worsening had to be observed before receiving a refill-exchange.

It is noted by the applicant that a Q24W refill-exchange regimen does not represent a risk of overdosing, since the remaining content of the implant is fully exchanged with fresh ranibizumab during each refill-exchange procedure. This implies that residual ranibizumab present prior to refillexchange is removed and that there is no risk of accumulation of drug product. Rather than an overdosing risk, by using a fixed Q24W regimen, the applicant aims to reduce the risk of underdosing patients if exposed to an extended dosing interval, which could result in long-term BCVA deterioration.

It is thus overall acknowledged that the fixed 24W regimen could ensure an earlier treatment for the patients, being an advantage for most of them if they are avoiding the risk of a clinically meaningful nAMD worsening that could be translated in a progression of the disease with functional parameters deterioration. The fixed 24W regimen is therefore agreed.

## 5.3.2. Main study

## 5.3.2.1. Study GR40548 (Archway)

## 5.3.2.1.1. Study title

A Phase III, Multicentre, Randomized, Visual Assessor-Masked, Active Comparator Study of the Efficacy, Safety, and Pharmacokinetics of the Port Delivery System with Ranibizumab in Patients with Neovascular Age-Related Macular Degeneration

## 5.3.2.1.2. Study design

Study GR40548 (Archway) is a Phase III, randomized, multicentre, open-label (visual assessor [VA]-masked), active-comparator study designed to assess the efficacy, safety and pharmacokinetics of ranibizumab 100 mg/mL every 24 weeks (Q24W) delivered via the PDS compared with ranibizumab 0.5 mg administered via IVT injection every 4 weeks (Q4W) in patients with nAMD.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Approximately 360 patients were planned to be enrolled at approximately 90 sites in the United States; a total of 418 were randomized in a 3:2 ratio, and 415 received treatment at 78 centres in the US. Due to a high speed of enrolment, combined with a lower screen failure rate than expected, the number of patients enrolled exceeded the number required according to the sample size calculation.

The study population comprised patients with choroidal neovascularization secondary to AMD, diagnosed within 9 months prior to screening and treated with at least 3 injections with any anti-VEGF agent within the last 6 months prior to screening. The primary efficacy endpoint (change in BCVA score from baseline) was assessed at Weeks 36 and 40. As a key secondary endpoint requested by the EMA, change in BCVA score from baseline was also assessed at Weeks 44 and 48.

Study GR40548 was designed to investigate if continuous delivery of ranibizumab from the implant, with a Q24W fixed period between refill-exchange intervals, results in less frequent need for treatment while maintaining optimal visual outcomes in patients with nAMD responsive to anti-VEGF treatment.

Figure 51: Study schema (Figure 1 from final CSR)

<!-- image -->

D=day; Rd=randomization.

- Patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm may be eligible for supplemental treatment with intravitreal ranibizumab 0.5 mg at Weeks 16, 20, 40, 44, 64, 68, 88, and 92.

At Week 96, patients could consent to enter the open-label extension Study GR40549 (Portal). In the event a patient was unable to complete Archway at Week 96 and enroll in Portal on the same day, the patient remained in Study GR40549 until the last study visit and enrollment in Study GR40549 (Portal) could occur and received regular interval treatment by following the alternate scenarios and the schedules of activities described in the Protocol Appendix 1 and Appendix 2.

At the end of the GR40548 study, patients had the option of entering Study GR40549 (Portal), where patients with an implant in place continued to undergo refill-exchanges with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W and patients in the IVT arm received the PDS implant and subsequently received refill-exchanges with PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W.

## Treatment

PDS and customized formulation of ranibizumab 100 mg/mL used to fill or refill-exchange the implant

The PDS and ranibizumab 100 mg/mL for the PDS were supplied by the Sponsor for the initial fill and refill-exchange of the implant. Ranibizumab was formulated as a sterile solution aseptically filled in a sterile 2-mL stoppered glass vial. Each vial contained 0.5 mL of the 100 mg/mL formulation of ranibizumab aqueous solution in 10 mM histidine hydrochloride, 240 mM sucrose, and 0.01% polysorbate 20, pH 5.5.

Week

It consists of a 214-residue light chain linked by a disulfide bond at its C-terminus to the 231 residue N-terminal segment of the heavy chain. Ranibizumab is not glycosylated and has a molecular mass of 48,000 Da. |

## Formulation of IVT ranibizumab 10 mg/mL

Ranibizumab for IVT injection was supplied by the Sponsor and was formulated as a sterile, colourless to pale yellow solution. Ranibizumab was supplied as a preservative-free, sterile solution in a singleuse contained designed to deliver 0.05 mL of ranibizumab 10 mg/mL solution with 10 mM histidine hydrochloride, 10% α,α-trehalose dihydrate, 0.01% polysorbate 20, pH 5.5.

## Study drug dosage and administration

## PDS with ranibizumab

The study-specific implant initial filling, insertion, refill-exchange and implant removal procedures are outline in the PDS Instructions for Use (IFU) document.

Prior implantation, the implant was filled with approx. 20µl of the 100 mg/mL formulation of ranibizumab (approximately 2-mg dose of ranibizumab). Then, the implant was surgically inserted in

the patient's study eye at the Day 1 visit following their randomization visit. After the initial fill of the implant with ranibizumab, patients received implant refill-exchanges at fixed 24-week intervals.

At each refill-exchange, a volume of approximately 100 µL ranibizumab was injected in situ into the implant through the septum to exchange the contents of the implant with newly introduced ranibizumab. The volume of newly introduced ranibizumab in the implant after the refill-exchange procedure was approximately 20 µL. Missed implant refill-exchanges were made up no later than the next scheduled study visit. Subsequent refill-exchanges were administered according to the study treatment schedule relative to Day 1 as outlined in Protocol Appendix 1 until study completion.

## Supplemental treatment

Patients randomized to the PDS 100 mg/mL arm were eligible for supplemental treatment with IVT ranibizumab (0.5 mg IVT injections of 10 mg/mL formulation) at Weeks 16 and 20 (after implant insertion) and at Weeks 40, 44, 64, 68, 88, and 92 if any of the following criteria were met in the study eye:

- Decrease of ≥15 letters from the best recorded BCVA in the study, due to nAMD disease activity.

## OR

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Increase of ≥150 µm in central subfield thickness (CST) on spectral domain optical coherence tomography (SD - OCT) from the lowest CST measurement in the study, due to nAMD disease activity.

## OR

- Increase of ≥100 µm in CST on SD-OCT from the lowest CST measurement in the study associated with a decrease of ≥10 letters from the best recorded BCVA during the study, due to nAMD disease activity.

(CST was assessed by central reading centre with boundaries of the inner limiting membrane (IML) to Bruch´s Membrane)

## Ranibizumab IVT injection

Ranibizumab (10 mg/mL), supplied by the Sponsor, was used in the study eye as follows:

- during the run-in period and at the screening visit for applicable patients (CSR Section 3.6.2.3),
- as study treatment for patients in the IVT arm; as supplemental treatment for the PDS 100 mg/mL arm (CSR Section 3.6.2.1),
- if delaying surgery was required,
- as nAMD treatment in the fellow eye for patients, per investigator's discretion;
- and if a patient in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm discontinued study treatment and started receiving ranibizumab IVT injections in study eye, per investigator's discretion.

Commercially available IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg supply was used only if an IVT ranibizumab injection was necessary per protocol and study supply was not available at the site.

Patients in the IVT arm received their first IVT injection of 50 µL of the ranibizumab 10 mg/mL (0.5 mg dose) at the Day 1 visit, which occurred at the conclusion of the randomization visit. Afterward, patients received IVT ranibizumab injections of 50 µL of the 10 mg/mL formulation Q4W at each scheduled study visit until Week 92. Missed treatments were not made up.

## IVT dosing prior to screening

There were 5 eligibility scenarios based on prior IVT anti-VEGF treatment history:

## 1. Patients newly diagnosed with nAMD who are anti-VEGF treatment-naive

If patients in this group satisfied all other eligibility criteria (other than prior anti-VEGF treatment criteria in the study eye, see List of Inclusion Criteria) and sign the Informed Consent Form, patients:

- Received 3 monthly (28 [±7] days) IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injections in the run-in period to determine whether they demonstrated response to anti-VEGF treatment as outlined per the eligibility criteria.

- After the third run-in IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection, patients proceeded to screening, scheduled 28 (±7) days from the last administered IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection, and received an additional IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the end of the screening visit.

- If all eligibility criteria were then met, patients proceeded to the randomization visit.

## 2. Patients diagnosed with nAMD in the study eye within 9 months prior to screening who had been treated with one or 2 intravitreal anti-VEGF injections within the last 6 months

If patients in this group satisfied all other eligibility criteria (other than prior anti-VEGF treatment criteria in the study eye, see Section 3.5) and sign the Informed Consent Form, patients:

- Received up to 3 IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injections in the run-in period to meet the required 3 anti-VEGF IVT injections within 6 months prior to screening.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- After completing the required run-in treatments, patients proceeded to screening, scheduled 28 (±7) days from the last administered IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection, and received an additional IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the end of the screening visit.
- If all eligibility criteria were then met, patients proceeded to the randomization visit.
3. Patients diagnosed with nAMD in the study eye within 9 months prior to screening who have been treated with 3 intravitreal anti-VEGF injections within 6 months prior to screening

If patients in this group satisfied all other eligibility criteria (see Section 3.5) and signed the Informed Consent Form, patients:

- Proceeded directly to screening, scheduled ≥21 days from the last IVT anti -VEGF injection administered per standard of care, and received an additional IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the end of the screening visit.
- If all eligibility criteria were then met, patients proceeded to the randomization visit.
4. Patients diagnosed with nAMD in the study eye within 9 months prior to screening who had been treated with at least 4 intravitreal anti-VEGF injections within 6 months prior to screening and with the most recent dose being aflibercept or bevacizumab

If patients from this group satisfied all other eligibility criteria (see Section 3.5) and signed the Informed Consent Form, patients:

- Proceeded directly to screening, scheduled ≥21 days from the last IVT anti-VEGF injection, and received an IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the end of the screening visit.
- If all eligibility criteria were then met, patients proceeded to the randomization visit.
5. Patients diagnosed with nAMD in the study eye within 9 months prior to screening who had been treated with at least 4 intravitreal anti-VEGF injections within 6 months prior to screening, with the most recent dose being ranibizumab

If patients from this group satisfied all other eligibility criteria (see Section 3.5) and signed the Informed Consent Form, patients:

- Proceeded directly to the screening visit, which should be scheduled ≥7 days from the last administered ranibizumab dose, and did not receive another IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the screening visit, provided that the randomization and Day 1 visits was completed within 21-28 days after the last ranibizumab dose administered in the study eye prior to screening.
- If the randomization and Day 1 visit cannot be completed within the allotted time window, the patient should be scheduled for a screening visit at ≥21 days from the last ranibizumab dose and will receive an additional IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injection at the end of the screening visit.
- If all eligibility criteria were then met, patients proceeded to the randomization visit.

## Criteria for dose interruption or withdrawal from study/ treatment

Patients had the right to voluntarily withdraw from the study at any time for any reason; likewise, the investigator could withdraw a patient from the study at any time.

Study treatment dose interruption and/or study treatment discontinuation or study discontinuation following an AE was determined according to the criteria in the Table below.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>
