Proportion of Patients with a BCVA Score of 38 Letters or Worse

## Patients from Study GR40548 using GR40548 Baseline

The proportion of patients from Study GR40548 that had a BCVA score of 38 letters or worse (20/200 approximate Snellen equivalent) remained under 10% from Week 96 (end of Study GR40548) through Week 288 in both arms (Figure 14). At Week 288, the proportion of patients with a BCVA score of 38 letters or worse (95% CI) was 9.0% (5.5, 14.3) and 7.5% (3.9, 14.2) in patients from the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL and IVT arms, respectively.

A similar trend was observed when using the GR40549 baseline.

## Patients from Study GX28228 using GR40549 Baseline

The proportion of patients from Study GX28228 that had a BCVA score of 38 letters or worse (20/200 approximate Snellen equivalent) remained under 20% through Week 240 from entry into Study GR40549 across all arms, except in patients from the PDS 40 mg/mL arm, with proportions above 20% at Weeks 216, 228, and 240 (Figure 15). At Week 240, the proportion of patients with a BCVA score of 38 letters or worse (95% CI) was 11.1% (4.4, 25.3), 24.3% (13.4, 40.1), 17.8% (9.3, 31.3), and 3.6% (0.6, 17.7) in patients from the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, 100 mg/mL, and GX28228 IVT arms, respectively.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Proportion of Patients with a BCVA Score of 69 Letters or Better

## Patients from Study GR40548 using GR40548 Baseline

The proportion of patients from Study GR40548 that had a BCVA score of 69 letters or better (20/40 approximate Snellen equivalent) generally decreased over time from Week 96 (end of Study GR40548) through Week 288 in both arms. At Week 288, the proportion of patients with a BCVA score of 69 letters or better (95% CI) was 50.9% (43.4, 58.4) and 67.9% (58.5, 76.0) in the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL and IVT arms, respectively.

A similar trend was observed when using the GR40549 baseline.

## Patients from Study GX28228 using GR40549 Baseline

A decrease in the proportion of patients with BCVA score of 69 letters or better (20/40 approximate Snellen equivalent) was observed for patients from Study GX28228 through Week 240 across all arms apart from the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm where the proportion remained generally constant over time. It is important to note that the mean baseline BCVA scores at entry into Study GR40549 differed across arms (ranging from 64.9 letters in the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm to 75.2 letters in the GX28228 IVT arm) and the sample size in these arms was small, limiting any meaningful data interpretation.

At Week 240, the proportion of patients with a BCVA score of 69 letters or better (95% CI) across arms showed a similar trend, with 58.3% (42.2, 72.9), 45.9% (31.0, 61.6), 44.4% (30.9, 58.8), and 42.9% (26.5, 60.9) in the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, 100 mg/mL, and GX28228 IVT arms, respectively.

Proportion of Patients with Loss of Visual Acuity from Baseline over time

## Patients from Study GR40548 using GR40548 Baseline

The proportion of patients from Study GR40548 that experienced a loss of &lt;15 letters remained high but generally decreased over time in both arms. At Week 288, the proportion of patients (95% CI) that experienced a loss of &lt;15 letters was 74.3% (67.1, 80.3) in the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 79.2% (70.6, 85.9) in the GR40548 IVT arm. The proportion of patients with a loss of &lt;10 letters and &lt;5 letters showed a similar trend over time.

A similar trend was observed when using the GR40549 baseline.

## Patients from Study GX28228 using GR40549 Baseline

The proportion of patients from Study GX28228 that experienced a loss of &lt;15 letters generally decreased over time in all arms. At Week 240, the proportion of patients (95% CI) that experienced a loss of &lt;15 letters was 86.1% (71.3, 93.9) in the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm, 62.2% (46.1, 75.9) in the GX28228 PDS 40 mg/mL arm, 60.0% (45.5, 73.0) in the GX28228 PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 67.9% (49.3, 82.1) in the GX28228 IVT arm. The proportion of patients with a loss of &lt;10 letters and &lt;5 letters showed a similar trend over time.

## Change from Baseline in Centre Point Thickness (CPT)

## Patients from Study GR40548 using GR40548 Baseline

Overall, a small numerical decrease from baseline in CPT over time was observed for patients from Study GR40548 in the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and in the GR40548 IVT arm at Week 288. The mean change in CPT thickness (95% CI) was -11.3 µm (-25.5, 3.0) for the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and -22.9 µm (-33.0, -12.9) for the GR40548 IVT arm.

A similar trend to the GR40548 baseline was observed when using the GR40549 baseline.

## Patients from Study GX28228 using GR40549 Baseline

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

All arms displayed an overall small numerical decrease in CPT over time for patients from Study GX28228. At Week 240, the mean change from baseline in CPT (95% CI) was -36.0 µm (-68.0, -4.0) for the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm, -24.5 µm (-63.0, 13.9) for the GX28228 PDS 40 mg/mL arm, - 16.6 µm (-35.6, 2.4) for the GX28228 PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and -1.0 µm (-37.8, 35.8) for the GX28228 IVT arm.

## Supplemental Treatment with Intravitreal Ranibizumab

## Patients from Study GR40548

A total of 352 patients from Study GR40548 (220 patients from the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 132 patients from the GR40548 IVT arm, who attended at least one non-refill visit in the study period from day of implantation) were assessed for supplemental treatment through the CCOD of 31 January 2025. A total of 49/220 (22.3%) patients from the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 18/132 (13.6%) patients from the GR40548 IVT arm received supplemental treatment.

The majority of patients did not receive supplemental treatment from the day of implant (77.7% of patients in the GR40548 PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 86.4% of patients in the GR40548 IVT arm).

Within each treatment interval, the proportion of patients requiring a supplemental treatment was low, generally fluctuating at around 5% in each of the GR40548 arms (range through Week 288 from GR40548 baseline: 1.2%-11.1% in PDS 100 mg/mL arm, 0-4.6% in the IVT arm).

## Patients from Study GX28228

A total of 189 patients from Study GX28228 (49 patients from the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm, 55 patients from the GX28228 PDS 40 mg/mL arm, 56 patients from the GX28228 PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 29 patients from the GX28228 IVT arm who attended at least one non-refill visit in the study period following enrolment into Study GR40549) were assessed for supplemental treatment in GR40549.

In total, 9/49 (18.4%) patients from the GX28228 PDS 10 mg/mL arm, 10/55 (18.2%) patients from the GX28228 PDS 40 mg/mL arm, 10/56 (17.9%) patients from the GX28228 PDS 100 mg/mL arm, and 8/29 (27.6%) patients from the GX28228 IVT arm received supplemental treatment.

Within each treatment interval, the proportion of patients requiring a supplemental treatment was overall low, with fluctuations generally around 5% in each of the GX28228 arms (range 0-14.3%).

## 5.3.5. Analysis performed across trials (pooled analyses and metaanalysis)

Given the differences in PDS refill-exchange procedure frequency (PRN in Study GX28228 versus Q24W in Study GR40548) and the difference in pre-randomization IVT ranibizumab injections (2 in Study GX28288 and 4 in Study GR40548), the comparative efficacy between the PDS and the monthly IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg arm is described separately for Studies GR40548 and GX28228. No pooled analysis has been performed.

In Study GR40548, changes in BCVA observed in the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W arm were non-inferior and equivalent to those in the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W arm, at the average of 36 and 40 weeks with a difference of -0.3 letters (95.03% CI: -1.7, 1.1), and at the average of weeks 44 and 48 with a difference in adjusted means between the PDS and IVT arms of -0.2 letters (95.03% CI: -1.8, 1.3).

In Study GX28228, change in BCVA observed at the average of Month 9 and Month 10 in the PDS 100 mg/mL PRN arm were similar to those in the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W arm with a difference of 1.8 letters (95% CI: -1.7, 5.2).

It is considered acceptable that the applicant does not present pooled efficacy data, due to the differences between the two studies (differences in pre-treatment requirements [2 prior anti-VEGF

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

treatments vs 4 prior anti-VEGF treatments] as well as in refilling frequencies [PRN in the Ladder study vs Q24W in the Archway study]).

## 5.3.6. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical efficacy

## 5.3.6.1. Discussion

This is a re-submission for Susvimo, a port delivery system (PDS) with ranibizumab, consisting of an intraocular drug delivery device designed to continuously release the customized formulation of ranibizumab into the eye over time. Susvimo is proposed for the treatment of adult patients with neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD).

The initial MAA was withdrawn in May 2023 (EMA/206018/2022), after the occurrence of cases of septum dislodgement, due to the Sponsor's inability to provide the required certificates ('Declaration of Conformity') for the PDS devices as part of the responses to the D180 LoOI.

The clinical dossier of Susvimo comprises efficacy data from three clinical trials.

Study GR40548 (Archway), conducted in patients with wet AMD responsive to anti-VEGF treatment, was considered pivotal for demonstrating therapeutic equivalence in terms of visual acuity and anatomical outcomes between PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W and IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W.

Supportive efficacy data were derived from the dose-finding study GX28228 (Ladder) and the longterm extension study GR40549 (Portal) in the same patient population.

Studies were conducted in accordance with the principles of GCP. Regulatory consultation for Scientific Advice was requested, and recommendations were implemented. The port delivery system (PDS) enables sustained release of ranibizumab, which is already an established treatment for nAMD in the EU. The number and design of clinical studies was adequate for an efficacy evaluation. The clinical development programme was generally appropriately designed.

The applicant proposes the following indication wording:

Susvimo is indicated in adult patients for the treatment of neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD) who have achieved stable disease after previously responding to intravitreal treatment with a vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) inhibitor medication.

The indication wording is supported by appropriate study data.

## Design and conduct of main study

Demonstration of equivalence/ non-inferiority on efficacy level is based on one pivotal Phase III study. The GR40548 (Archway) study is a randomized, multicentre, open-label (VA-masked), active comparator study comparing efficacy, safety and PK of the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W to monthly IVT ranibizumab injection 0.5 mg in patients with nAMD responsive to anti-VEGF treatment. Only patients with a maximum of 9 months since diagnosis who had received at least 4 prior anti-VEGF injections were eligible.

In total, 418 patients were 3:2 randomized to one of the two treatment arms at 78 centres in the US. Baseline BCVA score was used as stratification factor (&lt;74 letters versus ≥74 letters). Study duration for participants was 96 week an interval considered sufficient to evaluate the efficacy, immunogenicity, and safety of the PDS.

The applicant states that the study was conducted in accordance with the ethical principles of the Declaration of Helsinki and were consistent with ICH Guidance and the applicable local regulatory requirements and laws.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The study was completed in 2021 (LPLV 23 June 2021). With this submission, the final CSR has been submitted, including Week 88-92 analyses. In addition, the primary CSR with the primary analysis (CCOD 27 March 2020) as well as an updated CSR, including data through Week 48 (CCOD of 11 September 2020) have been submitted. Five study amendments to the protocol are reported by the Sponsor. GR40548 is an open-label study. Patients and study site personnel were not masked with regard to patient assignment because of difficulties of maintaining masking following the surgical procedure. Additional safety visits were planned only to the implant arms, and implant visualisation occurred during ophthalmic examinations. According to the Sponsor, the visual acuity examiners (VAE) were masked to the treatment as best as possible to patient study eye assignment, study visit eye and patient treatment assignment. The VAE had no access to patient's BCVA scores from previous visits, and patients and unmasked personnel were advised not to discuss the study eye assignment. However, in November 2019, the Sponsor identified that, due to a limitation within the Clinical Trial Management System (CTMS), 144 VAEs at 66 study sites were listed under the category of the Study Coordinator Role, and therefore were inadvertently registered to receive automated emails from the portal used to share study information with site staff. Within the subject line, information of the patient number, study visit week and type was included, which could indirectly inform the patient's treatment arm and study visit type to the masked VAEs (assigned to 232 patients total). With regard to the issue of potential un-masking of the VAE due to erroneously sent automated emails including information of the patient number, study visit week and type, which could indirectly inform the patient's treatment arm and study visit type to the masked VAE, the applicant states that dedicated data analyses have been conducted, not suggesting any bias resulting from this GCP breach. However, to further clarify that there is no critical issue with regard to data integrity resulting from the erroneously sent automated emails, the applicant performed descriptive analyses to explore whether data would suggest any bias resulting from this breach. Mean BCVA and mean change from baseline in BCVA were summarized according to treatment group and whether or not the VAE received an email at Week 16, Week 20, and Week 40 visits. Furthermore, the applicant conducted post hoc analyses to assess whether this issue introduced bias into the primary endpoint results. The primary analysis was repeated in the Efficacy Population (n=409), stratifying patients by whether their Week 36 visit occurred on or before and after the discontinuation of the emails on 5 Nov 2019. Treatment arm differences in the primary endpoint were comparable across both subgroups. To further test robustness, the same subgroup analysis was repeated after restricting the Efficacy Population to patients at sites where VAEs had received the emails (n=387). Results were also consistent across both subgroups. Analysis of the results did not suggest biases that would likely impact the efficacy assessments. The general study design of the GR40548 study was in line with previous EMA SA. It was agreed by the CHMP that subjects enrolled should have received and been found responsive to prior treatment with anti-VEGFs. The requirement of 3 or more previous IVT injections plus a mandatory boost injection at the screening visit was also supported. Furthermore, it was anticipated that this should be reflected in the label, which has been followed by the applicant. The applicant has chosen a study population with an initial diagnosis of wet AMD within 9 months prior the screening visit, in order to avoid inclusion of a very advanced patient population. It is endorsed that patients should already have received and been found response to prior anti-VEGF treatment. The restriction of enrolment to patients diagnosed within 9 months prior to screening limits external validity to the broader nAMD population of patients with longer disease duration typically encountered in clinical practise. However, the applicant justified the inclusion criterion based on the risk of irreversible long-term structural damage in nAMD, which may contribute to excessive visual acuity loss. Limiting inclusion to recent onset nAMD is therefore considered plausible to reduce bias from chronic anatomical

damage and to allow clearer interpretation of treatment effects. Moreover, data from LTE Study GR40549 indicate comparable treatment effectiveness of the PDS in patients with both shorter and longer disease duration, supporting its efficacy beyond recently diagnosed nAMD. Accordingly, the proposed patient population is considered acceptable.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The sample size calculation for study GR40548 is comprehensible and reproducible, the randomisation scheme is considered acceptable.

From the Phase II dose-finding study GX28228 (Ladder), the PDS 100 mg/ml formulation was chosen for the subsequent pivotal study. Selection of this dose is overall considered acceptable, based on the available efficacy data from study GX28228.

## Endpoints:

The primary efficacy objective was to show equivalence and non-inferiority (NI) of the PDS 100 mg/mL arm compared to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg arm, as assessed by visual acuity changes. This objective is generally of clinical interest, as Susvimo is intended to maintain optimal visual outcomes while potentially resulting in less frequent need for treatment.

The corresponding primary efficacy endpoint was the mean change from baseline in BCVA score averaged over Wks 36 and 40, assessed using the ETDRS visual acuity chart at a starting distance of 4 metres with a pre-specified equivalence/ NI margin of 4.5 letters, in the Efficacy population.

However, during EMA advice the chosen time points as well as the margin were critically reflected. The evaluation of the mean change in BCVA at the time of trough for the PDS (i.e. when the amount of ranibizumab in the implant should be the lowest) just before implant refill was proposed. In addition, the non-inferiority margin of 4.5 letters was considered too wide by SAWP/ CHMP, since a BCVA loss of 5 letters is a criterion for re-treatment and is thus of clinical relevance. This was overall followed by the Sponsor: the mean changes in BCVA from baseline averaged over Week 36 and Week 40 and averaged over Week 44 and Week 48 were evaluated as key secondary endpoints, with a NI margin of 3.9 letters. This is acknowledged.

In the EMA Scientific Advice, it was also recommended to the applicant to add secondary endpoints evaluating efficacy at later time points, such as Week 64 and 68, and Week 88 and 92. Those data have been submitted with this application.

Overall, change in BCVA is considered to be a clinically meaningful measure that reflects functional visual outcomes and captures disease progression; thereby, serving as a robust outcome for maintaining therapeutic efficacy. The study design appears appropriate for its intended purpose of comparing the efficacy (and safety) of Q24W PDS 100 mg/mL treatment with the Q4W IVT ranibizumab injections.

Several secondary endpoints, such as the change of BCVA over time, the proportion of patients with a BCVA score of 38 letters or worse and of 69 letters or better, the proportion of patients who lose &lt; 10 or &lt; 5 letters or gain ≥ 0 letters over time, and the proportion of PDS-treated patients requiring supplemental treatment with IVT ranibizumab injections were included. The anatomical endpoints of change in CPT and CST over time were also evaluated, serving as indicators of disease activity and therapeutic response. Patient-related outcomes (PROs) were incorporated to evaluate preferences for ranibizumab delivered via PDS and treatment satisfaction at Week 40. Although PROs were also included in the long-term extension study GR40549, only questionnaire data from patients in the former IVT treatment arm were collected at the 40-week timepoint in this extension study. It would have been also informative to collect data at study completion of the pivotal study GR40548 (after 3 years) or even in the longer-term (study GR40549). Overall, the secondary and exploratory endpoints are of clinical relevance and are supported.

Methodologically, the study design and analyses are appropriate to investigate the research question, but some assumptions are questionable and need further clarification.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The estimand framework was only introduced in the second version of the SAP and upon request of the FDA. The second version of the SAP was finalised on 21 April 2020, whereas the clinical cutoff for the primary CSR was designated as 27 March 2020 (in the first page of the CSR, this is stated as '27 March 2019', but this is thought to be a typo as 1) the last patient was included in June 2019 and the primary analysis included all randomised patients and 2) the date of March 2020 is mentioned in the CSR text). Hence, formally, the SAP was finalised a few weeks after the database lock for the primary analysis. However, most critical aspects (e.g. population definition, strategies to handle supplemental treatment and discontinuation) were already defined in the first protocol version, so this issue is not considered critical.

The primary analysis population includes subjects 'grouped according to treatment actually received'. In a non-inferiority/equivalence setting, this approach can be considered conservative, i.e. favouring the null, and is thus acceptable.

The PEP was to be measured via MMRM including the treatment group, time, treatment-by-time interaction, baseline BCVA score (continuous), and the randomization stratification factor of baseline BCVA (&lt; 74 letters vs. ≥ 74 letters) fixed effects. The MMRM assumes Missingness At Random (MAR); the applicant is aware of the criticalities of this assumption and proposes several supplemental analyses (see below).

The applicant states that 'all observed measurements will be included in the analysis regardless whether or not a patient has an intercurrent event', i.e., all intercurrent events are handled as treatment policy. In general, treatment policy in a NI/equivalence setting is not guaranteed to be conservative. Especially intercurrent events like 'more than 1 supplemental treatment', that may have a compensatory effect on the outcome, may lead to an underestimation of treatment differences, which is not endorsed in an equivalence trial. On the contrary, intercurrent events (ICE) that may have a worsening effect on the outcome are acceptable to be treated by the treatment policy estimand. Indeed, many of the ICEs defined in the estimand might have washed out the effect of the IMP and a different strategy (e.g. composite strategy or co-primary endpoints) would have been preferable. An analysis of a supplementary estimand using a different strategy for these ICEs was performed but its rationale is not fully understood (see below).

The analyses were performed:

- Trimmed mean analysis: the analysis aimed to 'assess the difference in BVCA between two treatments using a truncated distribution, truncating patients with the worst outcome, with the assumption that patients have the worst outcome after intercurrent events'. The description of the method seems to suggest that patients who had an intercurrent event throughout the first 40 weeks and/or patients with missing week 36 or 40 assessment will be either completely removed from the analysis or trimmed.
- Analyses using different handling rules for intercurrent event: with the primary analysis addressing every ICE as treatment policy, this sensitivity analysis aims to use different methods to handle ICEs: 1) last observation carried forward (LOCF) - in case of ICEs, this refers to the last (nonmissing?) observation pre-ICE; 2) exclusion of the data post-ICE and implicit MAR imputation via MMRM.

These approaches are not considered conservative in a non-inferiority/equivalence setting. The trimmed mean analysis removes or ignores the largest values, hence it seems to be anti-conservative in a non-inferiority/equivalence setting. The applicant was therefore requested to provide an additional analysis, of an estimand handling the ICEs with a composite strategy. The applicant acknowledged that the strategy to handle intercurrent events (ICE) using a treatment policy approach, although agreed with the FDA, is not conservative in the investigated non-inferiority setting, but also stated that a considerably small proportion of participants (2.8%) experienced ICEs which might have affected the primary estimand. Nevertheless, the requested sensitivity analysis still resulted in a 95% CI fully within the -3.9 non-inferiority margin, which corroborated the conclusions of the study.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Furthermore, the applicant provided analyses of a secondary estimand, defined post hoc, that handles all intercurrent events with the hypothetical strategy. The results are within the NI margin also for this estimand, which is reassuring.

The Applicant also provided further details on the number of missing values per timepoint and treatment arm were requested. The proportion of missing values at Weeks 36/40, 44/48 and 88/92 were generally low with slight imbalances between the treatment arms. However, due to the low number of missing values, this is considered to have little impact on the efficacy results. It was noted that starting from Week 44, the number of missing values and missed visits increased compared to earlier time points. The applicant explained these to be potentially COVID-19-related.

An additional estimand to assess the impact of COVID-19 on the study conduct was formulated. Most ICEs are addressed via hypothetical estimand, which is agreeable. Some ICEs (discontinuation due to AE or lack of efficacy and use of prohibited medication) were handled via treatment policy as in the main estimand, which is not optimal, but considering the supportive results available from a range of sensitivity analyses performed, no concern arises.

95.03% CIs were presented; 0.03% was taken away from the global alpha level of 5% to account for the three iDMC unmasked data reviews (each review got a 0.01% penalty). This approach was requested by FDA but is not formally backed up by literature nor motivated by the applicant. For the efficacy endpoints change from baseline in BCVA, CPT and CST, as well as the proportion of patients with a change of vision from baseline averaged over weeks 36 and 40, supplementary results were provided in the dossier using the 95%CI. Results were consistent with analyses using a 95.03% CI.

Analyses of relevant subgroups were prespecified and the corresponding forest plots were provided.

With regard to participant flow, 418 patients were enrolled and randomized. Of these 418 patients, 3 patients randomized to the PDS arm were never treated. The Efficacy population (i.e. randomized and treated) therefore comprised 415 patients (248 in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 167 in the IVT arm).

Study completion was overall high.

Through Week 96, 14 patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 13 patients in the IVT arm discontinued the study; thus 87.9% and 65.9%, respectively, completed study at Week 96). Through Week 96, patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm had received an initial fill and a mean of 2.9 refills while patients in the IVT ranibizumab arm had received a mean of 22.9 treatments out of a possible 25 treatments. This demonstrates that the PDS regimen significantly reduces the number of injections required when adhering to the Q4W regimen with IVT. However, in the EU, where T&amp;E protocols are used, the number of injections and the overall burden of the healthcare system have already been reduced. The PROs generally pointed towards a higher acceptance for the PDS regimen; but the extent of benefit in treatment burden with the PDS in the EU remains uncertain.

The overall time on study was balanced across treatment arms (mean 96.2 weeks in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm and mean 94.6 weeks in the IVT arm through the entire study).

The most common reason for study discontinuation in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm was death (8/248 [3.2%]) while the most common reason in the IVT arm was withdrawal by patient (7/167 [4.2%]).

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

With this submission, the applicant provides the results of the primary efficacy analysis for study GR40548 with the primary CSR (CCOD 27 March 2020), including Week 40 data. In addition, an updated CSR (CCOD 11 Sept 2020) has been provided, including Week 48 data, as well as the final CSR (LPLV 23 June 2021), including Week 88-92 data.

Baseline demographic data were well balanced between treatment groups in terms of age, gender, ethnicity, race, weight, BMI and tobacco use. The majority were female (59.0%) and white (96.6%).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Other ethnic groups were only represented to a small extent. As there is no evidence that the therapeutic effect of ranibizumab varies across ethnic populations, this matter will not be subject to further consideration. Baseline ocular characteristics of the study eye were also comparable, by mean BCVA score, number of anti-VEGF injections, time since diagnosis and number of IVT ranibizumab injections administered prior to initiation. A greater proportion of patients in the PDS group subsequently received anti-VEGF therapy for the fellow eye (FE) compared to those in the IVT group (20.2% in the PDS arm vs. 11.4% in the IVT arm). This is a direct consequence of a higher rate of nAMD cases in the FE in this group (28.2% vs 19.8%, respectively). As there is no reason to doubt integrity of the randomisation process and other baseline characteristics were balanced, this seems to be a chance finding. The use of ocular concomitant medications was notably higher in the PDS arm (76.6%) compared to the IVT arm (37.7%), which was primarily attributable to protocol-mandated short-term use of ocular medications for PDS implant procedures. As these finding reflect procedural requirements, it is not considered a cause of concern. Other baseline ocular and systemic conditions were generally balanced across treatment arms.

The primary efficacy population, defined as all patients who received the study treatment and analysed according to the actually received treatment, is acceptable for this equivalence trial, especially as a sensitivity analysis for the PP population, defined as all patients from the primary efficacy population without major protocol deviations, is provided.

The PP population used for the sensitivity analysis excluded a total of 18 patients in the PDS arm and 10 patients in the IVT arm.

Within this context, prior to study unblinding, major protocol deviations were reviewed and a determination of the population for PP analysis was made, based on whether the deviation was expected to impact the planned efficacy assessments.

Up to Week 48, major protocol deviations were reported in 120 patients (28.9%) overall (78 [31.5 %]

in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm and 42 [25.1%] in the IVT arm). The imbalance in rate of major protocol deviations was driven largely by medical deviations applicable only to PDS-treated patients (for example, aspirin/NSAIDs not interrupted per protocol in 14 patients [5.6%]).

From the overall 120 patients who were reported to have major protocol deviations, only 28 patients (n=18 in the PDS arm and n=10 in the IVT arm) were excluded from the Per Protocol population. Those were patients with major protocol deviations likely to have a clinical impact on BCVA, such as: uncertified staff performed BCVA testing, selected missed visits at weeks 44 and 48, VA examiner performed other study-related tasks or assessed previous visit VA scores, patient with long-standing nAMD disease not representing the target population, patient not having received at least 3 anti-VEGF injections prior to the screening visit, patient having received an additional out-of-protocol anti VEGF injection at D7 per investigator´s discretion, and others. This is considered acceptable, against the background that the sensitivity analyses of the primary and key secondary endpoint (change in BCVA from baseline averaged over weeks 36 and 40, and over Weeks 44 and 48, respectively) performed in the PP population (i.e. excluding patients with major protocol deviations as detailed above) were consistent with the corresponding primary analyses in the efficacy population.

The pivotal study GR40548 met its primary endpoint - equivalence and non-inferiority have been demonstrated for the PDS 100 mg/mL arm (Q24W) compared to IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W (for the pre-defined margin of ±4.5 letters as well as for the ±3.9 letters margin requested by EMA), as measured by the change from baseline in BCVA at the average of week 36 and week 40. The difference in adjusted means between the treatment arms was -0.3 letters (95.03% CI -1.7, 1.1). This was supported by sensitivity analyses in the PP population as well as by supplemental analyses for the primary endpoint (trimmed mean analysis, MMRM model using different rules for measures after intercurrent events), which were consistent with the primary analysis.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Overall, it has to be pointed out that the BCVA changes from baseline in both arms were rather small, which can be attributed to the study population, having already reached a plateau of response at baseline due to the prior anti-VEGF treatments.

In addition, the key secondary endpoints, which had been requested by EMA, were met: Non-inferiority of the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W regimen to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W regimen was also demonstrated when using a NI margin of 3.9 letters for the change from baseline in BCVA at the average of Week 36 and Week 40, as well as at the average of Week 44 and Week 48 (difference in adjusted means of -0.2 [95.03% CI -1.8, 1.3]) and at the average of Week 88 and Week 92 (difference in adjusted means of -0.6 [95.03% CI -2.5, 1.3]). Supplemental analyses and sensitivity analyses further supported the robustness of the findings for this key secondary endpoint.

Notably, results of change in BCVA trended slightly in favour of IVT treatment; however, the differences are clinically negligible and statistically non-significant.

Several other secondary endpoints evaluated the effect of PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W on visual function compared to monthly IVT injections. The results overall supported the outcome of the primary analysis.

Retinal thickness was evaluated by measuring CPT change as secondary EP, supported by CST change as exploratory endpoint; both representing surrogate endpoints for anatomical response to study treatment. Overall, CPT and CST remained stable over time in both treatment arms, indicating that continuous supply with ranibizumab (either via PDS or via IVT injection) stabilizes retinal thickness.

In the PDS 100 mg/mL arm, a trend towards increase in CPT and CST leading up to the scheduled refill-exchange followed by a decrease after the refill-exchange was observed throughout the study, as stated by the applicant. After each refill-exchange, CPT and CST generally tended to decrease to baseline levels, according to the applicant. The average change for CPT was up to approximately 10 microns, for CST up to approximately 20 microns.

In order to ensure that patients were achieving sufficient vitreous concentrations of ranibizumab to control their disease, the protocol permitted patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm to receive supplemental treatment at 8 weeks and/or 4 weeks prior to each scheduled refill-exchange procedure if criteria based on BCVA and CST changes were met. During the first (up to 24 weeks) and second treatment interval (24-48 weeks), 1.6% and 5.4% of patients fulfilled supplemental treatment criteria and received such treatment. During the third (48-72 weeks) and fourth treatment interval (72-96 weeks), 5.2% and 5.3% of patients received supplemental treatment. A total of 12.6% of patients had received at least one supplemental treatment. Analysis of the Swimmer Plots depicting supplemental treatment and actual refill times indicated that additional IVT injections mainly occurred immediately before pre-specified PDS refills, with the highest frequency observed at Weeks 40 and 68. Most patients received one or two supplemental ranibizumab injections and only three patients needed multiple doses. Exploratory analyses indicated that the need for supplementary ranibizumab treatment in the PDS group could not be attributed to any systematic patient- or treatment-related factors (e.g., study sites, demographic characteristics, baseline ocular characteristics, drug exposure, or treatment compliance); rather, BCVA decline, and CST increase were the most common reasons for supplemental treatment eligibility. Patients who received supplemental treatment generally maintained or returned to baseline BCVA and CST levels. Therefore, supplemental treatment appears effective in stabilizing visual acuity and retinal anatomy.

Two different PROs were evaluated at Week 40. PPPQ assessed the proportion of patients who reported preferring ranibizumab treatment via the PDS compared with IVT treatment and MacTSQ measured treatment satisfaction in patients. After Week 40, 93.2% of patients (218 out of 234 patients) preferred the PDS method for continuous ranibizumab delivery over IVT injections, mainly due to fewer treatments and less discomfort. Only 1.3% favoured injections, while 5.6% had no preference. MacTSQ scores were similar in both treatment groups at baseline and remained comparable at Week 40. Since patients were unmasked in the pivotal study, subjectivity bias cannot be ruled out, but it appears that PDS offers a convenient option without compromising patient satisfaction. Subgroup analyses were performed for the primary endpoint, mean change in BCVA over Weeks 36-40, and the secondary endpoint, mean change in CPT at Week 36. Improvements in BCVA and CPT were similar regardless of age (&lt; 65 years, 65 to &lt; 75 years, ≥ 75 years) , sex (male/female), number of prior anti-VEGF injections (&lt; 5 vs ≥ 5 prior injections), or baseline BCVA scores (&lt; 74 letters vs ≥ 74 letters). There was only one subgroup in the primary endpoint in which the confidence interval even crossed the -4.5 letters margin (age 65-75, 95% CI: -4.5, 1.4); however, as the subgroup contains only ~32% of the study participant and the margin is not crossed, this is not considered critical. In general, these findings support the robustness of the primary and secondary endpoint results and indicate that the treatment is broadly effective across different patient subgroups.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Supportive studies

As already outlined above, dose response was investigated in the Phase II dose-finding study GX28228 (Ladder), where 3 different doses of the PDS formulation (i.e. 10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL) have been evaluated in the PRN regimen compared to monthly IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg. In this setting, a dose response with regard to efficacy outcomes (visual acuity as well as anatomical changes) was observed across the PDS treatment arms, favouring the PDS 100 mg/ml formulation. Choosing this dose is overall considered adequate, based on the available efficacy data from study GX28228.

In the GX28228 study, a minimum of 2 prior anti-VEGF injections (including ranibizumab, aflibercept or bevacizumab) was required for inclusion in the study (mean of 2.9 injections), in contrast to the Phase III study, where at least 3 prior injections and one additional ranibizumab IVT injection at screening were required (mean of 5 injections) in order to increase the likelihood for patients to reach a vision plateau prior to randomization. Thus, patients in the Phase II study had most likely not yet reached their vision plateau.

For the PDS 100 mg/mL arm, the median time to first meeting the protocol-defined refill criteria was 15.8 months; 79.8% of patients went ≥ 6 months until the first refill and 68.7% went ≥ 9 months. Nevertheless, the Q24W frequency was selected for implant refill in order to minimize the risk of visual acuity deterioration during continuous drug delivery, according to the applicant. Given that each refillexchange procedure fully replaces the implant content with newly introduced ranibizumab, the risk of cumulative overdosing from previous and new ranibizumab administration may be considered negligible. The applicant provided supporting literature indicating that fixed refill intervals with continuous drug release contribute to stable clinical outcomes and reduce the risk of vision loss compared to treatment after observation of disease activity. While this is acknowledged, extending the treatment interval may confer the advantage of reducing the frequency of hospital visits, aligning with the key aims of PDS therapy. In this aspect it is noted that the applicant investigates also a longer refill-exchange interval, supported by data from the dose-finding study, in which 68.7% of patients did not require a refill to maintain VA until 9 months. The Phase IIIb study (WR42221, Velodrome) evaluated the PK, efficacy, and safety of the PDS with ranibizumab 100 mg/mL delivered every 36 weeks (Q36W) compared with Q24W in patients with nAMD. Data from this study are not included in this MAA. Extending the refill-exchange interval could further minimise the treatment burden; however, this is out of scope of this application and in this procedure the benefit/risk profile of the 24week regimen will be evaluated.

In the ongoing long-term extension study GR40549 (Portal), the primary objective was to evaluate the long-term safety and tolerability of ranibizumab 100 mg/mL delivered via the PDS administered Q24W to patients who elected to enrol in the extension study from studies GX28228 and GR40548.

While the study focuses on safety, it also explores sustained efficacy (CCOD: 31 January 2025). However, the interpretability of long-term efficacy is limited due to the single-arm design without a comparator.

Despite variations in treatment duration and frequency due to differences in parent study designs and patient populations, all groups received consistent long-term therapy, supporting the feasibility of sustained PDS use, with the longest mean treatment duration of 270 weeks, and a mean of 11.3 doses per patient among patients from the pivotal GR40548 study who received already 100 mg/mL ranibizumab via the PDS administered Q24W.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Generally, no significant differences were observed between patients previously treated with 100 mg/mL ranibizumab IVT injections Q4W, patients switched from the PDS - PRN regimen (10 mg/mL, 40 mg/mL, and 100 mg/mL), and those who already received 100 mg/mL ranibizumab via the PDS administered Q24W.

Across all treatment arms, patients experienced a modest, but gradual decline in VA, with a median loss of approx. one line on the ETDRS chart over several years (mean study treatment duration ranging from 173.87 - 270.41 weeks). Despite this decline over time, the overall vision loss remained moderate compared to the natural progression of nAMD, where significantly faster progression is reported.

The applicant outlines that data from long-term clinical trials and real-world observational studies have shown that BCVA declines over time in patients with neovascular AMD despite treatment with antiVEGF, and that this might be expected given the progressive and chronic nature of this disease, indicating that the gradual decrease in vision might be due to other factors beyond targeting VEGF. However, in the cited Keenan study, an average of 3 anti-VEGF injections per year were self-reported by patients, which is not comparable to the setting of the Portal LTE study, where patients were continuously supplied with ranibizumab via the Susvimo implant.

One limitation of the Portal LTE study is that there is no direct comparison of long-term visual outcomes between the Susvimo PDS Q4W regimen and sustained ranibizumab IVT Q4W therapy - a direct comparison is limited to up to 96 weeks.

A comparison between the BCVA outcomes from the Susvimo PDS patients in the Portal study and long-term historical data from patients continuously treated with ranibizumab IVT Q4W is difficult to establish, since in the large long-term Phase 3 trials for anti-VEGF agents, patients were treated with ranibizumab IVT Q4W for not longer than 2 years, followed by IVT treatment with ranibizumab per investigators discretion (MARINA/ANCHOR and HORIZON studies, CATT study, IVAN trial). Background for this switch from monthly ranibizumab administration to a less frequent, 'as needed' dosing was the intention to reduce the long-term burden of fixed, (too) frequent injections. Therefore, to the applicant´s knowledge, no historical long-term data from patients treated with ranibizumab IVT Q4W exist.

The applicant has provided a surrogate analysis instead, comparing long-term visual acuity outcomes of patients who were initially treated in the Archway study with PDS 100 mg/mL and then continued with this regimen in the Portal LTE study and of patients from the HORIZON, IVAN, CATT and SEVEN UP LTE studies.

This comparison of visual outcomes, with its implicit limitations as outlined above, suggests more favorable long-term outcomes for patients with PDS as compared to patients who were initially treated with intensive ranibizumab IVT followed by treatment per investigators discretion: there were higher percentages of patients with a vision of 20/40 or better (PDS: 56.5% at 5 years of follow-up, 50.95% at 6 years; CATT: 50%, IVAN: 35%, SEVEN-UP: 23%).

Overall, it is acknowledged that long-term visual outcomes in terms of BCVA change appear to be more favorable in patients with a constant ranibizumab release via PDS than in patients treated per physician discretion in the long-term.

The proportion of patients who did not require supplemental ranibizumab therapy after PDS implantation was high (86.4% from the parent GX28228 study and 77.7% from the parent GR40548 study), with no increase in supplemental treatment over time.

Patients who switched from the IVT treatment arm to PDS treatment demonstrated a strong preference for the PDS system at Week 40, primarily due to the reduced treatment frequency. As nAMD is a progressive disease, the patient experience at time points later than Week 40 would also be of interest; however, this was not pre-specified in the study protocol.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

23 patients underwent PDS explantation and reimplantation due to septum dislodgement and had follow-up data for BCVA at Week 44/48 post-reimplantation, showing no average decline in BCVA or meaningful change in CST compared to pre-reimplantation values. The implant is generally intended to remain in the eye permanently; however, current evidence did not indicate that reimplantation negatively impacts visual function.

## 5.3.6.2. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

In the pivotal efficacy study GR40548 (Archway) in nAMD patients responsive for anti-VEGF treatment, equivalence and non-inferiority have been demonstrated for the PDS 100 mg/mL arm (Q24W) compared to IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W with regard to the primary endpoint, the mean change from baseline in BCVA score averaged over Wk 36 and Wk 40, assessed in the Efficacy population ('as treated') with a pre-specified margin of ±4.5 letters. This was supported by sensitivity analyses in the PP population as well as by supplemental analyses for the primary endpoint. In addition, the key secondary endpoints, which had been requested by EMA, were met.

Furthermore, the results for the secondary efficacy visual and anatomical endpoints supported that PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W was similar to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W.

Notably, the number of treatments received by patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm was significantly lower compared to the ranibizumab IVT arm. Until Week 40, patients in the PDS arm had received a mean of 2.0 study treatments per patient compared with a mean of 10.7 per patient in the IVT arm (Week 48 data: 2.9 versus 12.6 study treatments). This meaningful reduction of injections/ treatment visits is considered to be clinically relevant for this patient population, since the requirement of frequent anti-VEGF IVT injections and follow-up visits in order to achieve and maintain improved visual acuity poses a burden on patients with nAMD.

The applicant proposes the following indication wording:

Susvimo is indicated in adult patients for the treatment of neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD) who have achieved stable disease after previously responding to intravitreal treatment with a vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) inhibitor medication.

This is acceptable, since treatment is restricted to patients in which efficacy of anti-VEGF medication has been demonstrated (i.e. anti-VEGF responders).

In the supportive dose-finding study GX28228 (Ladder), the PDS 100 mg/mL implant refilled PRN improved vision in patients with fewer prior anti-VEGF injections (mean of 2.9 injections) - clinical benefit in terms of visual acuity and anatomical changes was comparable to that in the IVT ranibizumab control arm. However, it remains not fully clear why the applicant chose the Q24W regimen for refill. Extending the refill-exchange interval could further minimise the treatment burden; however, this is out of scope of this application and in this procedure the benefit/risk profile of the 24week regimen will be evaluated.

In the supportive extension study GR40549 (Portal), the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W implant overall maintained visual acuity for up to 240 weeks both in patients who had been receiving long-term treatment with anti-VEGF injections as well as in patients who switched from the PDS implant refilled PRN. Across all treatment arms, patients experienced a modest, but gradual decline in VA, with a median loss of approx. one line on the ETDRS chart over several years (mean study treatment duration ranging from 173.87 - 270.41 weeks). Despite this decline over time, the overall vision loss remained moderate compared to the natural progression of nAMD.

Overall, Susvimo (100 mg/mL ranibizumab IVT delivered via the PDS every 6 months) provides noninferior efficacy to monthly ranibizumab IVT 0.5 mg injections while reducing the injection frequency.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4. Clinical safety

Please refer to the table of studies in section 6.3.2

For the purpose of this document, the following definitions apply:

'Adverse event - AE' means any untoward medical occurrence in a subject to whom a medicinal product is administered and which does not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment.

'Serious adverse event - SAE' means any untoward medical occurrence that at any dose requires inpatient hospitalisation or prolongation of existing hospitalisation, results in persistent or significant disability or incapacity, results in a congenital anomaly or birth defect, is life-threatening, or results in death. The definition (in line with ICH E2A) includes important medical events that may not be immediately life-threatening or result in death or hospitalisation but may jeopardise the patient or may require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes listed in the definition above.

'Adverse drug reaction - ADR' means any untoward and unintended response to a medicinal product related to any dose administered, for which, after a thorough assessment, a causal relationship between the medicinal product and the adverse event is at least a reasonable possibility, based for example, on their comparative incidence in clinical trials, or findings from epidemiological studies and/or on an evaluation of causality from individual case reports.

## 5.4.1. Safety data collection

The collection and analyses of safety data was based on the following three studies:

- the pivotal Phase III Study GR40548 (Archway; final CSR through week 96),
- the Phase II Study GX28228 (Ladder; includes main study as well as the oral antithrombotic substudy; final CSR through 38 months), and
- the Phase III open-label extension Study GR40549 (Portal).

One study of the original clinical programme (the pilot Phase I trial Study FH-1.2), using a prototype implant, has been excluded from the safety analysis.

As of the clinical cut-off date (CCOD) of 31 January 2025, there are approximately 2691 patient-years of safety data in the All PDS population. The current safety database for PDS in nAMD is 577 patients (with follow-up after implantation ranging from 1.1 weeks to 384.9 weeks [over 7 years] and up to 20.refill-exchange procedures).

The safety data collected across the nAMD studies included AEs and ocular assessments such as slit lamp examination, indirect ophthalmoscopy, and intraocular pressure.

## 5.4.2. Patient exposure

## Patient exposure to ranibizumab (IVT injection)

In the last 18 years, since the first market approval of Lucentis (ranibizumab IVT was registered in the US in June 2006 and in the EU in January 2007), the total exposure to ranibizumab has been estimated to be 3.7 million patient-years.

## Patient exposure to Susvimo (ranibizumab via PDS)

As of 31 January 2025, in the All PDS Population, there were 48.2% of patients with &gt; 5 years ( &gt; 260 weeks) of follow-up. The maximum duration of exposure from the time of implantation was 384.9 weeks (~7.4 years). The mean duration of exposure was 242.50 weeks (4.66 years [range: 1.1

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- 384.9 weeks]) with more than half of the All PDS Population (347/577 [60.1%]) having received at least 10 PDS study treatments per patient (including PDS initial fill plus refill-exchanges plus, and if applicable, PDS re-implant fill).

2 Conjunctival bleb/filtering bleb leak: includes conjunctival bleb, conjunctival filtering bleb leak, conjunctival cyst and implant site cyst.

4 Retinal detachment: includes retinal detachment, rhegmatogenous retinal detachment and tractional retinal detachment.

3 Cataract: includes cataract, cataract nuclear, cataract cortical, cataract subcapsular and cataract traumatic.

8 Device material opacification: reported as opacities of unknown aetiology within implant.

9 Septum dislodgement: a type of implant damage where the septum dislodges into the body of the implant. The incidence of septum dislodgement was calculated from implanted patients across Susvimo clinical trials.

Determination of adverse reactions (ARs) was based on the components of the study treatment (ranibizumab 100 mg/mL formulation [drug product], implant or device, implant surgical/medical procedures including initial fill and implantation, refill-exchange and implant removal, when needed). All treatment-emergent AEs were examined as potential ARs. The list of designated medical event (European Medicines Agency 2020) was used to focus on reports of suspected ARs that deserved special attention, irrespective of statistical criteria used to prioritize safety reviews.

The ocular and non-ocular AE outputs with the PDS arm and IVT arm from the Study GR40548 CSR were reviewed to determine Ars.

The following events were considered for adjudication as ARs:

- Ocular events in the study eye and non-ocular events with a greater incidence in the PDS arm compared to the IVT arm
- Identified risks for PDS
- Procedural events and adverse device effects (ADEs) (European Medicines Agency 1995, International Organization for Standardization 2011; U.S. Food and Drug Administration 2019)
- Expected events for vitreo-retinal surgeries given the surgical nature of the study.

Medical review and judgment were applied to each qualifying event. Factors, such as onset time, causality, severity, frequency, etc. were considered. Verbatim terms and case details were reviewed in order to exclude non-relevant AEs.

Several PTs in the ocular AEs outputs were grouped under the Eye Disorders SOC for targeted selection of relevant ocular AEs in the study eye. For the purpose of AR data presentation in the SCS in-text tables and the corresponding outputs, several similar PTs were grouped together to combine similar medical concepts.
