## Deaths

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## All PDS Population

As of 31 January 2025, 51 deaths (8.8%) were reported in the All PDS population. The most frequent causes of death were myocardial infarction (4 patients), respiratory failure (3 patients), acute respiratory failure (3 patients), cardiac arrest (3 patients) and cardiac failure congestive (3 patients). The cause of death was not reported in 6 patients. No deaths were considered related to the implant (including implant, implant or refill-exchange procedure, or initial fill needle), IVT injection procedure, or study drug by the investigator.

Ocular adverse events of special interest (AESIs) were closely monitored and collected. Ocular AESIs included endophthalmitis, device dislocation, vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival retraction, conjunctival erosion, and conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak, detachment, hyphema, and cataract. The AESI were chosen based on the known safety profile of IVT ranibizumab injections, as well as on the potential risks associated with the implantation/ refill procedure.

## 5.4.5. Discontinuation due to adverse events

As of 31 January 2025, 82 patients (14.2%) in the All PDS population discontinued from study treatment due to an AE (Table 54). The AEs leading to treatment discontinuation with highest incidence (&gt; 0.5%) by PT were device issue (i.e. septum dislodgement) (19 [3.3%]), nAMD (11 [1.9%]), endophthalmitis (8 [1.4%]), device dislocation (8 [1.4%]), conjunctival erosion (7 [1.2%]), conjunctival retraction (5 [0.9%]), and retinal haemorrhage (5 [0.9%]).

## 5.4.6. Safety in special populations

With the initial application in 2021, the applicant provided safety data for ocular adverse events in the 'All PDS 100 mg/mL Population' analysed by specific age groups (&lt;65, 65-74, and ≥75) and gender (female/male). Subgroup analyses by race/ethnicity were not performed because over 90% of subjects were white with non-Hispanic origin. No differences in the frequency of total ocular AE and serious ocular AE by subgroups have been found. The applicant states that the incidence of ocular AEs in the fellow eye, non-ocular AEs, number of deaths, AESIs in both study eye and fellow eye, and non-ocular AESIs was also comparable among different age groups, and among males and females.

No data have been collected on the safety of Susvimo in pregnancy and during lactation. Due to ranibizumab's mechanism of action, the applicant states that Susvimo must be regarded as potentially teratogenic and embryo-/fetotoxic. Susvimo may also be excreted in human milk.

## 5.4.7. Immunological events

The immunogenicity analysis population consisted of all treated patients with at least one ADA assessment. Patients were grouped according to treatment received. There were no major differences in the ocular or non-ocular adverse event (AE) profiles between ADA-positive patients in PDS 100 mg/mL arm and ADA-positive patients in IVT arm. Intraocular inflammation occurred with similar frequency in ADA positive and ADA negative patients.

## 5.4.8. Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No studies were performed that evaluated drug interactions with PDS.

## Bilateral Implantation

Following the market approval of the PDS in the US in October 2021, eleven patients in Study GR40549 received a bilateral (pre-septum improved) PDS implant in their fellow eye between January 2022 and October 2022, when the PDS implantation pause/voluntary recall was initiated (see Section

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

1.2.4.3). Since resumption of implantations in the commercial setting on 3 July 2024, an additional two patients have received a bilateral implant (with the post-septum improved implant). No AntiPlatelet Trialists Collaboration (APTC) events were reported after implantation in the fellow eye. The ocular AE and AESI profiles in the fellow eye post-implantation were consistent with the overall profile for the study eye. One serious ocular AE of conjunctival erosion was reported in the fellow eye after implantation and resolved after surgical repair (7 days after onset).

## 5.4.9. Vital signs and laboratory findings

General chemistry, haematology, and urinalysis laboratory results were collected as part of screening eligibility only. These data may be used for interpretation of some AEs, but no general summary was generated.

Vital signs were collected as part of screening eligibility, explant visit, and at study termination visit only. Therefore, vital signs results were reviewed by the Sponsor on an individual patient basis. No aggregate data summaries or shift tables are provided for vital signs. Clinically significant vital sign abnormalities were reported as AEs and evaluated in the adverse event assessments.

## 5.4.10. Post-marketing experience

PDS was approved for the treatment of nAMD in the United States on 22 October 2021. Through 12 June 2025, an estimated 560 patients had been implanted in the post-marketing setting (note that no implantations were performed in the commercial setting from 10 October 2023 through 3 July 2024 due to the voluntary recall).

Through 31 October 2023, the most frequently reported ocular AESI in the post-marketing setting was conjunctival erosion (n=5), followed by conjunctival bleb (n=4), retinal detachment (n=4), and vitreous haemorrhage (n=3). These events are also among the most frequently reported ocular AESIs in clinical trials. From 1 November 2023 through 30 April 2024, the AESIs reported were conjunctival erosion (2 cases) and endophthalmitis, conjunctival retraction and device dislocation (1 case each). Through 1 May 2024 through 30 April 2025, the AESIs reported were conjunctival erosion (3 events), conjunctival retraction (1 event), endophthalmitis (1 event), vitreous haemorrhage (1 event) and cataract (1 event).

Through 31 October 2023, serious ocular AESIs were reported in 10 patients; the most frequent was retinal detachment (n=4), followed by conjunctival erosion (n=3), and cataract, vitreous haemorrhage and hyphema (n=1). The 3 events of conjunctival erosion required surgical repair. One patient had the implant explanted due to recurrent conjunctival erosion. One conjunctival erosion was resolved, and one conjunctival erosion was ongoing as of the cut off. One patient with retinal detachment required vitrectomy.

From 1 November 2023 through 30 April 2024, 2 serious AESIs were reported, endophthalmitis, and device dislocation. In the follow-up report of endophthalmitis, it was determined that the event had resolved following antibiotic treatment with no loss of vision. No causality assessment was reported. In the device dislocation event, the implant dislocated into the vitreous cavity during a refill-exchange. The event was reported as a product complaint with no associated AE. The applicant assessed the complaint as a serious AE. Through 1 May 2024 through 30 April 2025, 2 serious AESIs were reported.

Through 30 April 2024, septum dislodgements (PT 'device issue') were reported in 2 patients, both of whom had been implanted with Phase III implants in the clinical trial setting. In one of these patients, septum dislodgement occurred while enrolled in Study GR40549. From 1 May 2024 through 30 April 2025, one case of valve in the implant was not working (PT 'device issue') has been reported in one patient. Limited information was provided for this event, and it cannot be confirmed that the septum dislodgment truly occurred in the post-marketing setting.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.11. Overall discussion and conclusions on clinical safety

## 5.4.11.1. Discussion

## 5.4.11.1.1. Overall assessment of available safety data

Susvimo is a drug-device combination for continuous delivery of ranibizumab via an implanted port delivery system (PDS). The safety profile of the active substance ranibizumab, if administered via IVT injection, is well established. The most frequently reported ocular ADRs following injection of Lucentis are: eye pain, ocular hyperaemia, increased intraocular pressure, vitritis, vitreous detachment, retinal haemorrhage, visual disturbance, vitreous floaters, conjunctival haemorrhage, eye irritation, foreign body sensation in eyes, increased lacrimation, blepharitis, dry eye and eye pruritus. Less frequently reported, but more serious adverse reactions include endophthalmitis, blindness, retinal detachment, retinal tear and iatrogenic traumatic cataract.

The safety analyses for Susvimo are based on the following three studies: the pivotal Phase III Study GR40548 (Archway; PDS 100 mg/mL, refill-exchanged Q24W vs. IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injections Q4W;), Phase II dose-finding Study GX28228 (Ladder; PDS 10 mg/mL, refill-exchanged PRN, PDS 40 mg/mL, refill-exchanged PRN, PDS 100 mg/mL, refill-exchanged PRN vs. IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg injections Q4W), and the ongoing Phase III open-label extension Study GR40549 (Portal; PDS 100 mg/mL, refill-exchanged Q24W).

Since study GR40549 (Portal) did not include an IVT ranibizumab arm, patients treated with IVT ranibizumab were not included in the pooled safety analyses.

Comparative safety data of the PDS vs. IVT ranibizumab are therefore presented for the phase III study GR40548 (Archway) only. These have been presented for specific periods (within 9 days of implant procedure, within 9 days of refill, within 9 days of injection; onset within 37, onset after 37 days, overall) to address the question, which of the procedures -PDS implantation, refill or IVT injections - were associated with specific AEs and ARs.

The limitations of the study design relevant for the interpretation of safety data include the open label design (applicable to all three studies) and the absence of a comparator beyond 96 weeks.

The applicant provided a pooled analysis ('all PDS'), which combines data from Ladder, Archway and Portal. The results of this pooled analysis were used as the basis for the AE frequency presented in the SmPC.

## Size of the safety database

The All PDS Population comprises a total of n = 577 patients with nAMD. Hereof, 469 patients received the target dose of PDS 100 mg/mL. 84.9% of these patients were followed up for &gt; 3 years and 48.2% of patients for &gt; 5 years (maximum of ~7.4 years). The mean duration of exposure was ~4.7 years with 60.1% of patients having received at least 10 study treatments.

Based on the requirements for drug exposure as specified in ICH E1, the size of the safety database is sufficient. The number of patients treated for at least 1.5 years and the average number of treatment cycles received allows for a meaningful assessment of the long-term safety of Susvimo.

## Demographics

Baseline demographics and ocular characteristics were generally well balanced across treatment groups. Consistent with the epidemiologic characteristics of patients with nAMD, the mean age of the patients at randomization was 75.0 years, 56% of the patients were aged ≥ 75 years, and the majority were female (59%). The demographic characteristics for Study GR40548 are in line with previous pivotal trials for IVT ranibizumab injection (e.g., a majority of women, predominantly white, with mean ages between 76 and 79 years) and is therefore considered representative for the group of patients to be treated in a real-world setting.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Adverse events

Based on data from study GR40548 (Archway), the safety of PSD ranibizumab is worse than the safety of IVT ranibizumab. A higher number of patients experienced at least 1 AEs (any AE) with PDS compared to IVT ranibizumab [247 (99.6%) vs 142 (85.0%)].

## Ocular Safety in Study Eye

The overall number of ocular AEs in the study eye was significantly higher across all PDS arms compared to the IVT control group. Based on results from study GR40548, the overall number of ocular AEs in the study eye was approximately twice as high in the PDS 100 mg Arm compared to the IVT Arm (97.6% vs. 52.1%, respectively).

The large difference in AEs between treatments was primarily driven by AEs which occurred in the post-operative period (≤37 days of initial implantation) (91.9% vs 10.8% in the PDS and IVT arm, respectively). In the follow-up period (&gt;37 days after the surgery), the difference between treatments w/r/t AEs in the study eye decreased; nonetheless, the safety in the PDS arm remained somewhat worse (60.5% vs 49.1%).

Therefore, the major difference in AEs incidence between treatments can largely be attributed to the initial surgical implantation, while refill-exchange procedures were associated with much less AEs. Refill-exchange procedures were associated with less AEs than IVT injections. This appears plausible since the IVT injection technique requires a deeper and therefore more traumatic penetration of the eye.

In the PDS 100 mg/mL arm, the ocular AEs in study eye with the highest incidence (≥15 patients) by PT were conjunctival haemorrhage (72.2%), conjunctival hyperaemia (27.0%), iritis (21.0%), eye pain (11.3%), vitreous floaters (10.1%), punctate keratitis (8.1%), foreign body sensation in eyes (7.7%), vitreous detachment (6.9%), conjunctival bleb (6.9%), hypotony of eye (6.5%), cataract (6.0%), and vitreous haemorrhage (6.0%). In the IVT arm, the ocular AE in study eye with the highest incidence by PT was conjunctival haemorrhage (13.8%).

The majority of events in the PDS arm had an onset within the first 9 days after implantation, while events were almost equally distributed over time in the IVT arm. From Week 40 through CCOD, the incidence of ocular AEs in the study eye were similar in the PDS 100 mg arm and the IVT arm.

The greatest imbalance was found for conjunctival haemorrhage, conjunctival hyperaemia, and iritis, which occurred with much higher frequency in the PDS arm than in the IVT arm. Nearly all of these cases occurred in close timely association with the PDS implantation procedure and prior to the first refill-exchange procedure.

The same picture was found for study GX28228 (Ladder). In the first 10 months, ocular AEs in the study eye were found more than twice as often in the PDS arms compared with the IVT arm. The incidence of ocular AEs was similar across all PDS arms, irrespective of the dose administered. This also contributes to the assumption that the imbalance in ocular AEs between the PDS arms and the IVT arm was mainly driven by the implantation and refill procedure itself, rather than by ranibizumab exposure. Analyses of the number of AEs caused by implant during the first 10 months revealed that approximately 88 % of patients experienced at least one adverse event caused by implant. After the postoperative period (up to day 37 after implant insertion), the rates of ocular AEs were similar in the PDS arms and IVT arms. While the number of AEs considered caused by implant were in the range of 80-95% across PDS arms, the number of AEs considered caused by refill was between 5%-12% (which is in the range of the IVT Arm).

Similar to study GR40548, in study GX28228 the greatest incidence was found for conjunctival haemorrhage, followed by conjunctival hyperaemia, eye pain, vitreous floaters, iritis, eye irritation, foreign body sensation in eyes, and vitreous haemorrhage.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Most ocular AEs in the PDS arm and IVT arm in the study eye were mild in intensity. More patients in the PDS arm experienced AEs of moderate or severe intensity compared to the IVT arm (27.0% vs 14.4% and 6.9% vs 1.8%, respectively).

## Ocular AESI

The AESI were chosen based on the known safety profile of IVT ranibizumab injections, as well as on the potential risks associated with the implantation/ refill procedure. Ocular AESIs included endophthalmitis, device dislocation, vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival retraction, conjunctival erosion, and conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak, detachment, hyphema, and cataract.

A total of 237 patients (41.1%) in the All PDS population experienced at least one ocular AESI in the study eye, hereof 63 patients (10.9%) during the post-operative period. After the post-operative period, there was no notable trend in the overall number of ocular AESIs by refill-period. Likewise, by individual AESIs, there were no notable trends after the post-operative period.

The incidence of the AESI was higher in patients who received the PDS implant compared to IVT injections and appears to be causally related to the device implantation and refill procedures.

Of note: the incidence rates of AESI differ from the rates of the same ocular AE, as for AESI similar PTs were grouped together.

The ocular AESIs in the study eye with the highest incidence (≥2 patients) were cataract (including cata nuclear, cataract cortical, and cataract subcapsular; 8.9% vs. 6.0%), conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak (6.9%) vs. 0), conjunctival erosion (4.0% vs. 0), conjunctival retraction (2.4% vs.0), device dislocation (1.6% vs. 0), and vitreous haemorrhage (6.0% vs. 3.6%). PDS was also associated with a higher rate of serious AESI. All serious events except for one event of endophthalmitis in the PDS arm recovered and patients largely recovered vision.

A higher rate of some AESI was reported in the pooled analysis compared to the Archway study: cataract (18.5%), conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak (14.7%), conjunctival erosion (7.8%) and vitreous haemorrhage (7.5%).

As of 31 January 2025, 16 patients (2.8%) in the All PDS population (pooled analysis) experienced a total of 18 endophthalmitis events in the study eye. Most of these events were severe (12/16 [75.0%]) and associated with a conjunctival erosion or retraction event (11/18), most of which needed surgical repair. All except 1 event were considered serious and 7/18 events were sight threatening. Half of the patients who experienced endophthalmitis discontinued treatment (8/16, 50%) with explantation subsequently performed in 6 of them). Refill-exchange was delayed in 3 cases. Most patients made a full recovery except one patient, who had not recovered at the time of CCOD and 2 others who were reported to have recovered with sequelae.

To ensure the correct initial scleral incision length, the implant procedure IFU was updated (update issued 3 June 2020) to more tightly control the scleral incision length during the implant insertion procedure. The provided data (457 patients in the pre-optimization group vs. 908 patients in the post-optimization group) suggest lower endophthalmitis rate trends with the new IFU compared to the old IFU. PDS was associated with a higher rate of sight threatening events compared to IVT (12/248 [4.8%]) vs 4/167 [2.4%], respectively).

The rate of IOP increase≥ 30 mmHg was comparable between treatment arms in Archway. The majority of IOP increased AEs recovered/resolved, with half of AEs recovering/resolving without any treatment, and the other half of the AEs recovering/resolving with standard IOP lowering medications.

PDS was associated with markedly higher rate of intraocular inflammation (IOI) compared to IVT (29.0% vs 0.6%). Most patients in the PDS arm had anterior uveitis, mostly iritis (52/248 [21.0%]; there were no cases of posterior uveitis. The incidence of uveitis was markedly higher with PDS compared to IVT (21% vs 0.6%, respectively). The incidence of vitritis in the PDS arm was 2.4%, whereas in the IVT arm no cases of vitritis were reported. Most of the intraocular inflammation events in the PDS arm were associated with the PDS implantation surgery.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Ocular Serious Adverse Events

The overall number of ocular SAEs in the study eye is increased across all PDS arms. 141 patients (24.4%) in the All PDS population experienced at least one ocular SAE in the study eye (as of 31 Jan 2025). Most events had an onset before week 40 in the PDS arms.

The highest incidence was found for septum dislodgement (9.5%), conjunctival erosion (4.5%), endophthalmitis (2.6%), device dislocation (1.9%), conjunctival retraction (1.7%), conjunctival bleb (1.4%), rhegmatogenous retinal detachment (0.7%), visual acuity reduced (0.7%), and vitreous haemorrhage (0.7), hypotony of eye (0.5%), cataract (0.5%), conjunctival filtering bleb leak (0.5%), and retinal haemorrhage (0.5%). Most cases occurred before week 40 and might, hence, be attributed to the PDS implantation or to the first refill-exchange procedure.

## APTC Events

As of 31 January 2025, 73 patients (12.7%) in the All PDS Population had experienced at least 1 APTC event. Twenty-six patients (4.5%) were in the APTC category of vascular death or death of unknown cause (21 patients and 5 patients, respectively). The non-fatal events reported were stroke (37 patients [6.4%]) and myocardial infarction (22 [3.8%]). None of these events were reported as related to study drug or procedure. However, in study GR40548 APTC events occurred twice as often in the PDS 100 mg arm (4.8%) compared to the IVT arm (2.4%). An increased rate of APTC events was also found in study GX28228. The reason for this imbalance is not clear yet, since the systemic ranibizumab exposure is not substantially increased with the PDS. A theoretical risk of systemic adverse events exists for anti-VEGF drugs administered by IVT route, as stated in the SmPC of other IVT anti-VEGF medicinal products. A warning has been included in section 4.8, and section 4.4 has been complemented with observed incidence rates (see next section).

Based on these data, an increased risk for APTC events cannot be ruled out and needs further evaluation.

## Deaths

As of 31 January 2025, 51 deaths (8.8%) were reported in the All PDS population. The most frequent causes of death were myocardial infarction (4 patients), respiratory failure (3 patients), acute respiratory failure (3 patients), cardiac arrest (3 patients) and cardiac failure congestive (3 patients). The cause of death was not reported in 6 patients. No deaths were considered related to the implant (including implant, implant or refill-exchange procedure, or initial fill needle), IVT injection procedure, or study drug by the investigator. No increased risk for death was found in study GR40548 and study GX28228. Based on these data, there is currently no safety signal for an increased mortality rate.

## Laboratory findings and Vital signs

General chemistry, haematology, and urinalysis laboratory results were collected as part of screening eligibility only. No general summary was generated. Vital signs were collected as part of screening eligibility, explant visit, and at study termination visit only. Therefore, vital signs results were reviewed by the Sponsor on an individual patient basis. No aggregate data summaries or shift tables are provided for vital signs.

The systemic exposure of ranibizumab after IVT administration is extremely low and no direct effects on laboratory parameters or on vital signs are expected based on current knowledge on ranibizumab's PK/PD and adverse effect profile. The lack of systematic laboratory evaluation and regular measurement of vital signs is therefore acceptable. However, the latter might at least be temporarily affected by the procedure of PD implantation itself and any short statement on this issue would have been useful.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Safety in special populations

With the initial application in 2021, the Applicant provided safety data for ocular adverse events in the 'All PDS 100 mg/mL population' analysed by specific age groups (&lt;65, 65-74, and ≥75) and gender (female/male). Subgroup analyses by race/ethnicity were not performed because over 90% of subjects were white with non-Hispanic origin. No differences in the frequency of total ocular AE and serious ocular AE by subgroups have been found. The applicant states that the incidence of ocular AEs in the fellow eye, non-ocular AEs, number of deaths, AESIs in both study eye and fellow eye, and non-ocular AESIs was also comparable among different age groups, and among males and females.

No data have been collected on the safety of Susvimo in pregnancy and during lactation. Due to ranibizumab's mechanism of action, the applicant states that Susvimo must be regarded as potentially teratogenic and embryo-/fetotoxic. Susvimo may also be excreted in human milk. Respective wording has therefore been included into section 4.6, including a statement that the implant may be flushed with saline using a refill needle should the patient become pregnant. This is agreed. The wording regarding pregnancy and breastfeeding in section 4.6 in the SmPC is largely aligned with the approved wording for Lucentis. This is agreed.

## Immunological events

The immunogenicity analysis population consisted of all treated patients with at least one ADA assessment. Patients were grouped according to treatment received. There were no major differences in the ocular or non-ocular adverse event (AE) profiles between ADA-positive patients in PDS 100 mg/mL arm and ADA-positive patients in IVT arm. (13.4% vs 9.1%, respectively). In patients with treatment-induced ADA, ADA positivity was persistent in a similar percentage of patients across arms (around 70%). PDS induced ADAs earlier compared to the IVT (median time to ADA detection was 23.18 weeks in the PDS arm and 35.99 weeks in the IVT arm).

The incidence of treatment-emergent NAbs was also higher in PDS arm compared to IVT arm (6.1% vs 2.4%).

Please refer to the pharmacology section for the influence of ADA/nAb on PK and efficacy.

Intraocular inflammation occurred with similar frequency in ADA positive and ADA negative patients. Intraocular inflammation was therefore most likely related to the surgical and treatment procedure itself rather than to an immunogenic reaction. This assumption is also supported by the consistently and significantly higher number of intraocular inflammations in the PDS Arm compared to the IVT Arm, irrespective of ADA status.

It seems that the occurrence of ADAs (including NAbs) to ranibizumab does not result in any clinically meaningful consequences with respect to safety.

## Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No drug-drug interaction studies were needed due to extremely low systemic exposure after implantation.

## Discontinuations

In the All PDS Population, 35 patients discontinued study treatment in the parent studies. Of the 539 patients who received study treatment in Study GR40549, 174 discontinued study treatment early. The most frequently reported reasons for treatment discontinuation were AE (58 patients), withdrawal by subject (45 patients), and death (38 patients).

Based on the data for 58 patients, available for a follow up of 80 weeks (median), the safety profile in patients who discontinued the treatment with PDS implant but remained in the study appears similar to that in the All PDS population, while some AE were reported with a higher frequency compared to the all PDS population, e.g retinal haemorrhage (reported with frequency very common), and punctate keratitis (reported with the frequency common). These AEs are however in line with the safety profile of IVT ranibizumab, which was used in the study as supplemental treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Explantations and re-implantations

As of 31 January 2025, 68 patients had been explanted. Explantations were generally performed because of an AE. Of the 68 patients who were explanted, 34 were re-implanted (28 following a septum dislodgement, 6 for other reasons). 3/34 had their re-implanted device removed, while 31 patients continued on study treatment.

Of the 68 patients who were explanted, 34 patients were not re-implanted. Of explanted patients with no reimplantation (N=34), 26 patients were explanted due to an AE. The majority of these AEs resolved.

## Septum dislodgment

Septum dislodgement (including both full septum dislodgement and partial septum dislodgement) is a type of device deficiency where the septum has dislodged into the body of the implant.

In the All PDS Population (nAMD), 57 patients (9.9%) reported septum dislodgement.

Due to an increasing number of septum dislodgements observed in the PDS clinical program a PDS implantation, pause/ recall and a detailed investigation into this device deficiency were initiated. A root cause was identified and risk mitigation measures were put in place, with updates to the implant and refill needle. PDS implantations with an improved implant (post-septum improved implant) were resumed in February 2024.

The applicant has conducted extensive testing to evaluate the performance of the implant over its use life in accordance with the General Safety and Performance Requirements (GSPRs), which was reviewed as part of the conformity assessment performed by the Notified Body TUV SUD Product Service GmbH. Design verification testing confirmed implant septum performance over the use life of the device. Drug product quality data from multi-puncture compatibility studies confirm that repeated exposure to siliconized RFN does not adversely impact drug product quality.

At present, satisfactory performance at the quality level is acceptable. Nonetheless, it is expected that the absence or low frequency of septum dislodgment with the updated version of the device is also supported by clinical data. Currently clinical evidence is limited and does not allow definitive conclusions to be drawn. The applicant plans to closely monitor patients in the ongoing study GR40549 (Portal) who will receive the updated version of the device and are undergoing refillexchanges with the updated refill-needle and share this data once available. It is expected that data will be available from 300 patients who are followed for up to 216 weeks post-implantation and up to eight refill-exchanges. As septum dislodgment cases with the previous device mostly occurred only after multiple refill-exchange procedures (from week 144 onwards), the proposed follow-up of 216 weeks is considered sufficiently long to allow comparison between the older and the newer version of the device.

## Bilateral implantation

In total only 13 patients have received bilateral treatment. Bilateral treatment could cause bilateral ocular adverse reactions and/or potentially lead to an increase in systemic exposure, which could increase the risk of systemic adverse reactions. Although no APTC events were reported after implantation in the fellow eye and the ocular AE and AESI profiles in the fellow eye post-implantation were consistent with the overall profile for the study eye, this is based on very limited data.

## Postmarketing data

Only few post-marketing data are available to date. Currently, post-marketing data reveal no new or increased risks but rather resemble the same AEs and SAES as were found in clinical trials.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 5.4.11.1.2. Adverse drug reactions (ADRs) in the SmPC

The ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion in the SmPC are described in section 5.4.3.1. above (Table 55).

There are no objections against the ADRs proposed by the applicant for inclusion into the SmPC.

## 5.4.11.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Susvimo is an innovative drug-device combination for continuous delivery of a customized formulation of ranibizumab via an implanted port delivery system (PDS). Due to procedural risks of the surgical implantation, the invasiveness of the 6-monthly refill-exchange procedures, and the mechanism of release with potential differences in drug-exposure, the safety profile differs from the safety profile of conventional IVT ranibizumab injections.

In order to better understand the underlying cause of the differences in the safety profile, the Applicant defined and evaluated different safety populations: a pooled 'All PDS Population', a population receiving lower dosing regimens (10mg/mL/ 40 mg/mL), a population representing the target dosing regimen of 100 mg/mL, and the comparator population, consisting of patients treated with IVT injections of authorized ranibizumab. The applicant also analysed the timely association of AEs with the implantation, and with the initial fill and refill procedures, to better discriminate between AEs due to PDS-related surgery and AEs associated with the refill procedure.

Safety analyses revealed that the overall number of ocular AEs, ocular SAEs and ocular AESI was substantially higher in the PDS arms compared with the IVT arm.

In study GR40548, the overall number of ocular AEs in the study eye was almost twice as high in the PDS 100 mg arm compared to the IVT arm. The imbalance of events was found within the first 37 days after implantation of the PDS with 93% of patients in the PDS 100 mg arm having at least 1 AE within 37 days, and only 23.4% of patients in the IVT arm having at least 1 AE. Importantly, after that initial 37-day period, the number of AEs in patients in the PDS 100mg/mL arm was only slightly increased (74.6%) compared with the number of AEs in patients in the IVT arm (63.5%). From Week 40 through CCOD, the incidence of ocular AEs in the study eye were similar in the PDS 100 mg arm and the IVT arm.

The majority of ocular AEs in both arms were mild in intensity, however more patients in the PDS arm experienced AE of moderate or severe intensity compared to IVT arm.

The rate of ocular SAEs overall was low (8.9%), though higher compared to IVT ranibizumab (2.4%). In the PDS arm, the severe ocular AEs in study eye with the highest incidence were device dislocation, conjunctival haemorrhage, conjunctival retraction, endophthalmitis, and rhegmatogenous retinal detachment. The majority of ocular AE resolved, though some needed prolonged treatment or even surgery.

The greatest imbalance was found within the first 9 days after implantation. 84.7% of the All PDS population had at least 1 AE that was classified as 'eye disorder' within the first 9 days after implantation. The AEs that occurred most commonly within that period were: conjunctival haemorrhage (66.7%), conjunctival hyperaemia (18.7%), iritis (16.5%), eye pain (12.1%), and foreign body sensation (6.4%). Injury, poisoning and procedural complications were reported in 59% of the ALL PDS population with procedural pain being most frequently (1.7%) reported. The same picture was found for study GX28228.

It can therefore be concluded that the implantation procedure itself led to the imbalance in the safety profile between the PDS arms and the IVT arms. This imbalance was very profound within a short time window (9 days) after the implantation procedure, but in the following, the number of AEs caused by refill was only slightly increased or not increased compared to the number of AEs seen with the conventional IVT injection of ranibizumab.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The incidence of ocular AEs was similar across all PDS Arms, irrespective of the dose administered. This also contributes to the assumption that the imbalance in ocular AEs between the PDS arms and the IVT arm was driven by the implantation and initial fill procedure rather than by an overexposure to ranibizumab or due to the sustained release pattern. Analyses of the number of AEs caused by the implant during the first 10 months revealed that approximately 88 % of patients experienced at least one adverse event caused by implant.

Importantly, APTC events (including myocardial infarction, stroke, TIA, vascular death, or arterial thromboembolic events) were rare but 2-fold higher among PDS compared to IVT as of 31 January 2025. %). The reason for this imbalance is not clear yet, since the systemic ranibizumab exposure does not appear to be substantially increased with the PDS. Ranibizumab levels achieved with the PDS are within the Cmin -Cmax achieved with IVT injections and remain below the levels required to inhibit the systemic biological activity of VEGF. A theoretical risk of systemic adverse events exists for anti-VEGF drugs administered by IVT route, and has been included in the SmPC, however, these events need to be further monitored.

It can therefore be concluded that the safety profile of Susvimo within the first 9 days after implantation is worse compared to IVT ranibizumab injections. However, this initial disadvantage seems to diminish over time. With longer treatment periods, the safety profile appears quite similar to the known safety profile of intravitreally administered ranibizumab and has to be put into perspective with the significant advantage of less frequent re-treatment.

To date, some uncertainties with regard to long-term safety remain. This also includes the question, whether the implant should remain in the eye or be explanted when Susvimo treatment will be permanently stopped. Respective analyses on these questions need to be conducted in the context of the proposed post-authorization studies.

The safety profile of the PDS is adequately described in the PI. All issues have been resolved, and the product is approvable from the safety perspective.

## 6.1. Safety specification

## 6.1.1. Proposed safety specification

## 6.1.2. Discussion on proposed safety specification

The information of the RMP aligns well with the non-clinical and clinical safety findings.

Findings from non-clinical studies, specifically with regard to repeat-dose toxicity, reproductive/ developmental toxicity, genotoxicity, carcinogenicity, and vitreous haemorrhage are summarized and the relevance of data to human use is sufficiently discussed.

Data from the three clinical studies in the PDS clinical development program: GX28228 (Ladder), GR40548 (Archway), and GR40549 (Portal) are well summarized and thoroughly discussed against the background of their clinical relevance. Limitations regarding the detection of ARs in the clinical trial development program, either due to the rarity of the reaction, having a long latency, or only being developed after prolonged/cumulative exposure have been taken into account.

A sound justification for including or not including an identified or potential risk in the list of safety concerns in the RMP has been provided. Missing information, specifically regarding long-term safety and to implant removal-related risks and reimplantation-related risks has been taken into consideration. The Applicant has updated 'Long-term safety in post-marketing setting' to 'long term safety' as requested. Moreover, the Applicant added 'Reimplantation-related risks' alongside as well as 'Post-surgical intraocular inflammation'. This approach is endorsed.

Upon the CHMP's request, the applicant has updated the RMP to replace the important identified risk Post-surgical intraocular inflammation by the overarching term Intraocular inflammation. The list of important identified risks and important potential risks is based on the totality of safety data from Studies GX28228, GR40548, and GR40549 and is considered well justified and complete.

All issues concerning the safety specification are considered resolved.

## 6.2. Pharmacovigilance plan

The applicant proposes routine and additional pharmacovigilance activities to monitor the safety concerns.

## 6.2.1. Proposed pharmacovigilance plan

The proposed routine pharmacovigilance activity beyond adverse reactions reporting and signal detection is a specific follow-up questionnaire related to following safety concerns:

- PDS implant and PDS procedures complications (vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb/bleb leak, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, endophthalmitis, retinal tear and retinal detachment, device dislocation, and traumatic cataract)
- Septum dislodgement
- Intraocular inflammation
- Implant damage during the PDS procedures (other than septum dislodgement)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The applicant describes the purpose of the follow-up questionnaire as to collect key information from post-marketing case reports on the following aspects:

- Duration of device implantation
- Details about refills and any additional surgical corrections performed
- Additional clinical information on reported events.

The applicant describes further that while the follow-up questionnaire represents the complete form, only the sections relevant to the specific reported event, such as endophthalmitis, will be sent to the reporter. The applicant explains that this targeted approach is intended to ensure efficient and accurate data collection while minimizing the burden on healthcare professionals.

In addition, the applicant has proposed the following additional pharmacovigilance activities.

## 6.2.1.1.1. Study GR40549 Portal

A long-term extension study (GR40549; Portal) where patients with nAMD from the Phase II and Phase III parent studies are being followed up for additional safety data collection (see Table 58). The duration of this study will allow for collection, in Study GR40549, of a total of 240 weeks of follow-up safety data for patients from Studies GX28228 (Ladder) and GR40548 (Archway), and 144 weeks for patients from Study WR42221 (Velodrome), starting from the date of enrolment in GR40549 study.

Table 58: Study GR40549 (Portal)

## Study/activity short name and title:

Study GR40549 (Portal): A Multicentre, Open-Label Extension Study to Evaluate the Long-Term Safety and Tolerability of the PDS in Patients with nAMD

## Rationale and study objectives:

The objective of this study is to evaluate the long-term safety and tolerability of the PDS (100 mg/mL) in patients with nAMD, including further characterizing:

- The risk of PDS implant and PDS procedures complications (vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb/bleb leak, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, endophthalmitis, retinal tear and retinal detachment, device dislocation, and traumatic cataract)
- The risk of septum dislodgement
- The risk of intraocular inflammation
- The risk of implant damage during the PDS procedures (other than septum dislodgement)
- The missing information on implant removal-related risks
- The missing information on reimplantation-related risks

The missing information on long term safety

## Study design:

This is a global, multicentre, open-label, visual assessor-masked, multiple-cohort extension study designed to evaluate the long-term safety and tolerability of ranibizumab 100 mg/mL delivered via the PDS, with refill-exchanges administered Q24W or Q36W to patients who elect to enrol in the extension study from the parent studies.

## Study populations:

Patients with nAMD who have either completed Phase II Study GX28228 (Ladder), Phase III Study GR40548 (Archway), Phase IIIb Study WR42221 (Velodrome), or completed Week 24 visit in Study WR42221 but were not eligible to be randomized.

Patients rolled over from Study GX28228 (Ladder) and Study GR40548 (Archway) are followed up to allow the collection of 240 weeks of safety data.

## Milestones:

Clinical Study Report: Q4 2030

LPLV = last patient last visit; nAMD = neovascular age-related macular degeneration; PDS = Port Delivery System with ranibizumab.

A re-implantation substudy in association with study GR40549 (Portal) to evaluate the safety of reimplantation of the PDS implant and the refill-exchange procedures following reimplantation in patients who experienced septum dislodgement while in the main study or after having exited the main study.

This substudy will evaluate the safety of the reimplantation of PDS implant and the refill-exchange procedures following reimplantation in patients, previously enrolled in the main study, who experienced septum dislodgement while in the main study or after having exited the main study.

This substudy is a non-randomized, uncontrolled, open-label study to evaluate the safety of reimplanting the new updated PDS implant and the refill-exchange procedures following reimplantation in patients who experienced septum dislodgement while in the main study or after having exited the main study.

## 6.2.1.1.3. Study GR46011

A non-interventional, secondary data use study (Study GR46011) to evaluate the safety of Susvimo (ranibizumab administered via the PDS implant) compared to other approved IVT medications in nAMD patients treated in routine clinical practice.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Table 60: Study GR46011(SightARC)

## Study/activity short name and title:

Study GR46011(SightARC): A Non-Interventional, Secondary Data Use Study to Evaluate the Safety of Susvimo (Ranibizumab Administered via the PDS Implant) in nAMD Patients Treated in Routine Clinical Practice

## Rationale and study objectives:

This retrospective, non-interventional study will utilize secondary data from one or more existing data sources to estimate the incidence of ocular safety outcomes and PDS-specific surgical and procedural complications associated with Susvimo in routine clinical practice. The study aims to further characterize safety outcomes of Susvimo, including endophthalmitis, retinal tear and retinal detachment, and will use patients treated with other approved IVT medications as a contextualizing reference cohort for these outcomes. Additionally, it aims to evaluate PDS-specific surgical and procedural complications (including vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb complications, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, and device dislocation) of patients receiving Susvimo, using both direct diagnostic codes and indirect procedural/medication proxy codes.

## Study design:

A retrospective, non-interventional study of patients treated for nAMD in clinical practice using secondary data from one or more existing data sources. A comprehensive Feasibility Assessment will be conducted following marketing authorization approval in the EU of data sources to evaluate the ability to identify exposure and relevant outcomes. Patients treated with standard IVT anti-VEGF therapies will serve as a contextualizing reference cohort for ocular safety outcomes (endophthalmitis, retinal tear and retinal detachment). Surgical and procedural complications associated with the PDS implant and procedure (including vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb complications, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, and device dislocation) will be characterized solely within the Susvimo treated cohort as determined by the feasibility assessment.

## Study populations:

Patients treated for nAMD

## Milestones:

First Annual Monitoring Report: Q4 2027

Interim Study Report: Within 12 months of first and intermediate extractions*

Final Report: Q4 2033 Within 12 months of last extraction*

nAMD = neovascular age-related macular degeneration; PDS = Port Delivery System with ranibizumab.

*First, intermediate, and last data extractions will occur within 6 months, 30 months, and 54 months of analysis trigger, respectively.

## 6.2.2. Discussion on the pharmacovigilance plan

## 6.2.2.1. Routine pharmacovigilance activities

The applicant states that the purpose of this follow-up questionnaire is to collect key information from post-marketing case reports on the duration of device implantation, on details about refills and any additional surgical corrections performed and any additional clinical information on reported events. The collected information is related to the safety concerns 'PDS implant and PDS procedures complications (vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb/bleb leak, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, endophthalmitis, retinal tear and retinal detachment, device dislocation, and traumatic cataract), Septum dislodgement, Intraocular inflammation and Implant damage during the PDS procedures (other than septum dislodgement).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In other words, the applicant wants to ensure the adequate follow-up of post-marketing case reports and the robust collection of all the appropriate information deemed necessary to further characterise the above listed safety concerns associated with the PDS.

The applicant intends to send only the sections relevant to the specific reported event, such as endophthalmitis, to the reporter and therefore minimize the burden on healthcare professionals. In view of the length of the complete form, this sounds sensible.

Not all sections of the questionnaire are intended to be completed for every single report, i.e., only the data fields relevant to the reported AE are required to be filled in, and the two last sections are only applicable to patients/participants who were previously enrolled in PDS clinical trials. Furthermore, only drug therapy details or suspected drugs associated with adverse event and relevant medical /ocular history are covered, i.e., not the complete past medical and drug histories.

Although there remains a risk that the HCP is put off by the length of the overall questionnaire, taking above into account, it is overall considered acceptable in its current form. As requested, the Applicant has added a question to the follow-up questionnaire in order to collect information on whether the adverse event was associated with medication errors related to device/procedure.

## 6.2.2.2. Additional pharmacovigilance activities

Three post-authorization safety studies are proposed by the applicant:

The ongoing extension study (GR40549 Portal) is a multicentre, open-label extension study designed to evaluate the long-term safety and tolerability of the Port Delivery System with ranibizumab (PDS).

It will include approximately 300 patients who receive the updated implant at the time of their initial implantation surgery and mainly targets surgical complications. The aims are considered suitable for a long-term extension study, and the study is overall agreed as a relevant component in the pharmacovigilance plan. One important aim for this study is to verify a reduced incidence of septum dislodgement with the new modified device.

With the previous version of the device septum dislodgments appear to have occurred only after multiple refill-exchange procedures. An increase was observed starting from week 144. In the current application, data for the new implant have been presented until week 48 and are currently based on low numbers.

However, they will receive up to 216 weeks (&gt;4 years) of follow-up post-implantation procedures (up to 8 refills) and these will procure comprehensive data on safety endpoints, including incidence and severity of ADEs, ASADEs, and device deficiencies (including septum dislodgement). This is considered appropriate.

To evaluate the effectiveness of the implemented risk minimisation measures, the Applicant plans to monitor and analyse the incidence rates of the specific safety concerns identified in the EU Risk Management Plan (RMP) within the Portal (GR40549) study population. This detailed information will be communicated via PBRER. The proposed strategy for evaluation of effectiveness of risk minimization based on study GR40549 (Portal) is appropriate. The specific details regarding study GR46011 are pending the feasibility assessment and the ultimate study design.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A substudy GR46011 in association with the extension study GR40549 (Portal) will focus on the safety concerns related to re-implantation of the PDS device. This is accepted as a relevant focus for collection of further safety data.

Study GR46011 (SightARC) is a non-interventional, secondary data use study to evaluate the safety of Susvimo (ranibizumab administered via the PDS Implant) in nAMD patients treated in clinical practice.

Such a non-interventional study conducted with secondary data from existing data sources and including relevant comparator groups for contextualization can be relevant to provide further characterization of safety concerns. The strategy to use secondary data from existing data sources for further characterization of the (long-term) safety profile is supported.

All design aspects currently outlined by the Applicant as a basis for a feasibility assessment are fully agreed. The Applicant's request for a 6-month timeline for the submission of the final feasibility report has been adequately justified and is acceptable.

The applicant explains that at this stage, the specific methodology for utilizing Study GR46011 to evaluate the effectiveness of risk minimisation measures is contingent upon the upcoming feasibility assessment of the potential secondary data sources. The applicant further states that because the ability to accurately capture the impact of risk minimisation measures depends entirely on the granularity and quality of the data recorded in these routine clinical databases, the evaluation of effectiveness of risk minimisation measures for this study will be further detailed as the feasibility assessment is progressing. This is considered acceptable.

## 6.3. Plans for post-authorisation efficacy studies

Not applicable

## 6.4.1.2. Additional Risk Minimization Measures

## Summary of physician guide and patient education material by the applicant as in annex VI

Prior to the launch of Susvimo in each Member State, the Marketing Authorization Holder (MAH) must agree about the content and format of the final educational program, including communication media, distribution modalities, and any other aspects of the program, with the National Competent Authority.

The MAH shall provide information on the implant insertion procedure, implant removal procedure, and refill-exchange procedure to ophthalmologists experienced in vitreoretinal surgery, with the aim to establish consistency in following the instructions for use and confidence in the surgical procedures associated with Susvimo. The refill-exchange procedure information will also be provided to ophthalmologists who will perform the refill-exchange procedure for Susvimo. The MAH shall also provide patient educational materials.

The MAH shall ensure that, following discussions and agreements with the National Competent Authorities in each Member State where Susvimo is marketed, at launch and after launch, all ophthalmological clinics where Susvimo is expected to be used are provided with an up-to-date physician guide.

The physician guide will contain the following elements (as reflected in annex VI of the RMP and annex II of the PI):

- Physician information
- Instruction video and pictograms on vitreoretinal surgery and performing the port delivery system (PDS) procedures
- Patient guide

The physician information will include the following key elements:

- Sterile techniques to minimise risk of infection
- Techniques for the vitreoretinal surgery and performing the PDS procedures
- Patient monitoring after the vitreoretinal surgery and the PDS procedures
- Key signs and symptoms of vitreoretinal surgery related adverse events
- Management of adverse events related to vitreoretinal surgery and performing the PDS procedures

The patient educational materials are provided in written and audio format, and will include the following key elements (as reflected in annex VI of the RMP and annex II of the PI):

- How to prepare for Susvimo treatment
- What are the steps following treatment with Susvimo
- Key signs and symptoms of serious adverse events including endophthalmitis
- When to seek urgent attention from the health care provider

## 7. Pharmacovigilance

## 7.1. Pharmacovigilance system

The CHMP considers that the pharmacovigilance system summary submitted by the applicant fulfils the requirements of Article 8(3) of Directive 2001/83/EC.

## 7.2. Periodic safety update reports (PSURs) submission requirements

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

Based on a fundamentally new administration form, i.e., an intraocularly implanted port delivery system which constantly releases ranibizumab into the eye and requires six-monthly refills, the PRAC is of the opinion that a separate entry in the EURD list for Susvimo is needed, as it cannot follow the already existing entry for ranibizumab. The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the Annex II, Section C of the CHMP Opinion. The applicant requested the alignment of the new PSUR cycle with the current DSUR schedule. Susvimo first PSUR data lock point (DLP) is 7 January 2027.

## 8. Product information

## 8.1. Summary of product characteristics (SmPC)

## 8.1.1. SmPC section 4.1 justification

The proposed indication wording is aligned with the population studied in the pivotal Archway study and is thus considered acceptable:

Susvimo is indicated in adult patients for the treatment of neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD) who have achieved stable disease after previously responding to intravitreal treatment with a vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) inhibitor medication.

In SmPC Section 4.2, the following is stated regarding patient selection criteria:

Treatment with Susvimo is initiated when disease stability after intravitreal anti-VEGF medication is achieved as determined by the treating physician based on anatomic and/or visual parameters.

The benefits and risks of treatment should be individually considered by the treating physician and the patient.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The wording for stable disease is considered appropriate and clear for individualised, physician-driven decision-making.

## 8.2. User consultation

The results of the user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet submitted by the applicant show that the package leaflet meets the criteria for readability as set out in the Guideline on the readability of the label and package leaflet of medicinal products for human use.

## 8.3. Additional monitoring

Pursuant to Article 23(1) of Regulation No (EU) 726/2004, Susvimo (ranibizumab) is included in the additional monitoring list since

- it is a biological product that is not covered by the previous category* and authorised after 1 January 2011.

Therefore, the summary of product characteristics and the package leaflet include a statement that this medicinal product is subject to additional monitoring and will allow quick identification of new safety information. The statement is preceded by an inverted equilateral black triangle.

* As outlined in Good Pharmacovigilance Practise (GVP) Module X - Additional monitoring, section X.C.1.1. Criteria for including a medicinal product in the additional monitoring list - Mandatory scope, the previous category refer to medicinal products authorised in the EU that contain a new active substance which, on 1 January 2011, was not contained in any medicinal product authorised in the EU;

## 9. Benefit-risk assessment

## 9.1. Therapeutic context

## 9.1.1. Disease or condition, therapeutic indication

The target indication applied for by the Applicant is for the treatment of adult patients with neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD).

AMD is a chronic, progressive disease of the macula and a leading cause of central vision loss among people over the age of 50 years. nAMD (also known as choroidal neovascularization [CNV] secondary to AMD and wet AMD) is a form of advanced AMD that, if left untreated, causes rapid and severe visual loss, and remains a leading cause of visual impairment in the elderly.

The Applicant proposes the following indication:

Susvimo is indicated in adult patients for the treatment of neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD) who have achieved stable disease after previously responding to intravitreal treatment with a vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) inhibitor medication.

## 9.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

Until the introduction of anti-VEGF therapy, patients with nAMD were at high risk of severe vision loss and blindness. Verteporfin photodynamic therapy (PDT) was approved in 2001 to limit the proportion of nAMD patients losing &lt;15 letters compared to placebo. However, this treatment was not able to prevent progressive visual loss secondary to nAMD, and the availability of anti-VEGF therapy has markedly improved visual outcomes and management of nAMD (Brown et al. 2006; Rosenfeld et al. 2006; Heier et al. 2012). Anti-VEGF agents block the pathophysiological functioning of nAMD by preventing abnormal angiogenesis, and limit fluid build-up in the retina, thereby preserving vision (Rosenfield et al. 2006). Three anti-VEGF agents given via IVT route are currently approved for the treatment of nAMD (ranibizumab, aflibercept, and brolucizumab).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As a chronic disease, nAMD requires life-long treatment and assessment. IVT anti-VEGF injection therapy is the globally recognized standard of care treatment for nAMD. Ranibizumab was the first anti-VEGF agent proven to be more efficacious in reducing visual loss and blindness compared to other treatments such as PDT and also to improve and maintain vision when using a monthly regimen as in the FVF2587g ANCHOR study (Brown et al. 2009). After 5 injections and a gain of approximately 10 Early Treatment Diabetic Retinopathy Study (ETDRS) letters, monthly ranibizumab was able to maintain the gained visual acuity throughout the 24-month duration of the study.

An important challenge for anti-VEGF therapy is the requirement for frequent administration of IVT injections and monitoring visits (Heier et al. 2012; CATT Research Group 2016). Indeed, many patients are treated with monthly anti-VEGF injections for nAMD control. Less-than-monthly injection regimens are possible for some patients (i.e. PRN or Treat &amp; Extend); however, they still require frequent eye examinations and office visits to achieve the patient's best visual outcomes.

In addition, some fixed less-than-monthly dosing regimens are not as effective as monthly injections, even with regular assessment (Lucentis USPI). For example, patients enrolled in the FVF3192g PIER study had an initial visual gain after 3 monthly loading doses of ranibizumab; however, this gain was not maintained after switching to quarterly dosing, and patients did not experience any visual gain at month 12 or 24 (Regillo et al. 2008; Abraham et al. 2010).

In recent years, Treat &amp; Extend regimens have been used to reduce the treatment burden of anti-VEGF therapy by extending the interval between IVT injections. However, recent real-world data studies have indicated that the mean number of injections per year using this regimen is still high. In a metaanalysis of 42 real-world observational studies published between 2007 and 2015, patients receiving Treat &amp; Extend dosing received an average of 6.9 injections per year (Kim et al. 2016). Similarly, a retrospective review of electronic medical records of patients with nAMD in the United States showed after 6, 12, and 24 months of follow-up, patients received means of 5.4, 7.3, and 12.1 injections, respectively (Ciulla et al. 2018).

While accepting that the Treat &amp; Extend regimen still means a relevant number of injections/monitoring for patients, it should be noted that there are several published studies (Fallico M et al, Eur J Ophthalmol 2020; Wykoff CC et al, Am Acad Ophthalmol 2016; Silva R et al, Am Acad Ophthalmol 2017) supporting the non-inferiority of the Treat &amp; Extend regimen vs the monthly regimen of anti VEGF therapy (both from the efficacy and the safety point of view).

## 9.2. Main clinical studies

For a detailed description of the main clinical studies supporting this application, please refer to section 5.3.2 of this document.

Clinical evidence supporting the marketing authorisation application is primarily based on the pivotal Phase III clinical study (GR40548/ Archway) investigating the efficacy, safety and pharmacokinetics of the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W (n=251) compared to IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W (n=167) in patients with wet AMD responsive to anti-VEGF with maximum 9 months since diagnosis, having received at least 4 prior anti-VEGF injections (the last one being ranibizumab).

## 9.3. Favourable effects

One objective of the pivotal study GR40548 was the demonstration of non-inferiority in terms of efficacy for the PDS 100 mg/mL arm (Q24W) compared to IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W, as measured by the change from baseline in BCVA at the average of Week 36 and Week 40. The difference in adjusted means between the treatment arms was -0.3 letters (95.03% CI -1.7, 1.1), which fell into the margin of ±3.9 letters as requested by EMA.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

This was supported by sensitivity analyses in the PP population as well as by supplemental analyses for the primary endpoint (trimmed mean analysis, MMRM model using different rules for measures after intercurrent events), which were all consistent with the primary analysis.

In addition, the key secondary endpoints, which had been requested by EMA, were met: Non-inferiority using a margin of ±3.9 letters of the PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W regimen to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W regimen was also demonstrated for the change from baseline in BCVA at the average of Week 44 and Week 48 (difference in adjusted means of -0.2 [95.03% CI -1.8, 1.3]) and at the average of Week 88 and 92 (difference in adjusted means of -0.6 [95.03% CI -2.5, 1.3]). Supplemental analyses and sensitivity analyses further supported the robustness of the findings for this key secondary endpoint.

Subgroup analyses for the primary EP, the mean change from baseline in BCVA score averaged over Week 36 and Week 40, for the subgroups age, sex, number of prior anti-VEGF injections and baseline BCVA score were overall consistent with the primary endpoint analysis.

In addition, the results for the secondary efficacy visual and anatomical endpoints supported that PDS 100 mg/mL Q24W was similar to the IVT ranibizumab 0.5 mg Q4W.

Patients in the PDS arm received considerably fewer treatment interventions than the patients in the IVT arm (mean of approximately 2 vs 11 at Week 40). This is of clinical relevance for the target population, since the requirement of frequent anti-VEGF IVT injections and follow-up visits in order to achieve and maintain improved visual acuity poses a burden on patients with nAMD. Thus, the unmet medical need for reduced treatment burden in the nAMD indication is overall acknowledged.

In addition, it should be noted that although current IVT anti-VEGF regimens are highly effective when administered in controlled clinical trials, outcomes in clinical practice are often suboptimal. This is due to patient non-adherence to the established treatment regimen, which requires numerous treatment visits and injections each year.

## 9.3.1. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

None.

## 9.4. Unfavourable effects

The safety profile of Susvimo is determined by several components. On the one hand there are risks associated with the intraocular application of ranibizumab, which is the active substance of Susvimo. Risks associated with the IVT injection of ranibizumab are well known and include (ordered by frequency): conjunctival haemorrhage, conjunctival hyperaemia, iritis, eye pain, cataract, vitreous haemorrhage, conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak, conjunctival erosion, conjunctival retraction, endophthalmitis, hyphema, and rhegmatogenous retinal detachment.

In additional to these risks, the treatment with Susvimo is associated with risks associated with the initial surgical implantation procedure, with subsequent refill-exchange procedures and, if medically required, with the procedure of implant removal. Associated risks include device dislocation (0.7% in the ALL PDS 100 mg/mL population), device malfunction, and septum dislodgement.

Across all PDS arms, the number of ocular AEs in the study eye was significantly higher compared to the IVT control group. Based on safety results from studies GR 40548 and GX28228, the overall number of ocular AEs in the study eye was approximately twice as high in the PDS 100 mg Arm compared to the IVT Arm. Most events in the PDS arm had an onset within the first 9 days after implantation, while events were almost equally distributed over time in the IVT ranibizumab arm. From Week 40 through CCOD the incidence of ocular AEs in the study eye were similar in the PDS 100 mg arm and the IVT arm.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The greatest imbalance was found for conjunctival haemorrhage, conjunctival hyperaemia, and iritis, which occurred with much higher frequency in the PDS arm than in the IVT arm. Nearly all of these cases occurred in close timely association with the PDS implantation procedure and prior to the first refill-exchange procedure.

Analyses of the number of AEs caused by implant during the first 10 months revealed that approximately 88% of patients experienced at least one adverse event caused by implant. After the postoperative period (up to day 37 after implant insertion), the rates of ocular AEs were similar in the PDS arms and IVT arms. While the number of AEs considered caused by implant were in the range of 80-95% across PDS arms, the number of AEs considered caused by refill was in a between 5%-12% (which is in or even under the range of the IVT arm).

Importantly, most AEs were of mild to moderate intensity and well manageable. As of 31 January 2025, 237 patients (41.1%) in the All PDS population experienced at least one ocular AESI in the study eye (cataract (18.5%), conjunctival bleb/conjunctival filtering bleb leak (14.7%), vitreous haemorrhage (7.5%), and conjunctival erosion (7.8%)). A total of 41 patients (7.1%) of the All PDS 100 mg/mL Population experienced at least one serious AR, most commonly endophthalmitis (2.1%), conjunctival erosion (1.7%), and retinal detachment (1.4%). In addition, device dislocation was considered severe in 5 out of 14 cases.

After the post-operative period, there was no notable trend in the overall number of ocular AESIs by refill-period. Likewise, by individual AESIs, there were no notable trends after the post-operative period.

It can therefore be concluded that the implantation procedure itself seems to be associated with a relatively high number of typical complications, while the number of AEs caused by refill is in the range of AEs seen with the conventional IVT injection of ranibizumab.

In studies GR40548 and GX28228, the incidence of non-ocular SAEs was higher in the PDS arms compared to the IVT arm. 22.1% of the patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL population experienced at least one non-ocular SAEs. The non-ocular SAEs in this pooled safety population with the highest reported incidence were pneumonia, urinary tract infection, osteoarthritis and cerebrovascular accident, sepsis and hip fracture. APTC events ware overall rare but occurred more frequently in patients in the PDS 100 mg/mL arm compared to the IVT arm.

## 9.4.1. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

The safety profile of ranibizumab for IVT injection is well known and the overall safety database for Susvimo 100 mg/mL is considered adequate.

However, the PDS entails a new pattern of administration of ranibizumab (it continuously releases ranibizumab over the refill interval at steady concentrations), and some safety aspects may be rare or may only occur after long-term treatment and with cumulative exposure. Some risks may therefore have not been identified yet.

Some uncertainty remains about the bilateral administration of PDS ranibizumab. Since administration of PDS ranibizumab in both eyes has not been studied in clinical trials and pharmacokinetic data and safety data with bilateral PDS implant is missing. The Applicant presented simulations on serum ranibizumab concentrations with bilateral PDS as compared to PDS in one eye and monthly IVT ranibizumab injections in the fellow eye. Based on these, serum Cmax and AUC with bilateral PDS is expected to be 41% and 77% lower compared to the Cmax and AUC achieved with PDS in one eye Q24W and monthly IVT ranibizumab in the fellow eye. Given these predictions hold true, bilateral PDS treatment could be acceptable. However, in the absence of clinical data on bilateral treatment, some uncertainties with regard to the safety and tolerability remain. The evaluation of bilateral treatment will therefore be addressed in the PASS program.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Some events associated with the implant have not or only rarely been reported in clinical trials but may be observed more frequently when more patients are exposed outside of clinical studies. These potential risks include implant rejection, implant dislocation and migration, implant malfunctioning and consequences of an ocular traumatism. The need of training and fulfilment of the procedural requisites appears mandatory from the safety point of view, and correspondingly these requirements are stated in section 4.2 of the SmPC, and a physician guide to be provided upon delivery of the training is in the RMP as an additional risk minimisation measure. Another uncertainty associated with the implant is its durability and whether it will have to be exchanged or permanently removed (explanted) at some point.

No interaction studies with other medicinal products have been performed. However, patients are expected to receive concomitant topical or IVT medications in clinical practice. The Applicant adequately addressed this by respective wording in section 4.5 of the SmPC: No interaction studies have been performed. There are no known interactions of clinical relevance. In addition, information regarding the administration of anti-VEGF agents in the fellow eye has also been included in section 4.4 of the SmPC.

An increased number of systemic AEs and SAEs has been observed with the PDS. Whether this imbalance could be related to continuous release of ranibizumab with the PDS (leading to an absence of through-levels) is not completely clear. Yet, imbalances in risk factors for APTC events at baseline might have also contributed to this finding. This issue will therefore be addressed further by the Applicant in the post-marketing setting (PASS program).

An increasing number of septum dislodgements, which led to explantation of the device, had been observed with the previous version of the implant. This resulted in pause/recall and a detailed investigation into this device deficiency. Updates were made to the implant and refill needle. The updated device is intended for commercial use. The quality data for the updated implant and refill needle appear to be satisfactory. The clinical evidence that would support the absence or low frequency of septum dislodgment with the updated version of the device is currently limited. The applicant committed to closely monitor patients in the ongoing study GR40549 (Portal) who will receive the updated version of the device and are undergoing refill-exchanges with the updated refill-needle and submit data once available.
