## 9.6. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 9.6.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

## Efficacy

The PDS implant was developed to enable continuous delivery of ranibizumab, providing an alternative to frequent IVT injections and reducing treatment burden while maintaining visual and anatomical outcomes.

The pivotal study demonstrated that ranibizumab delivered via the PDS maintained therapeutic efficacy comparable to that achieved with standard IVT injections over a period of 2 years. This demonstration included changes from baseline in BCVA (an endpoint of widely accepted clinical importance) at several timepoints and anatomical endpoints.

Patients in the PDS arm received considerably fewer treatment interventions than the patients in the IVT arm (mean of approximately 2 vs 11 at Week 40). This is of clinical relevance for the target population, since the requirement of frequent anti-VEGF IVT injections and follow-up visits in order to achieve and maintain improved visual acuity poses a burden on patients with nAMD, as frequent clinical visits can lead to treatment fatigue, reducing adherence and potentially resulting in treatment discontinuation. The relevance of this dimension is supported by PRO endpoint results in the study. With the PDS implant it could be demonstrated that patients require substantially fewer treatments compared to IVT ranibizumab injections, translating into a clinically meaningful reduction in the injection frequency relative to the standard regimen of ranibizumab administered every four weeks.

## Safety

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The surgical procedure of the PDS implantation seems to be associated with a number of typical ocular AEs. In clinical trials, the incidence of ocular AEs and ocular SAEs was substantially higher compared to IVT ranibizumab injections. In the controlled study periods, the overall number of ocular AEs in the study eye was almost twice as high in the PDS 100 mg arm compared to the IVT arm. The greatest imbalance was found for conjunctival haemorrhage, conjunctival hyperaemia, and iritis, which occurred with much higher frequency in the PDS arm than in the IVT arm. In study GR40548, nearly all of these cases occurred within the first 37 days post-implantation and therefore appear to be attributed to the PDS implantation procedure. While most of the events were mild or moderate, severe ocular AE were also recorded.

## 9.6.2. Balance of benefits and risks

Evidence of maintaining visual and anatomical outcomes was achieved by comparable efficacy between the PDS implant and monthly IVT injections of ranibizumab. The use of the PDS implant resulted in a significant reduction in treatment frequency when compared to monthly ranibizumab IVT injections.

These potential benefits are contrasted by specific safety risks associated by the implantation procedure. As a result, the rate of ocular AEs within the initial 37-day post-operative period is substantially increased in patients treated with the PDS. However, subsequent refill-exchange procedures seem to have a comparable (or even slightly better) safety profile with regard to ocular AEs compared to conventional IVT ranibizumab injections. It is currently not clear whether the risk for systemic AEs, including APTC events, is increased for patients treated with the PDS. Further evaluation of this potential risk is considered necessary and will be done within the PASS program.

To date, some uncertainties with regard to systemic and to long-term safety remain. This also includes the question, whether the implant should remain in the eye or be explanted when Susvimo treatment will be permanently stopped. Respective analyses of these questions need to and will be conducted in the context of the proposed post-authorization studies.

Although the PDS is associated with a higher incidence of AEs, particular ocular AEs, compared to IVT injections of ranibizumab, most adverse events are manageable with routine clinical interventions and are outweighed by the favourable effect - demonstrated to be non-inferior to the IVT ranibizumab - and by the less-frequent retreatment interventions. The benefit risk is hence considered positive.

## 9.6.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

Not applicable

## 9.7. Benefit-risk conclusions

The overall B/R of Susvimo (PDS with ranibizumab) for the treatment of neovascular (wet) age-related macular degeneration (AMD) in adult patients who have achieved stable disease after previously responding to intravitreal treatment with a vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) inhibitor medicinal product, is positive.
